Outlook 2027-2028

Highlights
Global Economy: a solid but unusually narrow footing
The AI infrastructure build-out has neutralized tariff and energy headwinds 
and is driving over half of global trade growth, a large share of global GDP 
growth, and half of YTD equity gains. Our outlook hinges largely on that build-
out continuing and on some resolution in the Middle East, the main source of 
growth acceleration in 2027E. Bottom-up aggregation points to $1.75 trillion 
of hyperscaler capex next year, but public opposition to data centres could 
delay and reprofile near-term spending.  How the equity market responds to 
that has a significant bearing on our forecast.
Still  early in the AI adoption cycle 
Enterprise 
adoption 
remains 
low, 
and 
AI 
implementation 
spending 
is unusually concentrated. Although we cannot yet see AI in the productivity 
data, wage restraint during this energy shock has been notable, and global 
equity earnings growth is running at the 91st historical percentile. We expect 
the environment to become progressively more disinflationary in 2027, as 
inflation from tariffs, energy and memory price fades. We expect only modest 
additional tightening, and then a reversal of the hikes in 2028.  
Equities: Nasdaq over RTY. Industrials over Consumer. Growth over Value 
Data-centre growth is unlikely to slow when viewed across a broader 
geography (all US states, other countries) and a longer horizon (2-3y). 
Hyperscalers and chips remain the nucleus of AI, which continues to be the 
source of supernormal earnings growth and should drive 12% S&P500 returns 
in 2027E. We expect broadening into AI-adjacent sectors, such as Industrials 
and Financials, not a rotation away from AI. Both Price and Earnings Momentum 
strategies should work, as should Growth and Quality. Europe and EM should 
keep pace with the S&P500 in our base case, but both are exposed to higher 
energy and food costs. Buy V2X over VIX.
Fixed Income: Lower terminal. Steepeners in the US, Flatteners in Europe
After returning -2.6% annualised over the last 5y, fixed income should deliver 
middle-single-digit returns in 2027. Alongside steady bond volatility and a return 
to negative bond-equity correlation, this should move portfolios back from 
extremely high equity allocations towards 60-40 equity-bond. We think 
terminal rates are mispriced across most markets and expect front-end and 
belly yields to fall. As term premium rises, the US bull curve steepens, 
although back-end yields can also fall modestly. High food and energy 
inflation and weaker growth favour a flatter curve in Europe. We like Aussie 
and Mexican rates. European IG likely to outperform US IG credit.
FX and Commodities: Long Gold v EUR, Long JPY v CHF
In our base case, oil averages $80 per barrel through 2027. But oil and gas 
inventories are low, leaving risks strongly tilted towards higher energy prices. 
We see no clear signs of dedollarisation in flows or in how the dollar trades. 
The lack of a France or Middle East discount keeps us defensive on EUR axis 
currencies relative to JPY, and particularly Gold. Precious Metals have not 
behaved as diversifiers recently, but should benefit from lower real rates and 
higher term premium.

Global Economics & Markets Outlook 9 November 2026
ab 8
Global Economics & Markets Outlook
UBS Research
The Global Economy in the Year Ahead
The global economy has been on a solid but unusually narrow footing. Strength in the AI 
infrastructure build-out largely managed to neutralize the headwinds from tariffs in '25 
and  energy prices in '26. For context: trade in AI-related products is twice as large as in 
energy goods,  growth in technology exports is driving over half of growth in global trade 
(Figure 23Trade in technology acounts for 5%
 of global trade growth), and all the countries involved in the AI supply chain add up to  over half of 
global GDP. Similarly, the increase in the valuation of tech companies  this year has  driven 
nearly half of the increase in global equity markets YTD (MSCI ACWI). That narrowness is 
the theme of this outlook. We expect global growth to pick up to 3.5% next year, but an 
unusually large share of that rests on two issues: the continued build-out of AI 
infrastructure and a resolution of  the Middle East conflict. 
 For the economies in the AI supply chain, the build-out is also driving a significant share 
of GDP growth. In the US, we estimate hyperscaler capex will come close to $1 trillion 
this year. We think that has driven about 60% of all US growth over the last four 
quarters (adjusted for leakage via imports, but including wealth and indirect investment 
effects). A bottom-up aggregation of our equity analysts' forecasts points to $1.75 
trillion in hyperscaler capex in 2027, or over 5% of US GDP, with just five companies 
accounting for 85% of it (Figure 20Part of the credit strength in the US is the increased financing ned of the Hyperscalers). That would be the largest infrastructure investment 
boom in US history (Figure 21The AI infrastructure build-out is set to be the largest infrastructure investm
ent bom
 in US history). 
That money is being pushed into economies involved in the AI supply chain, a majority of 
which are a small fraction of the size of the US: the US economy is 14½ times the size of 
Korea's and 32 times Taiwan's. Those economies are running into capacity constraints, 
which is driving up memory inflation. So not all of the increase in projected capex is extra 
compute; part of it is simply higher prices (Figure 24Not al of the spending on AI infrastructure is extra com
pute; m
em
ory prices are inflating the total).
 Our outlook hinges to an unusual extent on the guidance reported by hyperscalers. 
Public opposition to data centers in the US is widespread (Figure 22The public's oposition to data centers is a risk) and a few newly 
elected officials openly campaigned against them, so there is a risk that some of the 
spending gets delayed. That could push part of the near-term strength in our forecast 
into later years. That, and how the equity market would react to it, is the first big risk to 
our forecast. 
But we first take a step back and look at the fundamentals. What is our starting point? 
As Figure 13Global growth is runing a touch below trend; with los of m
om
entum
 in both hard and soft data shows, global growth is tracking close to trend, at 2.7% QoQ annualized 
through September. The hard data is running at around 2.3%. The soft data was at 
3.1% in September but stepped down to 2.6% in October. Surveys have been on a bit of 
a roller coaster: confidence fell sharply when the Middle East conflict started, recovered 
over the summer, and is now dipping again. On the whole, though, we are not far from 
long-run averages (consumer confidence is the weak spot; see this separate note). 
Figure 14Next year growth to acelerate m
ainly due to an asum
ed volum
e recovery in the M
idle East  then shows our global growth forecast for 2027 (note that these are GDP-
weighted averages whereas our nowcast shows medians). We expect global growth to 
pick up from 3.2% to 3.5% (Q4/Q4), but almost all of that comes from the 'rest of the 
world', not the major economies. That group adds 38bp to global growth next year. 
Most of it (29bp) comes from Saudi Arabia and the UAE, where oil and gas volumes are 
projected to recover. Saudi growth, for instance, swings from -4.6% to 10.4%, adding a 
lot to the global number even on a small weight. If the Middle East conflict is not 
resolved and attacks continue, there is obvious downside risk, because more of our 
forecast than usual depends on the region. That is the second big risk to our forecast. 
Case in point: Figure 15Suply chains rem
ain disrupted by the M
idle East conflict. Dark transits and other bypass routes have been successful: 
roughly 70% of the >20mb/d of oil and oil products from the Strait of Hormuz is still 
reaching global markets. Even so, supply chains remain severely disrupted. Most of that 
shows up in high shipping and air freight costs, but the disruption on our global supply 
chain tracker is broad-based. We are still missing 6% of global supply, and product and 
LNG shortages are even larger (LNG flows are at only 20% of pre-conflict levels). That in 
turn is a risk to the normalization of inflation.Figure 16Inflation is runing above target in DM
 stil m
ainly because of energy prices  shows that the excess inflation in 
advanced economies is still mostly energy, with limited pass-through into underlying 
inflation so far. But the longer inflation stays high, the greater the risk that it spills over 
into wages (Figure 17Partly because of inflation and partly because of wage m
oderation, real wages are trending lower shows how we expect wage growth to trend down).

The global economy is on a solid but 
unusually narrow footing. The AI 
infrastructure build-out has been 
driving over half of global trade 
growth, a large share of GDP growth 
in the economies along the AI supply 
chain (together, half of global GDP), 
and nearly half of the gains in global 
equity markets YTD. 
Hyperscaler capex is currently 
projected at close to $1.75 trillion in 
2027.
Pushing over 5% of US GDP into 
much smaller capacity-constrained 
economies is driving up tech-related 
inflation.
Public opposition to data centers 
could lead to delays and a re-
profiling of the near-term strength 
we have in our forecast.
Global growth at present is at trend-
like levels, with the surveys a bit 
better than the hard data.
Most of the acceleration in growth 
we have in our forecast next year 
comes from the Middle East.
For now, supply chains remain 
significantly disrupted and are a 
source of inflation.

Global Economics & Markets Outlook 9 November 2026
ab 9
With central banks responding to higher inflation, real interest rates are creeping higher 
again. In advanced economies at least, real rates are now close to their 2024 peak, 
reached at the end of the last hiking cycle. That tightening appears to be dampening the 
global credit impulse (Figure 19The global credit im
pulse has started to lose m
om
entum
, though m
ainly because of China). The impulse currently depends to an unusual extent on 
the US. Credit growth in other advanced economies (Japan, the Eurozone) and parts of 
EM had also inflected higher, but that recovery now seems to be fading. China's credit 
impulse and the weakness in its investment more broadly remain a drag. 
Are there any signs that productivity is starting to improve? After all, we are now a few 
years into the AI adoption cycle, and every firm in the world must at least be thinking 
about how to build AI into its workflow. Unfortunately, there is so far little evidence of a 
pick-up. US  productivity has grown at just 1.3% annualized over the last three quarters, 
though taking a wider lens there has been some modest improvement over the last 
three years relative to the pre-pandemic trend. For 42 of the 48 other economies we 
looked at (Figure 26There is so far very litle deviation globaly from
 pre-pandem
ic productivity trends), the gains are even smaller than those in the US. Part of the 
problem is that adoption is  mile wide but inch deep: 80% of executives use AI, but for 
only 2.1 hours a week. AI spending is also unusually concentrated, with 2% of firms 
accounting for half of the total. Overall enterprise adoption remains low too, at around 
20% in both our own UBS Evidence Lab survey and the US Census survey. The capital 
deepening that is underway suggests the gains could come, but the pace of adoption 
makes it unlikely we will see them clearly in the 2027 data. 
Yet despite growth and  productivity roughly on trend, US profitability is at all-time highs 
(Figure 27US profits are at an al-tim
e high). Across listed and unlisted companies combined, gross profits are running at 
14½% of GDP and net (after-tax) profits at just shy of 12%. As we explain in one of the 
essays, a few variables account for most of the profit growth since the mid-1980s: 
globalization, tax changes that made it attractive for professionals and small businesses 
to become pass-through entities, and a few other macro variables. But over the last 
three years, profit growth has been completely outside the models, and roughly 90% of 
it relates to the surge in tech and financials profits. The US also seems to be moving 
much more rapidly from labour-based to capital-based production (Figure 28Busineses in the US apear to be becom
ing les labour-intensive). 
The global economy is projected to get little help from fiscal or monetary policy over the 
next two years.Figure 31In agregate, the im
pulse from
 global fiscal policy is close to neutral  shows the global fiscal impulse from a long-run perspective. It 
is about as neutral as it gets: cyclically adjusted primary balances are expected to loosen 
by just 4bp of global GDP, mainly because of China and the Eurozone (Figure 32But with stim
ulus in the Eurozone and China ofset by adjustm
ent in m
any other econom
ies). But by 
count, more countries are consolidating in '27 (25) than expanding (10) (Figure 30Com
paring the fiscal adjustm
ent efort in '27 to '26). 
Some of what currently looks like fiscal adjustment could disappear. On paper, the US 
fiscal contribution to growth flips from +0.5pp this year (thanks to the OBBBA) to a 
0.2pp headwind. But with the midterms handing Democrats control of Congress, some 
of the scheduled cuts could be offset by higher discretionary spending as part of the 
debt ceiling negotiations. There is also substantial uncertainty over fiscal policy in several 
other large economies, including Brazil (new government still being formed) and France 
(elections next year). And as we highlight in the fiscal essay, DM public debt continues to 
rise by 2pp of GDP a year on average, with little sign of that reversing in the next few 
years (Figure 29Public debt continues to rise). That keeps bond market stress firmly on our risk list. 
We expect monetary policy to stay restrictive through end-2027, with the hikes only 
starting to be reversed in 2028.Figure 33DM
 policy rates - anual changes  and  Figure 34EM
 policy rates - anual changes  show the projected policy rate 
changes for each country through 2028. Aggregating those up, DM policy rates peak 
over the next two quarters at roughly 75bp above neutral on average (Figure 35Policy rates in both EM
 and DM
 are runing above neutral). EM 
policy rates also peak in the next few quarters, but sit further from neutral, partly 
because of several large outliers such as Brazil. There are substantial risks around these 
forecasts. They are built on inflation forecasts that run off downward-sloping energy 
demand curves, which mechanically make the inflation problem disappear. 
Finally, as we always do, we model several alternative scenarios, given the binary nature 
of the energy outlook, the risk of AI disappointment, and a potential resurgence of bond 
market stress. A fuller section on scenarios follows after the essays, but as Figure 36Our baseline forecast is subject to substantial uncertainty  
shows, quarterly growth in  2027 varies by 250bp between the weakest and strongest 
scenarios, depending on which state of the world we end up in. In short, the outlook is 
solid in aggregate but the base of growth is narrow. Much depends on how the AI capex 
cycle and the Middle East evolve and, with elections on the horizon and debt still 
climbing, on how much fiscal slippage bond markets are willing to tolerate. 
Central bank tightening appears to 
be dampening the global credit 
impulse.
There is little evidence so far of an 
increase in global productivity. AI 
adoption is a mile wide but an inch 
deep, and AI spending remains 
unusually concentrated.
That notwithstanding, US 
profitability is at all-time-high levels 
– mostly because of Tech and
Financials (and recently also Oil). It is
moving away from labour-based
production faster than other
countries.
Global fiscal policy is roughly neutral 
in 2027.
DM public debt continues to increase 
at a rate of 2pp of GDP per year, with 
limited efforts in some of the larger 
economies to turn that trend around.
Monetary policy to stay restrictive in 
2027 before reversing hikes in '28. 
But using oil curves to project 
inflation may be embedding too 
much  disinflation optimism in the 
forecast.
The outlook considers four 
alternative scenarios - two for oil, AI 
disappointment, and bond market 
stress. The results imply a very large 
uncertainty range around our 
forecast.

Global Economics & Markets Outlook
UBS Research
 Asset Markets in the Year Ahead
 Over the last five years, the Bloomberg Global Agg bond index has returned -2.6% 
annualised, versus 12% for MSCI ACWI. According to AAII, equity allocations are above 
70%, versus 16% for government bonds, the widest gap in 25 years. Positive bond-
equity correlation has weakened diversification. Poor returns and diversification have 
pushed relative bond valuations close to a 100-year low (Figure 37Bonds close to their cheapest relative to equities in a century: Equity risk prem
ium
 proxy ). 
 We expect solid but lower equity returns and modestly higher volatility over the next two 
years. Bond returns should turn positive in the mid-single digits and bond-equity 
correlation negative as inflation declines (Figure 38Bond equity corelation depends on the level of inflation – W
e se negative corelation returning in 2027). Active risk parity and mean-
variance optimisation suggest reducing equity exposure from just over 85% in 2026 to 
70% in 2027 and just under 50% in 2028, while increasing fixed income (Figure 39Active risk parity and risk-adjusted return m
axim
isation aproaches sugest equity weight faling relative to fixed incom
e in the com
ing 2y ). 
Optimising for total returns implies higher equity allocation, but a similar trajectory. 
 2026 saw a large gap between macro and micro performance. Global GDP growth was 
around the 29%ile of its 30y distribution, while MSCI ACWI earnings were likely at the 
91%ile (Figure 40GDP growth is ordinary; Earnings growth is anything but ). Earnings seem on track for another ~15-20% year despite the 
valuation headwind from poor fixed income performance.
 AI spending remains the main engine of earnings growth. US datacentre construction 
may face local delays, but global build should remain strong. Our bottom-up estimates 
put hyperscaler capex next year at ~$1.75 trl, versus ~$1trl in 2026. Cumulative capex of 
$6-8trl between 2024 and 2030E will require monetisation. We estimate consumer and 
B2B demand could generate ~$2.5trl in annual revenues, with autonomous mobility, 
robotics, AI media and personalised medicine adding ~$1trl beyond 2030E. Cloud 
revenue growth, our preferred real-time monetisation indicator, accelerated to ~100% 
annualised in Q3. Only 8% of enterprises have deployed AI agents at scale, while UBS 
analysts expect ~380GW of data-centre capacity to be added over 2026-30, versus 
133GW currently operational. 
 The question is whether results can keep exceeding high expectations. This year began 
with 12m forward earnings growth expectations of 15.1% for US, versus likely realised 
growth of 34.7%. Expectations for 2027 and 2028 are already 17.8% and 16.9%. 
Markets have historically struggled when revisions in 12m and 24m forward earnings 
growth turn negative (Figure 41Faling earnings revisions do m
ater, especialy when the starting point of expectations is high ), particularly when initial growth expectations were 
high (>15%, or 0.6  standard deviations above 12% average). We expect any downward 
revisions to 1-2y earnings to be brief, but genuine data-centre capex cuts and weaker 3y 
growth could materially hurt markets. 
 Hyperscalers and chips remain the core of the AI trade; model and software companies 
face greater disruption risk. Successful AI monetisation should strengthen cash flow and 
sustain capex. For many chip companies, particularly memory, expected free cash flow 
over the next 3-5y is near or above current market cap (Figure 42M
em
ory fre cash flow in the next 3-5y close to curent m
arket cap ). Semiconductors 
became increasingly aligned with Momentum over the past 12-15m, leaving them 
vulnerable when Momentum sold off despite little fundamental change (Figure 43Sem
is and M
om
entum
 Factor were the sam
e trade this year ). 
Momentum performs poorly during liquidity shocks, particularly abrupt moves in rates 
or risk premia. With that shock largely behind us, this headwind should fade.
 High earnings expectations remain a concern, but falling multiples provide comfort, 
particularly in US and EM equities, where Tech and Chips have derated sharply. Such 
derating despite strong earnings suggests scepticism about sustainability, now evident 
in Tech (Figure 44US and EM
 equities have sen a large Tech-driven derating this year ). If negative revisions prove short-lived, equities could benefit from 
both earnings and valuations. For now, we assume no valuation support (Figure 45UBS’s S&P50 Price, Earnings and Valuation expectations ). 
 Bottom-up data shows 44% of S&P500 companies expect capex growth above 10% 
(Figure 46Botom
-up capex pointing to strong top-down capex), suggesting the US capex cycle is broadening (though we see few signs of 
that in the global data yet). Tech remains central, but we also favour AI-adjacent 
Industrials, Health Care and Financials. We prefer Nasdaq over S&P500, Equal Weighted 
S&P500 and Russell 2000. Russell 2000 has AI value-chain exposure, but smaller 
companies are more sensitive to financing costs than hyperscalers. We prefer Growth 
and Quality over Value. Europe has benefited from Industrials and Banks, while 
consumers face weak wage growth, high energy costs and potentially higher food prices 
from Super El Niño. Across the US, EU, EM and Japan, Industrials earnings revisions 
Please refer to the narrative of this report for an explanation of this image/chart

Global Economics & Markets Outlook 9 November 2026
ab 15
remain positive while Consumer Discretionary revisions are negative. 
 Europe's earnings are more vulnerable than US earnings to higher oil or food prices. 
Valuations are cheaper than in the US, but less so relative to history. Index volatility 
remained unusually subdued (until last week) as low correlations offset high single-stock 
volatility (again, this changed last week) (Figure 47A pick-up in corelation should lift volatility ). Correlation and volatility could rise 
further with oil, food prices or borrowing costs, particularly in France. We think the wide 
V2X-VIX gap (Figure 48Buy European equity volatility relative to US equity volatility ) offers an attractive hedge for European energy, food and 
sovereign risks. 
 We forecast MSCI EM returns of ~20% in 2027, supported by EPS growth of 38% in 
2027E and 22% in 2028E. Around three-quarters comes from Korea and Taiwan, while 
visibility elsewhere remains weak until the Middle East conflict abates. Accelerating 
shareholder returns, light positioning and conservative 2028 EPS expectations should 
support Korea and Taiwan outperforming larger EM markets. 
 Our base case is Brent averaging $80/bbl in 2027 and TTF gas €45/MWh. Oil and gas 
inventories are low (Figure 49Oil inventories at their lowest eb), with diesel and jet fuel particularly exposed to upside 
risk. Our recommendations should perform well if energy costs remain high. Conversely, 
a sharp decline could revive consumption and broaden investment beyond Tech, 
benefiting Small Caps and global equities relative to US Large Caps and Tech. This is a 
larger risk to our recommendations than higher energy prices. Consumer and Financials 
would benefit most from a reversal (Figure 50US Consum
er and Financials stocks are m
ost sensitive to oil suply shock). 
 Global capex is largely an AI-driven near-term phenomenon, with little evidence of 
higher trend productivity outside of Singapore, Taiwan and a handful of other 
economies. Del Negro, Elbarmi and Pham show that neutral rates have increasingly 
converged since the 1980s, while low FX volatility suggests global rates still move 
together. US terminal rates are priced too high  (Figure 51UBS expects central banks to cut rates m
ore than is being priced ). As inflation falls, US front-
end and belly yields should fall, with smaller gains at the long end. Deteriorating supply-
demand for US debt (Figure 52Suply of US governm
ent and IG: a pick-up in 2027 and 2028 ) should push term premia gradually higher (Figure 53W
e expect the decline in front end rates to dom
inate rising term
 prem
ium). 
Treasuries retain a high convenience yield through liquidity, repo collateral and 
regulatory value. We expect inflation to approach the Fed’s target range by H2 2027, 
pushing bond-equity correlation negative. Higher yields this year have reflected real 
rates more in the US and inflation more in Europe and the UK (Figure 54US rates back up driven by real rates; Europe and UK by inflation). The ECB’s 
headline inflation focus requires high front-end rates, while European growth remains 
weaker and vulnerable to energy shocks. Hence, we recommend 2s10s ESTR flatteners. 
 We see no catalyst to reverse OAT widening before next year’s election. Lower global 
rates and carry should limit further widening, at best holding spreads around 150 bps. 
ECB intervention appears unlikely without substantially wider spreads, while Europe’s 
election calendar leaves peripheral risks skewed wider. The selloff has made European IG 
more attractive. Nearly 50% of French IG names now trade inside OATs (Figure 55Nearly half of French IG corporates trading inside OATs), but 
weakness extends beyond France. We prefer European to US IG. Recent Hyperscaler 
issuance has been oversubscribed (Figure 56Hyperscaler debt isues have ben 3-5x oversubscribed ), but rising issuance will demand a risk 
premium. HY data-centre issuers trade above 10% yields, while US CCC widening has 
not materially spread to broader HY (Figure 57C widening infection has not yet spread to the rest of US HY ). With spreads already wider, we are 
closing our long CDX HY protection recommendation. 
 There is little firm evidence of de-dollarisation in flows or pricing. US portfolio inflows 
into equities and fixed income continue to rise, with no systematic dollar discount 
relative to interest-rate differentials (Figure 58Residuals of FX regresions on interest-rate diferentials don’t sugest a system
atic dolar discount ). Against stronger credits, the dollar looks 
modestly cheap to theoretical value, but the divergence is small. 
 Sustained dollar weakness has historically coincided with a steady US economy and 
much stronger growth elsewhere. Today, the US expansion remains stronger than 
Europe’s, while China’s structural slowdown continues. The US advantage is clearer in 
earnings and margins than GDP. The dollar could become vulnerable if US fiscal or 
geopolitical risks worsen, surplus countries reduce dollar purchases, or the Fed turns 
materially more dovish. Gold will be most elastic to these factors, and can rally 
independently of them as well. Gold’s diversification benefits have weakened, with 
positive beta to the S&P 500 on both up and down days (). If sustained, this would 
weaken the case for gold. For now, rising term premium and falling front-end real rates 
remain positive for Gold (Figure 60Lower real yields should help Gold higher ). To limit USD exposure, we prefer being long gold 
against EUR.

case for gold remains strong.

Global Economics & Markets Outlook 9 November 2026
ab 20
Global Economics & Markets Outlook
UBS Research
The Big Themes for 2027 
We sat down as a team in October to consider the key debates for 2027. Twenty essays 
emerged across five themes.
Theme 1: AI
Scale, duration and mechanics of the AI investment cycle:  An extraordinary 
investment cycle is under way, with implications for corporate profits, labour markets 
and economic growth. The question now is how far the infrastructure build-out 
translates into broader adoption, monetisation and productivity gains, especially as 
constraints around power, infrastructure and skills start to emerge. Six essays look at AI 
and technology from these different angles.
Theme 2: A new Macro Regime
Inflation, debt, energy and rates in a very different world: The global economy is 
adjusting to more frequent supply shocks, higher public debt and greater volatility in 
inflation and interest rates than before the pandemic. Growth and profits have held up 
well, but policymakers have less room for manoeuvre as energy markets tighten, debt-
service costs rise and demand for government bonds shifts. Five essays consider what is 
driving this new macro regime and where the pressure points lie.
Theme 3: Asset Allocation
How to position portfolios: The changing macro environment also has important 
implications for investors. Long-standing relationships between growth, inflation, 
volatility and asset returns are shifting. Bonds have a role to play again, while 
opportunities in carry, currencies and alternatives require greater selectivity, particularly 
where leverage and illiquidity are building. Five essays consider what this means for asset 
allocation.
Theme 4: Regional debates
Major regional nuances: The outlook also looks increasingly different across the major 
economies. China is contending with a difficult macro backdrop but has significant 
opportunities in AI; Europe continues to prove more resilient than many expected; and 
Japan is moving beyond deflation. As these paths diverge, local policy, structural change 
and valuations matter more for both domestic markets and global returns. Three essays 
focus on these regional debates and their market implications.
Theme 5: Politics
Potential risks to the cycle: Politics remains an important source of macro risk, 
particularly with several significant elections ahead and governments already facing 
difficult fiscal choices. Our final essay focuses on the US and France, where electoral 
politics could have important consequences for fiscal policy, monetary institutions, 
investment and regulation, with effects that extend well beyond their domestic markets.

Global Economics & Markets Outlook 9 November 2026
ab 21
Summaries of the Essays
Theme 1: AI
1. Are we on the cusp of a global tech-driven investment cycle?
The AI build-out is likely the largest infrastructure boom in US history, exceeding those in 
canals, railroads, electrification, highways and telecoms. We now forecast $1.8 trillion in 
AI capex for the US next year but the question is whether the boom is spreading to other 
parts of global investment. There is little sign of that – in part, perhaps, because only 
companies buying their own GPUs and server would count as capex, while paying for 
tokens and cloud services to embed AI in workflows would count as operational costs.  A 
bottom-up aggregation of  +3,000 companies suggests capex growth will rise only 
slightly, from 7.2% in 2026 to 7.6% in 2027, and fade into 2028, while hyperscalers 
(which are 'only' a third of global capex by weight) would  account for 80% of global 
investment growth next year.
2. AI monetization – what do we know so far?
AI monetisation needs to reach $2-4tr annually to justify $6-8tr of cumulative 2025-30 
capex, with current applications potentially clearing the lower hurdle. Productivity is the 
largest opportunity at $0.7-2.1tr by 2030, as slowing labor-force growth and fading 
globalisation gains increase the need for firms to raise output per worker. Consumer and 
B2B demand take potential AI compute revenue to $1.1-3.9tr, or ~$2.4tr at the 
midpoint, clearing the low hurdle. Beyond 2030, next-generation products such as 
autonomous mobility, robots, AI-generated media and personalised medicine could add 
$0.3-1.2tr of cloud revenue, lifting the midpoint to ~$3.3tr and clearing the central 
hurdle. Cloud growth accelerated to ~100% q/q annualised in Q3, nearly three times 
Q1, suggesting adoption has entered the steep phase of the S-curve even as consensus 
assumes dollar growth flattens in 2H27. Risks are two-sided, but we lean to the upside: 
better power availability could extend the steep adoption phase through 2028, and 
markets are likely to price greater confidence in capex sustainability during 2027.
3. Data center build-out: what are the constraints and the growth implications?
Several headwinds are dovetailing to potentially create binding constraints on the pace 
of the AI buildout. Forecasts for data-center compute installation and US power grid 
additions imply a shortage of approximately 100 GW of power by 2030. Hundreds of 
local moratoriums are slowing the permit approval process for planned data centers, 
and federal legislation has been proposed to limit land and power availability. Access to 
necessary inputs, including specialized labor, imported equipment and raw materials, 
also create bottlenecks that can throttle data center construction. These threaten to limit 
the US expansion, as AI-related investment and associated spillovers account for about 
1.5 pp of GDP growth through Q3 2026.
4. Are Chip stocks still attractive?
The AI cycle has further to run. The growth of Inference relative to training should 
broaden demand beyond GPUs into CPUs and memory. Supply discipline distinguishes 
this chips cycle. Producer capex is expected to rise ~200–300% between 2024 and 
2027, versus ~600–700% for hyperscalers. Technical barriers keep semiconductor 
bottlenecks concentrated. UBS analysts expect memory undersupply until 2Q28. We 
favour memory and leading-edge foundry. Five-year cumulative FCF is forecast at 80–
160% of current market cap for the three large memory producers, yet they trade at an 
average ~4x NTM P/E. Memory stocks historically peak two to three quarters before 
pricing. With prices expected to peak around mid-2028, we remain bullish into end-
2027. Even if growth were to flatten in 2028, cumulative cash generation through this 
cycle would remain extraordinarily large relative to current market values, with 
management teams already signalling materially higher shareholder payout. 
Competition from China remains a medium-term risk, but competition is still 
concentrated in conventional memory rather than HBM and advanced DRAM at this 
stage.
5. Why are we not seeing AI in the productivity data?
A broad AI-driven productivity boom remains unproven: recent US gains are modest 
relative to the internet-era acceleration and have not spread globally. Surveys indicate 
widespread but shallow adoption and highly concentrated spending. Sectoral evidence 
likewise shows that  AI adoption is highest in high productivity sectors, but does not 
explain the post-2023 acceleration in productivity. Implementation costs could be 
delaying the benefits through a “J-curve,” with infrastructure investment and capital 

Global Economics & Markets Outlook 9 November 2026
ab 22
deepening preceding improvements in total factor productivity. Ultimately, today’s 
investment boom is no guarantee of future gains: the decisive test is whether AI 
transforms production processes broadly enough to generate sustained TFP growth, 
which has not picked up yet in the latest data.
6. What are the implications of AI for labour markets?
AI is already disrupting parts of the US labour market, but its aggregate effects remain 
modest relative to normal job flows. As adoption spreads, we expect higher productivity 
and greater job creation and destruction, with the manufacturing experience of the 
1990s illustrating how sector-specific job losses can coexist with a strong broader labor 
market. The impact of AI on the labour market therefore hinges less on displacement 
alone than on the economy’s ability to create new opportunities and workers ability to 
transition into them.
Theme 2: A new Macro Regime
7. Are US corporate profits decoupling from economic fundamentals?
US corporate profits are at a record high relative to the economy. Pre-tax profits of all US 
corporations, listed and unlisted, are running at 14½% of GDP (NIPA basis), double their 
mid-1980s share, despite slower economic growth. The rise is mostly domestic (6pp of 
the 7pp increase) and concentrated in four sectors: financials, computers & electronics, 
retail and information. Together they account for 70% of the increase since the mid-
1980s, and nearly 90% of the increase over the past five years. Our regressions attribute 
most of the long-run rise to globalisation up to around 2010, and to tax changes that 
made it more attractive for book income as profits rather than wages (a shift from C 
corporations to S corporation or pass-through entities). In the past three years, however, 
profits have outrun these drivers. The unexplained residual has widened sharply, 
coinciding with the surge in tech profits. S&P 500 net income, which historically ran at 
about half of economy-wide profits, has risen to 90% of after-tax national profits.
8. What’s driving bond yields?
Global bond yields rose in 2026 driven by resilient growth, an inflation shock, and fiscal 
concerns. European and UK yields were driven mainly by rising inflation expectations 
after the energy supply shock, while higher US Treasury yields primarily reflected an 
increase in the market-implied neutral rate. Short run r* matters for market 
expectations, and estimates could move lower if supply shocks fade and productivity 
growth strengthens. Longer term, however, term premiums are likely to rise as growing 
Treasury and investment-grade corporate issuance increases duration supply while 
traditional demand weakens amid shrinking central bank balance sheets and reduced 
foreign-official participation. Consequently, policy-rate expectations should decline in 
2027–28, but higher term premiums may keep sovereign curves under steepening 
pressure. Ten-year yields are projected to reach 4.80% for Treasuries, 3.25% for bunds, 
and 5.00% for gilts by year-end 2027.
9. Can policy makers slow the rise of public debt?
Public debt in advanced economies is projected to rise by an average of 2pp of GDP a 
year through 2028, with little sign of slowing thereafter. The core problem is that 
primary deficits that opened up during recessions have been slow to close, even with 
economies near full employment and global growth at trend. The immediate challenge 
is that higher bond yields will increasingly reprice the cheap legacy debt of the 2010-20 
era as it matures: we estimate that each 1pp rise in yields adds, on average, 12bp of GDP 
to interest costs in the first year (42bp in the US) and 54bp by year five. Brazil remains 
furthest from stabilising its debt, needing a primary adjustment of around 5pp of GDP 
on our 2027 forecasts, largely because of its high real interest rate. China, France, 
Germany and the US follow, each roughly 3pp of GDP from a debt-stabilising primary 
balance (4pp for the US once its rising interest bill is included). These estimates exclude 
structural pressures from ageing and pensions; in the US, the exhaustion of the Social 
Security Trust Fund alone could require a further 1½pp of GDP if funded from the federal 
budget.
10. How tight are global energy markets?
Global energy markets are very tight as oil and gas exports from the Middle East remain 
disrupted but this tightness could ease pretty quickly, should we see a conflict resolution 
and full recovery of flows. The reduced buffers, increased demand to rebuild inventories 
and likely lingering risk premium mean that prices would not necessarily return 

Global Economics & Markets Outlook 9 November 2026
ab 23
immediately return to pre-conflict levels. Within oil, we see greater tightness in oil 
products than in crude, as disruption to Russian exports adds to Middle East constraints. 
LNG flows have not covered to the same extent as crude and the outlook is for global 
gas markets to remain tight next year amid low inventories.  
11. Inflation in an era of supply shocks, ageing and super El-Niño; what is the new
normal? 
The list of recent supply shocks continues to grow: the pandemic, the Russia-Ukraine 
war, tariff increases, rising crude oil prices. Geopolitical risks and economic uncertainty 
have increased, and this year a super El Niño has appeared. Population aging and 
increased productivity growth are potential upcoming supply shocks. These supply 
shocks make inflation more variable, but whether more variable inflation turns into a 
higher average level of inflation depends on the willingness of fiscal and monetary 
authorities to accommodate the shocks.
Theme 3: Asset Allocation
12. Is low volatility a bug or a feature, and what does that mean for markets?
In equities, low correlation (and volatility, by proxy) is now a structural feature – record 
Tech+/AI concentration in index weight and earnings has made the S&P 500 much less 
sensitive to macro shocks, and elevated dispersion is offsetting single stock-level 
volatility that has moved structurally higher. Leveraged ETFs, systematic option supply, 
and retail momentum chasing have amplified this dynamic. The result is a more fragile 
market prone to frequent but fleeting volatility bouts, though correlated drawdowns 
have become increasingly difficult to trigger. Increased market breadth beyond Tech+ 
(the healthier outcome) or the AI trade faltering are key to breaking this dynamic. In 
rates, the 'bug' is already being fixed. Volatility is already repricing higher on higher 
rates, a less guidance-driven Fed under Warsh, fiscal risks (notably France), and next 
year's slew of elections. FX volatility has lagged but should rise as central bank policy 
diverges near restrictive levels. Falling CNY volatility, Japanese intervention, and subdued 
equity volatility should cap the move, supporting a continued selective approach to FX 
volatility.
13. Does fixed income have a role in portfolios?
Fixed income has endured a difficult five years: global bonds returned -2.6% annualised 
versus 12% for equities, diversification deteriorated as bond-equity correlations turned 
positive, and investor allocations shifted heavily towards equities. Yet we think the case 
for bonds is strengthening. Markets are pricing terminal rates too high, reflecting 
excessive optimism around capex, productivity and r*. Evidence of a broad capex or 
productivity boom remains limited, while weaker demographics should restrain global 
neutral rates. We therefore expect front-end and belly yields to fall. The long end faces a 
different challenge: large fiscal deficits, rising public debt and weaker foreign demand 
for Treasuries should push term premia higher. However, falling inflation should 
eventually restore negative bond-equity correlation, increasing Treasuries’ diversification 
value and limiting this rise. The result should be a bull-steepening US curve, with 
modestly lower long-end yields. Portfolio optimisation consequently points towards 
materially higher fixed-income allocations as bonds regain both return and 
diversification appeal.
14. Will carry do well in 2027?
Carry has performed strongly since 2022, but its prospects are weaker in 2027. Our 
systematic FX strategy shows carry thrives when cross-asset volatility and credit-funding 
stress are low, and when equities perform well, particularly when global equities 
outperform the US. High US rates and inflation are not necessarily obstacles. Our 2027 
baseline of trend growth, above-trend inflation and reasonable global equity returns 
therefore remains supportive. However, the strongest tailwinds look exhausted: volatility 
and funding conditions have limited room to improve, while geopolitical risks skew the 
growth-inflation mix unfavourably. Bottom-up signals are also less compelling. High-
yielding EM currencies have limited appreciation potential, while policy support should 
prevent traditional funding currencies such as JPY from weakening materially. We 
therefore favour selective EM carry, including BRL and COP funded in EUR, rather than 
broad FX carry. Better opportunities may ultimately emerge in credit, particularly 
European banks and high yield, and potentially in selling technology equity volatility.

Global Economics & Markets Outlook 9 November 2026
ab 24
15. Gold and stablecoins: threats to dollar dominance?
Gold and stablecoins are attracting more attention, but neither looks close to 
challenging the US dollar’s dominant role. Gold is taking on a more active role in 
selected emerging markets, including in liquidity management and efforts to support 
local currencies. However, much of its growing share of central-bank reserves simply 
reflects higher gold prices, rather than a broad shift away from the dollar. Plans to use 
gold more widely as an alternative currency are still at an early stage. Stablecoin 
adoption has also grown quickly, although most activity remains tied to crypto trading 
rather than  payments.  Meanwhile, Asian investment continues to flow into dollar assets, 
and alternative fiat currencies face constraints of their own. There is also an important 
twist: because almost all stablecoins are dollar-pegged, wider adoption could make 
dollar-based payments more accessible—and ultimately strengthen the dollar’s position 
rather than weaken it.
16. Do we need to worry about private credit and private equity?
Private credit enters 2027 with stress deferred, not resolved. We expect US default rates 
to rise 300bp to 7–8% by year-end as higher rates meet refinancing needs for weaker 
2020–21 vintages and software borrowers. AI disruption could pressure pricing, seat 
growth and retention, driving outsized EBITDA declines. Liquidity is the second fault line. 
Selective PE exits, elevated BDC redemptions and weaker inflows constrain distributions. 
Dividend recaps, NAV financing and structured vehicles push layered financing to an 
estimated $1.5tn, supporting distributions by extending or repackaging risk rather than 
reducing it. Our base case remains dispersion, not contagion, with stress concentrated in 
weaker borrowers, older vintages and software. Yet reliance on bank and insurer 
funding, leverage and financial engineering creates more transmission channels. The 
defining question for 2027 is whether private markets can refinance and realise assets 
organically, or merely defer and redistribute risk.
Theme 4: Regional Debates
17. How strong is the case for Chinese equities?
Chinese equities in 2026 have been shaped by a combination of gradual policy 
tightening, limited broad-based stimulus, geopolitical and tariff concerns, and a 
powerful but increasingly volatile AI theme. Following the recent tech-led sell-off, 
valuations and positioning have reset significantly with MSCI China trading at around 
9.5x 12M forward P/E, 1.2 standard deviations below its historical average, and fast-
money positioning has returned to beginning-of-year levels, even as AI hardware 
earnings remain robust with 12–15% upgrades over the past three months. We believe 
the market has priced in an overly pessimistic outlook for AI fundamentals, leaving risk/
reward increasingly attractive despite still-fragile sentiment. A more sustained recovery 
in China equities would likely require catalysts, including more forceful policy support, 
further stabilization in the property market, progress in domestic HBM production, 
additional SOE fund purchases, and improving earnings revisions. 
18. Will Europe’s resilience last?
Eurozone growth has remained resilient despite geopolitical tensions and high energy 
prices, thanks to fiscal spending, robust labour markets, lower energy intensity, AI-
related activity and some transitory factors. We forecast growth to rise from 1.1% in 
2026 to 1.2% in 2027, helped by continued fiscal stimulus, lower energy prices and 
improving investment and exports, also related to AI. However, renewed geopolitical 
tensions and higher energy prices remain key downside risks. European equities show 
similar resilience: pent-up demand following the 2025 tariff uncertainty, accelerating 
German spending and AI-related investment are supportive, with consensus forecasting 
nearly 20% Stoxx 600 EPS growth in 2026 and 10% in 2027. Financials, industrials and 
tech hardware represent c. 51% of the European index, or almost 60% when energy 
and utilities are included. Much of the market is therefore exposed to structurally or 
politically supported demand, suggesting equities could remain resilient if oil prices 
moderate to c. $80/bbl in 2027, as UBS forecasts.
19. Has Japan beaten deflation, and what does it mean for global assets?
Yes, but the implications for global assets look less ominous than feared. Japan’s 
inflation expectations have reset around 2%, with the BoJ likely to normalise policy rate 
to 2% by 3Q27 (UBSF) and 10y JGB yields reaching 3.2% (40bps wider). Fading policy 
tailwinds and rich valuations weaken TOPIX’s relative appeal. But disruptive repatriation 
looks unlikely: Portfolio flows remain within historical ranges, JPY short-end rates are 
unattractive versus DM majors, and 10y real-yield spreads vs. US/EU are near 20-year 

Global Economics & Markets Outlook 9 November 2026
ab 25
lows. Moreover, Japanese overseas assets holdings, as a share of recipients' bonds/
equity markets, average only ~5% across the top ten destinations. Yen-funded 
leveraged carry trades are a risk, but historical unwinds owed more to VaR/risk-asset 
shocks and the pace of relative-rate adjustment than to absolute JPY rates. For instance, 
JPY gained 14% in 3Q24 even when the 2y JP-US spread was 175bps tighter than today. 
Risks to RoW assets would rise if fiscal policy turns expansionary or public asset 
managers are directed to shift overseas allocations.
Theme 5: Politics
20. Implications of the US and French elections?
Political uncertainty is rising on both sides of the Atlantic, following increased 
Democratic control of the US Congress and ahead of France’s April/May 2027 
presidential election. In the US, greater policy gridlock raises shutdown and fiscal risks, 
while potentially increasing scrutiny of AI/data-center investment and complicating 
appointments to the Federal Reserve Board. Upcoming negotiations over federal 
funding and the debt ceiling could result in wider deficits, while tighter oversight of data 
centers poses a risk to an important driver of US growth and equity markets. In France, 
Marine Le Pen currently leads presidential polling, with her policy platform potentially 
implying weaker European integration and a more uncertain fiscal trajectory. France’s 
already challenging fiscal position and fragmented politics point to prolonged 
uncertainty, likely keeping sovereign spreads under pressure and bringing the ECB’s 
possible policy response, including TPI, into greater focus.

Global Economics & Markets Outlook 9 November 2026
ab 26
Global Economics & Markets Outlook
UBS Research
1. Are we on the cusp of a global tech-driven
investment cycle?
The AI infrastructure build-out (data centres, power, cooling, networks and chips) could 
cost $10.3trn over 2025–32, according to a recent Brookings estimate. At roughly 3.6% 
of US GDP a year, that would make it the largest infrastructure investment boom in US 
history, bigger than the canal, railroad, electrification, highway or telecoms booms, and 
it would double the electricity consumption of the US residential sector.  The question is 
whether the boom is spreading to other parts of global investment, where AI spending 
is less about building infrastructure and more about using it to embed AI in workflows. 
The core of the boom, at least, keeps growing. Our tech analysts' bottom-up estimate of 
2027 AI capex has risen from $1.2trn in June to $1.45trn a few weeks ago, and to close 
to $1.8trn now, partly on SpaceX's sharply higher guidance. (Figure 62Hyperscaler capex to increase from
 $50bn to $1 trilion in 2026, and further to nearly $1.8trn in 2027). But not all of 
this is extra compute: surging memory prices alone add $463bn to 2026–27E capex. 
Nominal capex doubles in 2026E to $1trn and rises another 80% in 2027E, but adjusted 
for memory prices the real increase is  roughly half of that  over those two years. If the 
growth in capex slows, even as the level rises, the GDP growth contribution should start 
to decelerate. If investment is to support growth beyond that, it needs to broaden. 
We may be in the early phase of the build-out, given how much additional compute low 
enterprise adoption rates imply. So why do our AI capex forecasts stop rising after 2027? 
Partly because hyperscalers give little multi-year capex guidance (Oracle excepted), and 
partly because longer-run risks run both ways. Capex could be higher if the two big 
frontier labs start building their own data centers. Or it could be lower if technology cuts 
the compute needed per unit of AI use, through smaller models trained from larger 
ones, more efficient model designs, lower-precision computing and cheaper chips. So 
far,  falling costs have tended to raise total demand rather than reduce spending. 
Outside AI infrastructure, however, there are few signs of a broader investment boom. In 
the US, five technology sectors, just 18% of business fixed investment, account for all of 
its growth. The rest has been flat in real terms for more than two years (Figure 64However, outside of US tech, there has ben no growth in US busines fixed investm
ent for >2 years). 
Globally, too, there is little sign of a pickup. Figure 65Global investm
ent is runing at its long-run average growth rate shows z-scores of annual 
investment growth across 49 economies, and the median economy is exactly at its long-
run average. The chart also highlights the economies at the heart of the AI supply chain. 
On IMF data, China and the US account for nearly 80% of AI-related imports, as the 
main buyers of computing hardware and builders of data centers. Taiwan, Korea, 
Malaysia and the Netherlands supply over 70% of AI-related exports. Yet even among 
these, China, South Korea, and the Netherlands have below-average investment 
growth, and Malaysia is barely above its historical average. 
Although projected AI capex next year is enormous, equivalent to over 5% of US GDP, it 
is extremely concentrated: five companies account for 85% of the total. Outside the US, 
weakness in other sectors appears to be offsetting AI-related spend. One way to see 
where global capex is likely to pick up is to look at forecasts for all the companies 
covered by UBS Research. We have capex forecasts for 3,342 companies, totalling over 
$4trn in 2026, or roughly a fifth to a quarter of global business fixed investment 
(excluding government and housing). Excluding the hyperscalers, we expect capex 
growth to edge up only slightly, from 7.2% in 2026 to 7.6% in 2027 (Figure 66 Analysts se ex-hyperscaler capex growth edging up to 7.6%
 y/y in 2027 (from
 7.2%
), fading intra-year). 
Momentum fades through the end of 2027 rather than building, partly because 
technology investment outside the hyperscalers is expected to slow. The largest 
accelerations are expected in financials, transportation & infrastructure, and real estate. 
The largest decelerations are in industrials & capital goods, health care and technology.
The credit impulse, however, has turned more supportive of investment (Figure 61DM
 credit im
pulse vs investm
ent). 
With central banks again responding to an energy price shock, that recovery may be 
tested, but so far, higher borrowing costs have not derailed it, and US capex intentions 
have started to pick up. Much of the strength in both global investment and the global 
credit impulse, however, is in the US, where investment growth is driven largely by AI 
capex. US capex strength should not be mistaken for global strength.
Finally, wider AI adoption will not necessarily lift capex. Building data centers is capital-
intensive, but companies using AI mostly pay for it through tokens and cloud services, 
which are operating costs. Only buying their own GPUs or servers would count as capex.

2. AI monetization – what do we know so far?
Hyperscaler, neocloud and other AI capex is expected to approach ~$1.75tr in 2027 (ex-
China). To earn a 10% unlevered return on cumulative 2025-30 capex, every $1tr 
invested needs ~$375bn of annual revenue at a 55% cash margin. This reflects the 
shorter useful life of chips, servers and networking, and longer life of buildings. Flexing 
assumptions for asset lives and margins, the required revenue range is $2-4tr, which is 
1.3-2.6% of 2030E global GDP (Figure 68Required AI revenue based on 2025-30E capex and operating cash flow m
argin). The US currently spends 2.5% of GDP on 
software. 
 Slower labor force growth amid aging and deglobalisation trends means firms need AI 
to raise output per worker to maintain profit growth. To size what firms can pay for 
improved productivity from AI, we start from US private wages across 19 industries, 
weight Penn Wharton's task exposure scores by each industry's occupational mix, and 
scale to the world with IMF GDP and ILO labor shares, trimming exposure outside the US 
for its larger farm/factory workforce (Figure 69AI exposure is concentrated in high-wage, knowledge-intensive industries). That puts $14-21tr of 2030 global pay 
in work that AI can make more productive (Figure 70AI's econom
ic potential spans trilions in productivity, labor incom
e and new product revenue). Assuming AI is used on 10-20% 
of exposed work by 2030 and vendors retain 40-60% of the value created, enterprise AI 
revenue can reach $0.7-2.1tr. A separate seat-based approach produces a similar $0.7-
2.1tr range, based on 575-770mm paying workers spending $150-250 a month. 
Consumers are the next payer. BEA data show the PC and internet eras each lifted device 
and digital services by 70-96bp of PCE over 8-10yrs, with 60-72bp in the best five years 
(Figure 72Consum
er tech products and services jum
ped during the PC and internet eras, as did software and R&D). AI subscriptions, devices and services could generate $0.5-1.2tr of 
incremental sales by 2030, or 60-145bp of global consumption - with the high end 
assuming share loss of other categories. Subscriptions could account for $60-310bn, 
but that hinges on consumers converting to paid users at 2-4x the current level. Of this 
$0.5-1.2tr revenue, we assume $110-510bn reaches cloud sales, because devices and 
maturing apps retain some economics. 
Businesses will also sell each other AI products beyond workforce productivity, including 
software, security, advertising, purchasing agents, autonomous machines and AI-led 
R&D. Software plus R&D rose from 1.5% of US GDP in 1980 to 5.2% in 2025, and 
gained 117bp in the internet era. We estimate a $1.5-3.0tr B2B market, of which $0.3-
1.3tr can accrue to clouds. Adding the three layers gives $1.1-3.9tr of AI compute 
revenue in 2030, or ~$2.5tr at the midpoint (Figure 71Potential AI com
pute revenues could justify a large share of projected capital spending). Hence, current applications can 
potentially clear a lower revenue hurdle, while next-generation applications become 
more important if hyperscaler capex is subsequently revised higher.
 Next-gen products include autonomous mobility, robots, AI-generated media and 
personalized medicine, where the TAM is large but the timing is further out as adoption 
typically speeds up later in an innovation cycle. If these products scale in the 2030s, they 
could add $0.3-1.2tr of revenue for clouds, taking the total to $1.4-5.1tr. The midpoint 
would then reach ~$3.3tr and clear the central hurdle, with the upper bound clearing 
the high hurdle of $4.0tr. Although AI capex is front-loaded into 2026-30, the products 
needed to earn the full return are likely to mature in the following decade.
Annualized q/q cloud growth was ~100% in Q3 (+$86bn) nearly tripling vs Q1 (~
$31bn), on a ~$540bn run rate (Figure 73Cloud revenue surge shows AI com
pute dem
and on stepening adoption curve). AI compute demand moved to the steep 
part of the S-curve, where more dollars are added until adoption is about half done. 
Consensus instead has dollar growth flattening in 2H27, treating next year as the 
midpoint of adoption, and roughly matches what constrained power allows at today's 
revenue per MW. On those numbers, 2030 cloud revenue of ~$2.6tr, which includes 
legacy cloud, clears only the low end of the hurdle. However, UBS semis team's chip-
based forecast implies ~50GW can be deployed in 2027 (up ~2x) if behind-the-meter 
solutions can address power constraints. If the steep phase runs through 2028, AI 
revenue can reach the midpoint, and markets are likely to price that during 2027. 
 Risks are two-sided, and next year should show which way. On the upside, the steep S-
curve phase should run into 2028 and can lift 2030 AI revenue leading to confidence in 
cloud cash flow and capex sustainability, with faster behind-the-meter power key, while 
new products could arrive sooner as models improve. On the downside, bans and limits 
on new data centers are the biggest risk. AI prices could also fall faster than usage rises 
amid competition, slowing lab revenue before enterprises adopt at scale.

Global Economics & Markets Outlook
UBS Research
3. Data center build-out: what are the constraints
and the growth implications?
Construction of data centers and related infrastructure is critical to the US economic 
expansion. The AI component of business fixed investment accounted for between one-
quarter and one-third of GDP growth in the most recent three quarters (read more here). 
In our base case,  business fixed investment in equipment remains robust, up 15.3% Q4/
Q4 in 2027 versus 15.5% in 2026.  While the persistence of equipment investment over 
the last two years has been striking, higher financing costs, shortages of necessary 
inputs for the build-out, and public opposition turning into policy constraints are risks to 
the outlook. 
 Our colleagues covering semiconductors estimate total data-center compute capacity 
additions of 340 gigawatts from 2026-2030, 60%-70% of which is planned for the US. 
By comparison, our utilities colleagues see US power grid additions of 120GW over the 
same period, implying a cumulative gap of approximately 100 GW between compute 
and power (read more here), and potentially leading to a slower pace of construction. 
Pew Research found 40% of adults think the impact of AI will be net negative for society 
over the next 20 years, as compared to just 16% who think there will be a positive 
impact (link here). Souring sentiment has encouraged policymakers to intervene and 
slow the pace of construction and AI proliferation.  Federal legislative efforts have largely 
focused on creating environmental and land-use protections that would slow the pace 
of construction, limit available land, and require data centers to develop and/or access 
off-grid power sources.  The US also relies on imported minerals necessary for data 
centers. The USGS estimated 45% of copper consumed is imported, along with 47% of 
aluminum, 80% of rare earth elements and 75% of barite used for magnets and data 
storage (link here). Just as imported raw materials are subject to trade-policy risks, so too 
are imports of intermediate goods intended for installation in data centers. 
State and local governments are increasingly scrutinizing construction plans and the 
technology.A total of 529  moratoriums on data-center construction have been 
announced by local governments since January 2013, with the volume of 
announcements rising sharply in 2026. At the time of our writing, there are 415 active 
local moratoriums (link here). States are also more heavily scrutinizing AI models, and 
any limitation on the rollout and utilization of AI  as a technology reduces the viability of 
data centers intended for model training and inference.
 Higher rates and wider credit spreads have the potential to reduce expected data-center 
construction by constraining weaker issuers from accessing the debt market. In our 
Credit Strategy colleagues' base case for 2027, high-yield tech issuance rises to 
approximately $100 billion from $70 billion in 2026, but risk is skewed to the downside. 
If rates and spreads remain unchanged at current levels, borrowers' willingness and 
ability to raise debt financing will likely decline, putting the marginal project at risk of 
cancellation. In that case, the data-center build-out and its contribution to the 
continued economic expansion could be contingent upon tech companies' willingness 
and ability to access other expensive, dilutive, or otherwise unattractive financing 
options. 
 There are constraints in the real economy that have the potential to weigh on the pace 
of construction and AI-supported GDP growth, as well. The unemployment rate among 
construction workers has remained below 5.0% on a 12-month rolling basis for over 
four years, and while year-on-year wage growth is just 2.9% overall, electrical 
contractors have had year-on-year wage growth above 6.0% since the start of 2026, 
pointing to a relative shortage of specialized skills necessary for outfitting data centers.
While we expect data-center construction and investment in AI to continue supporting 
headline growth, constraints pose a potentially significant risk. Prospects for bringing 
enough power online to support expected data-center compute are questionable. Policy 
risks are mounting, particularly at the state and local level, potentially leading to delays, 
and elevated financing costs make the marginal project unviable. The US workforce 
already appears to be short the skilled tradesmen necessary for standing up all the 
planned data centers.

4. Are Chip stocks still attractive?
 The big picture is one where AI and compute demand is likely to rise aggressively over 
the coming years. Only 8% of enterprises have deployed AI agents at scale. The results 
of the US midterm elections notwithstanding, UBS analysts expect ~380GW of data 
centre capacity to be added across 2026-30,  compared to 133GW operational currently 
(Randy Abrams & Tim Arcuri). Some of these projects may have to shift across the 
US states, potentially to other countries, and may be delayed in their rollout. But the 1-
3y forecast on AI capex and the related demand for compute remains unchanged. 
Who will benefit from this? One way to assess the durability of the AI cycle is to split 
the ecosystem into three layers. First, at  the model layer, competition is rising and 
barriers to entry are relatively low (in a world of open weight models). Second, 
Hyperscalers, have defensible positions supported by scale, capital, distribution, and 
customer relationships. But substantial capacity is being added ahead of proven end-
demand monetisation. Third, the supplier layer, is more mixed: power, metals and real 
estate are more competitive industries, while semis (GPUs, leading-edge foundry and 
HBM) remain highly concentrated. Barriers here reflect technical capabilities, giving 
suppliers controlling critical  bottlenecks the strongest bargaining power. 
What is different in this cycle – surging demand vs slow moving supply. AI 
compute demand is unrelenting into 2027, with data centre capex expected to grow 
75% vs 2026, as shifts from chat to reasoning to coding and agents drive requirements. 
This has been met by supply tightness in a semis industry that struggles to expand 
capacity quickly, with a handful of companies at the cutting edge of AI compute. 2029 
Capex across Micron, SK Hynix, Samsung and TSMC is expected to be ~2-3x 2024 levels, 
versus ~8-9x for hyperscalers (Figure 80Actual and estim
ated capex (FY2024 Q1 =10) ). UBS memory analysts expect memory to 
remain undersupplied until 2Q28, with prices rising more than 600% through the cycle. 
The result is a once-in-a-generation demand-supply mismatch (Figure 81M
em
ory capex relative to m
em
ory price increase is low vs history& Figure 82Suply tightnes wil kep prices elevated as dem
and continues to grow). 
Further, as we shift from training to inference, demand for CPUs and memory increases 
relative to GPUs (Figure 84CPU and DRAM
 dem
and increases as we transition form
 training to inference), with our team expecting the CPU-to-GPU attach ratio to rise 
~3x and DRAM per accelerator 4x through 2030 (Arcuri). 
What if hyperscaler capex slows into 2028? At this point the 2028 capex schedule 
for hyperscalers is unclear, but it would not surprise us if hyperscaler AI capex growth 
decelerates. Semiconductor earnings growth should also slow commensurately, and 
UBS memory analysts expect pricing to peak around mid-2028. Historically, memory 
stocks have peaked two to three quarters ahead of profits, suggesting support  at least 
through the first half of '27. But the more important difference this cycle is the cash-flow 
uplift. Cumulative FCF over the next five years is expected to reach 100-180% of current 
market cap for the three large memory players, while longer-term customer agreements 
provide greater visibility (Figure 83M
em
ory com
panies' forecast FCFs exced curent m
arket cap). Much of that cash is also coming back: SK Hynix  
plans to return at least 50% of cumulative FCF during 2025–27 (Gaudois), while 
Samsung  has committed to return 50% of FCF in 2026 (Gaudois). Seen differently, the 
market believes nearly 70% of this projected FCF  does not come through  
Could China’s semiconductor production break this cycle? We think not. China is 
a competitive risk, but not yet at the industry's key AI bottlenecks. The semiconductor 
layer is split into largely non-overlapping China and US-led ecosystems, while even 
China still imports a substantial volume of AI hardware. The more immediate threat is in 
traditional applications as Korean and Taiwanese suppliers prioritise more profitable AI 
demand. CXMT's global DRAM bit share is expected to rise from ~7% in 2025 to ~9% 
by 2027, while its China server DRAM share could reach ~20% by 2028 vs 12% in 2025. 
China is therefore a genuine medium-term competitive risk in conventional memory, but 
not one that breaks the current AI-led cycle (Figure 85AI Driven dem
and vs traditional dem
and for m
em
ory). 
The spells of selling in chips stocks this year were not all fundamentally driven. 
Over the previous 12-15m, chip stocks increasingly became aligned with the 
Momentum Style. Any algos and CTAs investing in Momentum as an equity factor were 
essentially buying semiconductor stocks. Equally, though, whatever subsequently hurt 
the Momentum factor also hurt semiconductors, with no change in fundamentals of the 
companies. Our work on Momentum has shown that the Momentum dislikes a liquidity 
shock – any sudden change in the risk-free rate or risk premia.  We believe this liquidity 
shock is nearly past, and, as a result, the headwinds for semi stocks are thinning.

5. Why are we not seeing AI in the productivity
data?
Short answer: it’s still too early to tell, and there are good reasons to expect a lag. US 
labour productivity in the non-farm business sector has been growing  faster than its pre-
pandemic trend since around mid-’22, roughly when the first high-performing AI 
systems came online at scale (Figure 86The deviation in US labour productivity from
 the pre-pandem
ic trend is sm
al). However, growth over the most recent four 
quarters has been just 2.1%, relatively subdued, and in macroeconomic terms, three 
years do not make a trend. Plus, the recent experience is far  shallower than the trend 
pick-up in the mid-90s when the internet-based GPT spread deep into the fabric of the 
economy. Nor has AI spread through global productivity (defined as GDP per employee 
for cross-country comparability) like the internet did in the '90s: when we compute pre-
pandemic trends for economies representing more than 85% of world GDP, 42 of the 47 
deviations from trend since 23Q1 are smaller than the one for the US (Figure 87And globaly there are only 5 econom
ies (out of 48) that have sen a larger pick-up). 
Adoption, meanwhile, is  a mile wide but an inch deep, and despite a flood of anecdotes, 
a broad, causally identified macroeconomic acceleration due to AI remains unproven. 
The October ’26 update of the NBER survey of 9K executives in seven countries reports 
an AI contribution of 0.2% to annual labour productivity growth since ’23, but this is 
executives’ attribution, not a causal national-accounts estimate. The same update shows 
AI spending is highly concentrated, with just 2% of firms accounting for over half of the 
total. Furthermore, while 80% of executives report regular use of AI, that amounts to a 
paltry 2.1 hours per week. Executive hours do not measure workforce-wide usage but 
such shallow adoption is likely pervasive across occupations. Our own UBS Evidence Lab 
survey  (March ’26) tells the same story: only 19% of respondents said their company 
was using AI at scale across business functions, up from 17% six months earlier.
Sectoral data deepens the puzzle: industries that use more AI  have high productivity, but 
they are not the ones driving the post-'23 pickup reported above. Updating a study by 
the Kansas City Fed, Figure 90High productivity growth sectors have high AI adoption rates - but that's corelation, not causation plots sectoral contributions to aggregate productivity 
growth from the SF Fed against AI adoption rates from Census' BTOS survey: higher 
growth sectors do tend to take on more AI—with the caveat that causality may run both 
ways. But Figure 91AI adoption rates explain virtualy none of the increase in productivity growth since '23 shows that the increase in productivity growth since '23 is essentially 
uncorrelated with adoption rates (the slope is statistically insignificant). Case in point: 
manufacturing has contributed more to the aggregate increase than the information 
sector despite evidently lower take-up and lower growth. Other forces may explain the 
pickup instead; for example, firms may be reaping the post-pandemic rewards from 
reorganizing themselves more productively around new processes such as work-from-
home arrangements. 
Arguably, we shouldn't be expecting to see productivity gains at the macro scale 
anyway, because training, integration, and redesign around workflow bottlenecks 
absorb resources before benefits emerge. This is the 'J-curve' at work, where 
implementation costs precede returns to the new technology. Labour productivity can 
be decomposed as the sum of capital deepening and total factor productivity (TFP)—
broadly, more output per person from more capital and from new processes, 
respectively. Conceptually, such adjustment costs should be reflected in large part in TFP 
rather than capital deepening, which captures instead the rewards to accumulated 
capex per head (although the measurement  of such intangible features is fraught with 
difficulty). While US labor productivity growth is currently running above its pre-
pandemic trend as described above,  growth in TFP does appear to have stalled recently, 
consistent with the J-curve effect in which  capital deepening—the infrastructure 
buildout—runs ahead of TFP gains (Figure 88Labour productivity is runing hot, but TFP is staling).  At a lower frequency, investment growth 
and the two productivity components show no consistent correlation over the past 50 
years,  but in the '90s  high investment rates  clearly led productivity growth by about 
three years (Figure 89Investm
ent growth led TFP growth and capital depening by ~3 years in the '90s). If that lead is the ‘tell’ for the spread of a new GPT, today's high 
investment rates  have baked in higher productivity growth over the next few years. The 
'80s, however, provide a cautionary counterpoint: the oil shocks prompted firms to 
replace obsolete capital with more energy-efficient machines (the capital deepening part 
of labour productivity) but TFP grew only modestly, as the conversion did not require 
fundamentally new processes. Today’s investment boom is not proof of tomorrow’s 
productivity acceleration; the decisive test is whether adoption reshapes production 
broadly enough to deliver sustained TFP gains.
