6. What are the implications of AI for labour
markets?
The impact of AI is already visible in the labor market, but so far, the macroeconomic 
effects remain modest relative to the enormous scale of underlying US labor market 
flows. Layoff announcements citing artificial intelligence have increased, and labor 
demand appears softer in some of the occupations and sectors most exposed to 
automation, such as information. At the same time, adoption is advancing. Census data 
suggest businesses' current and planned use of AI continues to climb, particularly in 
information services, finance, and professional and business services. 
Yet, it is important to keep these developments in perspective. Every quarter, the US 
economy creates and destroys ~8 million jobs, with workers constantly moving between 
employers and industries. Against that backdrop, AI-related job losses remain relatively 
small, even if they are becoming increasingly visible in anecdotal reports, corporate 
announcements, and news headlines. The evidence points to the early stages of a 
reallocation process, characterized by rising disruption within certain occupations rather 
than a broad-based deterioration in labor market conditions. This is consistent with the 
findings of our colleagues in Singapore too, who note a slowing in AI-exposed sectors 
there, here. 
The manufacturing boom of the 1990s provides a useful historical analogue for thinking 
about AI-driven labor market disruption. During that period, productivity growth 
accelerated sharply, manufacturing output continued to expand, and manufacturing 
employment declined substantially as firms learned to produce more with fewer 
workers. What is often forgotten is the scale of the adjustment. Manufacturing 
represented ~20% of private-sector nonfarm payroll employment at the start of the 
decade, yet rapid advances in technology and production processes allowed firms to 
increase output while reducing headcount. In effect, millions of manufacturing jobs 
became unnecessary even as the sector remained productive and competitive. The 
broader economy continued to grow, unemployment remained relatively low, and new 
opportunities emerged elsewhere. The experience suggests technological progress can 
replace labor within a sector without producing a sustained deterioration in aggregate 
labor market performance. 
We tested how task-replacement would change with model advancements since the 
2023 seminal paper by Acemoglu, here. We found in a 1,000 sample of tasks that the 
share of tasks with at least 50% of components automatable rises from 42.9% to 
52.1%, while near-complete automation rises from 1.5% to 9.6%. The main labor-
market implication of our analysis is greater change in job content, skill demand, and 
worker reallocation but not necessarily large near-term job losses. AI is more likely to 
substitute for labor where it can perform a broad set of core tasks; elsewhere, it should 
augment workers and shift human effort toward judgment, verification, and 
interpersonal work. Humulm and Vestergaard (2026) surveyed 25,000 Danish workers 
in 11 occupations highly exposed to AI and linked their answers to monthly 
administrative records through December 2024, finding null effects of AI chatbots on 
earnings and hours, both for workers who use the tools and for the workplaces that 
adopt them, ruling out average effects larger than 2% (here).
For the labor market, the implication is not necessarily that many more occupations 
become exposed, but that a larger portion of the work within affected jobs could be 
performed by AI. Jobs are bundles of tasks: deeper automation could reduce the human 
input required for particular activities while shifting remaining work toward judgment, 
verification, interpersonal interaction and responsibilities that cannot be delegated. 
Where exposure remains concentrated in a few tasks, AI may primarily augment 
workers; where it extends across a broad set of core responsibilities, substitution 
pressures could become stronger.
From a macroeconomic perspective, AI should boost structural productivity growth and 
support stronger long-run GDP growth. We continue to expect higher rates of both job 
creation and job destruction as adoption spreads across industries, producing greater 
labor market churn but also stronger productive capacity. Whether this becomes a net 
positive for workers and the broader economy will depend on the balance between 
innovation-driven job creation and technology-driven displacement.

7.
Are US corporate profits decoupling from
economic fundamentals?
US corporate profits—of all corporations, listed and unlisted—reached 14½% of GDP in 
Q2 2026, the highest share since World War II (Figure 98US corporate profits are at the highest level since 1947). That is ~1pp of GDP above 
the previous two quarters and ~2pp above the average of the preceding five years 
(~1½pp after tax). Viewed through a longer lens, the profit share has risen steadily since 
the mid-to-late '80s, doubling from about 7% of GDP to over 14%, despite real GDP 
growth slowing from over 3% in the '80s and '90s to just over 2% in the past 25 years. 
Profit shares in most other advanced economies, by contrast, have been broadly stable 
(Figure 103Changes in international profits since 20). What explains the extraordinary profitability of US firms? 
This is not simply an S&P story; we are looking at all firms in the economy, and until 
about three years ago, net income for the S&P ran at around half of economy-wide 
after-tax profits in the national accounts. The two are not strictly comparable (the 
national accounts exclude capital gains and write-downs and use economic rather than 
accounting depreciation, while S&P earnings include constituents full foreign earnings), 
but they have historically tracked each other well. However, that relationship has shifted 
sharply in the past few years, with S&P profits growing much faster (Figure 101S&P profits vs NIPA profits) and 
reaching around 90% of economy-wide profits in the most recent quarter. 
First, the recent rise in profitability is largely domestic (Figure 100Dom
estic profits vs net profits on US overseas operations)—not driven by profits 
of foreign affiliates of US firms. Net profits from the rest of the world ran at ~2½% of 
GDP before the pandemic, then fell to ~1½%, and have since recovered only a few 
tenths. Part of this reflects the dollar: the nominal effective exchange rate has shifted up 
over the past six years, reducing the dollar value of profits earned in foreign currencies. 
Second, zooming in on  the change in domestic profits since the mid-80s (Figure 99The increase in profits since the m
id-1980s is concentrated in Financials and 'Tech' (70%
 of the total) , 
roughly 70% of the increase can be attributed to Financials (33pp), Computers/
Electronics (12pp), Retail (8pp) and Information (17pp).  Information includes Alphabet, 
Meta and Microsoft; Computers and electronics includes Apple, Micron and Nvidia; and 
Retail includes Amazon. The same four industries account for 80% of the more recent 
rise in profits (Q2 vs preceding 5 years).  Excluding Retail, where profits have shrunk since 
the pandemic boom, Financials, Information and Computers and Electronics account for 
90% of the recent increase, with most of the remainder coming from Oil and Mining. 
What happened in the mid-80s to drive the sharp increase in profits? We regressed the 
corporate profit share from the national accounts on four macro-financial drivers. Lower 
real interest rates support profits by easing financial conditions and cutting the cost of 
debt service and capacity expansion. The excess bond premium (credit spreads net of 
default risk) measures risk appetite and firms' access to funding, so a rise points to 
tighter credit and weaker profits. A stronger real trade-weighted dollar hurts US 
producers' competitiveness and shrinks the dollar value of foreign earnings. Finally, the 
trade share (exports plus imports as a share of GDP) is a proxy for globalisation: more 
trade opens foreign markets and gives firms access to cheaper imported inputs and 
offshore production, supporting margins despite more foreign competition. 
Until about 2010, increased globalization explained the bulk of the improvement in 
profits (interesting in light of the increase in tariffs), whereas over the next 15 years 
lower real rates and dollar weakness provided meaningful support (Figure 102M
acro drivers of US profits). 
However, the last three years have seen a swing of about 2pp of GDP, which largely 
coincides with the surge in tech profits and cannot be explained by  macro-factors. 
The regression includes  a fifth variable called 'S-corp share', which helps account for the 
kink in the mid-80s in the profit share. The 1986 Tax Reform Act changed incentives for 
businesses (particularly small businesses and  professionals such as lawyers and doctors) 
to organise as pass-through entities (S corporations and partnerships) rather than as C 
corporations. A C-corp's profits are taxed twice—once at the company and again when 
paid out to shareholders—so owner-managers have an incentive to pay themselves 
wages, which are tax-deductible. S-corp  profits are taxed only once, at the owner's 
personal rate, and are not subject to payroll taxes, so owner-managers prefer to pay 
themselves in profits rather than wages. The rise of pass-throughs has therefore 
relabelled some labour income as profits.

8. What’s driving bond yields?
The rise in global bond yields in 2026 has been driven by an inflation shock, growth 
resilience, and lingering fiscal concerns against a backdrop of structurally higher primary 
deficits. European and UK markets have been much more exposed to the energy supply 
shock, with inflation expectations accounting for most of the move in front-end and 
intermediate nominal yields (Figure 104A rise in real policy rate expectations can explain the m
ajority of the YTD m
ove in US Treasury yields). In Treasuries, real yields have driven the move, 
as resilient growth has pushed up market estimates of r*. Meanwhile, real term 
premium estimates account for a relatively small share of the rise in Treasury and gilt 
yields, and term premium in Germany has even declined (Figure 105Real term
 prem
ium
 estim
ates acount for a relatively sm
al share of the YTD rise in DM
 governm
ent bond yields). 
Some long-run r* models, such as Holston-Laubach-Williams, remain close to pre-
pandemic levels due to slow-moving structural forces, such as productivity growth and 
demographic trends. But those long-run estimates may not capture the cyclical forces 
that determine how sensitive today’s economy is to changes in the policy rate. In 
essence, markets care more about the short-run r* (see here). In contrast to the post-
GFC period, governments have continued to spend despite higher rates, while 
household balance sheets remain supported by low leverage and strong asset values. 
The AI investment cycle may also be unusually rate-insensitive: firms are competing to 
secure scarce data-center and power capacity before physical constraints, technological 
change, competitive pressure, or the political cycle alter the opportunity set. 
At the same time, repeated supply shocks and persistent inflation have changed the 
apparent Phillips-curve trade-off. If growth remains resilient and inflation sticky, markets 
may expect that the real policy rate required to return inflation to central bank targets 
will prove higher than long-run, structural estimates of r* imply. But that process is not 
necessarily permanent. If supply shocks fade and productivity growth strengthens as we 
expect, reducing inflation without a material weakening in activity, estimates of the 
neutral rate should move lower again. In that sense, inflation dynamics are likely to 
remain central to the market’s evolving view of r*.
Meanwhile, even as the rise in term premium has stalled somewhat this year, we believe 
higher term premium is justified by structural supply/demand imbalances, higher rate 
volatility, and higher inflation volatility. While the duration supply of Treasuries has 
declined modestly this year, as Treasury has funded a greater share of issuance via T-bills, 
the acceleration of IG corporate issuance, primarily driven by AI capex financing needs, 
has driven US fixed income duration supply higher by roughly $140bn 10Y UST 
equivalents relative to the 2025 pace (Figure 106Even as the duration suply of Treasuries has slowed this year, increased IG isuance has driven duration suply to pick up). Without a commensurate increase in 
desired duration holdings, investors require a higher yield to absorb the additional rate 
risk. By our estimates, this rise in duration supply can account for roughly 20bp of the 
YTD rise in 10Y yields. Looking ahead, assuming AI capex of $1.8tn, we estimate 17% 
growth in IG corporate issuance in 2027, from $1.8tn to $2.1tn, while we could see 
some moderation in 2028. At the same time, we expect Treasury duration supply will be 
picking up over the coming years, rising from $2.6tn 10-year equivalents in 2026 to 
$2.8tn in 2027 and $3.0tn in 2028. 
The demand backdrop remains somewhat challenged. Central bank QT is ongoing for 
most central banks, except for the US. We project a total of $1.95tn of QE assets will run 
off G4 central bank balance sheets over the next two years (Figure 107QE asets continue to run of acros G4 central bank balance shets). Other structural 
buyers have also stepped away. The share of the Treasury market owned by foreign 
official investors has fallen from above 30% a decade ago to under 12%, while foreign 
private investors now own a larger share of the market (Figure 108 Foreign private investors own a growing share of the Treasury m
arket as foreign oficial investors have steped away). In this context, a 
principal risk is that rising JGB yields drive repatriation by Japanese insurers or the GPIF. 
Next year, we expect a gradual and opportunistic shift in asset managers’ portfolios 
toward domestic assets, while household NISA flows into foreign bonds gradually slow. 
On the whole, we expect this to be a gradual portfolio rebalancing story, not a 
repatriation shock (see Japan essay).
Overall, we think that market expectations of monetary policy rates will move lower in 
2027 and 2028 as inflation shocks fade. However, structural forces are likely to continue 
to support a trend higher in term premium across markets, adding marginal steepening 
pressure on global sovereign yield curves. We project 10-year Treasury yields to fall to 
4.80% by YE2027, while 10-year bund and gilt yields fall to 3.25% and 5.00%, 
respectively (Figure 109Quarterly 10y forecast ).

9. Can policy makers slow the rise of public debt?
Public debt in advanced economies is on a steady upward path (Figure 110Public debt continues to clim
b). For the 
median advanced economy, debt is projected to rise by around 2pp of GDP a year 
through 2028, with little sign of slowing thereafter. On a GDP-weighted basis, DM debt 
has risen by roughly 11pp since 2019, to  98% of GDP, but that rise would have been far 
larger without the post-pandemic inflation surge: cumulative fiscal deficits over the 
period added up to some 45pp of GDP, but higher nominal GDP absorbed much of the 
impact (Figure 116Cum
ulative increase in deficits and debt since 2019). Emerging-market debt looks somewhat better, though much 
depends on how much of China's quasi-government and local-government debt is 
counted: we forecast its central government debt at 32% of GDP this year, against an 
IMF general government estimate of 104%. 
Most of the debt was built up during the two major recessions: the global financial crisis 
and the pandemic. But there has been an element of hysteresis: deficits widened sharply 
with stimulus and weaker growth, but never retraced. Before the GFC, the average 
advanced economy ran a 2% GDP primary surplus; three years after the crisis, it was still 
running a 1.5%-of-GDP deficit. The pattern repeated after the pandemic:  a primary 
surplus of nearly 1% of GDP just before Covid is still a 1% deficit, six years on. Growth is 
not the problem: its contribution to lowering debt quickly returned to pre-crisis levels. 
The persistence lies in the primary balance, and our forecasts show little urgency in fixing 
it: the GDP-weighted advanced-economy primary balance improves by 30bp in 2027, 
merely reversing this year's deterioration, and by 10bp in 2028; EM primary balances are 
unchanged over 2026-28. So little adjustment, with global growth at trend and labour 
markets near full employment, suggests things will only get harder. 
The immediate challenge is higher borrowing costs. In recent years, cheap legacy debt 
from the  2010-20 era kept carrying costs low and inflation eroded real interest rates 
further. With market yields back at pre-GFC levels, real rates will now push debt up. For 
most governments, interest spending is already at the top of its post-2015 range, 
though often still several pp of GDP below its post-2000 peak (Figure 111Governm
ent interest expenditure (%
 GDP) . The US is the 
exception: interest spending is at a 26-year high, reflecting higher debt and a shorter 
maturity profile that passes the post-2022 rate shock through faster. In a CBO simulation 
with 10y yields stabilising just above 5%, federal interest would exceed 5% of GDP by 
the early 2030s, from roughly 3¼% today (Figure 112Net interest costs likely to be higher if yields land just above 5%).
Figure 113Sensitivity of governm
ent interest bil to a 1 percentage point increase in yields runs a similar sensitivity exercise across countries, highlighting the degree to 
which debt dynamics are hostage to bond markets. Using average maturity and the 
share of debt due within a year, we  estimate the rise in interest/GDP after one and five 
years. US debt reprices quickly: nearly a third matures within a year, largely reflecting the 
high T-bill share, vs only 13-14% for France and Italy, for instance. But average maturity 
dominates: for a similar shock, countries with long-duration debt (the UK, Switzerland, 
Belgium) are far better insulated in the near term.
AI is a further risk. If it raises labour productivity without displacing workers, potential 
growth and debt dynamics improve. If it displaces labour on a large scale, the tax base 
erodes: roughly 60% of global tax revenue comes from labour and only around 12% 
from corporate taxation (Figure 114Labour-based taxation is at risk if AI leads to labour sheding). Anthropic in its recent paper had a US 
unemployment range of 3.9% to 11.9% depending on how disruptive the technology 
proves. 
Who faces the largest adjustment to stabilize debt dynamics? Figure 115Debt dynam
ic & distance from
 debt stabilizing prim
ary decomposes 
debt dynamics for 35 advanced and emerging economies. Brazil remains furthest from 
its debt-stabilising primary balance (around 5pp of GDP), though the Brazil section 
shows how scenario-dependent this is; meaningful consolidation could substantially 
lower the risk premium in its real interest rate, the highest in our sample. China ranks 
second on a general government basis. The US, Germany and France are each about 
3pp of GDP from stabilisation (4pp for the US including rising interest costs), though 
note that these calculations are mechanical and exclude structural pressures from 
ageing and pensions. In the US, the Social Security Trust Fund's projected exhaustion in 
2032 could require a further 1.5pp of GDP if funded from the federal budget. At the 
other end, Greece, Singapore and Portugal have the best dynamics, with Japan close 
behind, helped by a very low net interest rate given its large holdings of interest-earning 
assets.).

o the narrative
 of this report for an explanation of this image/char t
Public debt in advanced economies is 
increasing at an average pace of 2pp 
of GDP per year.
The main problem is that primary 
balances that widened during 
recessions are slow to retrace, and 
we forecast little adjustment out to

10. How tight are global energy markets?
Global energy markets  are very tight  as oil and gas exports from the Middle East remain 
disrupted, but this tightness could ease pretty quickly, should we see a conflict resolution 
and full recovery of flows. These flows have already improved materially over the past six 
months, at least for crude oil: per our tracker, >70% of the >20Mb/d of oil and products 
flowing via the Strait of Hormuz pre-conflict are now reaching global markets, i.e. we 
may be missing >6Mb/d or >6% of global supply. We estimate that this still leaves the 
global oil market in a deficit of close to 2Mb/d or 2%, thanks to lower demand and 
supply response elsewhere. The improvements for LNG flows have been much more 
modest, at only ~20% of pre-conflict levels.
The oil market has displayed remarkable adaptability this year, considering that it has 
experienced the largest supply disruption in its history based on cumulative lost bbls. The 
oil price did not rally as high and as quickly as we initially expected, on a combination of: 
1) quick demand response, 2) China reducing imports, 3) greater resilience of Middle
East exports, and 4) solid buffer of existing inventories, including strategic reserves. The
inventory buffers are thinner now, however: we estimate global oil inventories have
fallen by ~8% as of end October and are below the lower end of the five-year
range. There is still some buffer but any increased disruption to flows would likely be
met by a sharper price reaction. A resolution would not necessarily mean an immediate
return to pre-conflict prices, as we will need to rebuild inventories. For oil, this implies an
extra 2Mb/d over two years, on top of trend demand growth of 1Mb/d pa.
Most of the improvement in flows has been in the form of crude oil, leaving the refining 
market very tight. Oil products exports from Russia have been heavily disrupted too. We 
estimate that global refining outages were still at ~11Mb/d in October, vs. a normal level 
closer to 5Mb/d. Reduced output from the Middle East is close to 3Mb/d and Russian 
output is down by close to 1.5Mb/d, albeit improving. Diesel/jet fuel have been most 
impacted and the spread widened to $100/bbl at times, on top of Brent. As more 
capacity returns, and some new capacity starts up, refining margins should come down, 
but it should take longer to normalise, possibly until late 2027 given greater damage to 
assets and reduced inventories. We model our benchmark margin falling back to $10/
bbl, from $26/bbl in 2026, still above normalised levels of $5/bbl.
 For natural gas, there is a double uncertainty: Middle East disruptions and the weather. 
European gas storage is ~75% full at the start of the withdrawal season, their lowest in 
>15 years. Around 50% of storage capacity is used on average during winter. A cold
winter can pull an extra 10% and aggregate storage level rarely ever goes below 20%,
leaving a very limited buffer. At least, the super El Niño means a milder winter is more
likely in Europe. There is more LNG capacity coming but not until the later part of
2027 and 2028. The global gas market should remain tight over the next year, before
turning  over-supplied in the late 2020s.
Middle East producers are actively looking into new pipelines to increase their ability to 
bypass the Strait of Hormuz. Except for the UAE, these projects are at an early stage and 
they should not impact the market in 2027. This also does not apply to LNG. 
 The Middle East conflict has shown that China can be a major swing consumer in the oil 
market, influencing the price to an extent usually reserved to OPEC+.  China cut its oil 
imports by as much as 5Mb/d at the trough in June. These are now "only" 2Mb/d below 
normal. This has been driven by a combination of lower demand and inventory 
draws. The country is still sitting on very large visible inventories of nearly 1.2bn bbls. It 
could in theory continue at reduced import levels for an extended period of time. 
 Our base case is for Brent to average $80/bbl in 2027, assuming a gradual normalisation 
of  flows through 1H27 on the basis of a similar arrangement to the June MoU by the end 
of the year. We do not model a drop to $70/bbl, as in June, because of the reduced 
buffers, need to rebuild inventories and likely lingering risk premium. In an upside 
scenario, new attacks and disruptions to flows could send prices to $120+/bbl. 
Conversely, the main downside is a quick return to the MoU and greater stability around 
Middle East flows, including higher Iranian exports. This could take prices below $80/bbl 
more quickly than we model. For gas, we have a base case of €45/MWh TTF average 
next year, €55/MWh in the upside scenario and €35/MWh in the downside scenario.

11. Inflation in an era of supply shocks, ageing and
super El-Niño; what is the new normal?
The jump in crude oil prices as a result of the Middle East conflict marked another in the 
growing list  of supply shocks in recent years that includes disruptions from the 
pandemic, the Russia-Ukraine war,  and tariff increases, among other shocks. These 
continued shocks limit the ability of businesses to rely on a smooth pattern of trade and 
lead to the question whether  an era of supply shocks is the new normal. 
Indeed, relying on a consistent pattern in the international transactions has become 
increasingly difficult. The index of economic policy uncertainty has surged upwards in 
recent years (the red line in Figure 123Econom
ic policy uncertainty has risen sharply in recent years. Geopolitical risk is also up, but only about to its level in the early 20s). Geopolitical risk (the black line) has also risen 
sharply since hitting a low five years ago. However, it is worth remembering that earlier 
periods also saw notable  risks and uncertainty:  the “Global War on Terror” in the early 
2000s, the Iraq War in the early 1990s, along with various other conflicts over the years. 
In some ways the long period without a spike in geopolitical risk from the early 2000s to 
the pandemic was the outlier, not the more recent period. 
Supply shocks are not only man-made. This year, a super El Niño has appeared that could 
change weather patterns across the globe.  More broadly, the number of reported large 
natural disasters roughly doubled between the late-1980s and the early-2020s (Figure 
124The num
ber of large reported natural disasters has trended up, but the destruction from
 natural disasters has sen litle increase). That said,  the increase could simply be a result of improved reporting, though the 
number of major Atlantic hurricanes (not shown), which is likely less subject to reporting 
bias, has also trended up. Despite the increase, the cost of those disasters (in terms of 
GDP) has moved little over the past 25 years, perhaps because one strength of a market 
economy is its ability to adapt over time. 
Other possible supply shocks on the horizon: population aging and productivity growth. 
As noted in our recent Q-Series,  there is a general concern that an aging population 
could lead to shortages in labor and an increase in inflation (particularly in Europe) as the 
dependency ratio (the share of non-working-age population to working-age 
population) swings from falling to rising.  Overall, however, the impact of ageing on 
inflation is ambiguous, perhaps because an aging population generally coincides with 
lower overall population growth, which means less demand growth for new housing 
and other durable items. In contrast, higher productivity growth, which many expect 
from the elevated level of investment in AI and technology, is generally correlated with 
lower inflation, though (as we noted in our May compendium) it could take a handful of 
years before the downward impact on inflation appears.  
What does this all mean in terms of inflation? Where is the new normal? Generally, 
supply shocks have had their biggest impact on the parts of inflation that are the least 
persistent, such as food and energy. On this front, the recent swings in commodity prices 
do not look unusual compared to many other swings since the 1970s (Figure 125If suply shocks are contained to the norm
al, volatile com
ponents, they wil likely have litle persistence: Recent swings in prices for crude oil and fod-related com
odities do not sem
 unusual). 
However, if supply shocks become a more common occurrence outside of these volatile 
commodities, then firms will  likely adapt by re-optimizing and diversifying supply and 
production chains, perhaps by having multiple suppliers and customers spread across 
multiple geographies. But adaption can take years and appear as higher inflation in the 
meantime. Further, those  adaptations have a price: less efficient production, lower 
aggregate output, higher aggregate costs, and a poorer economy. 
At the macro level, more frequent or broader  supply shocks  mean  central banks have 
less control over year-to-year swings in inflation. Future inflation volatility might be more 
similar to the 1980s than the 2010s. (Figure 126The volatility of 12-m
onth US CPI inflation has surged in recent years due to the pandem
ic. Greater suply shocks could kep it from
 returning to the lows of the couple of decades prior to the pandem
ic)But more volatile inflation does not 
automatically mean  higher average inflation. That will  depend on the willingness of 
monetary authorities to accommodate the supply shocks. Accommodative monetary 
policy, in our view, has not been the proximate cause of inflation surges since the start of 
the pandemic, but has likely been a factor in why inflation has not fallen more quickly 
from its 2022 peak (and also why a recession has been avoided).  Indeed,  inflation over 
the past 10 years has been higher  in countries where the monetary base has been 
expanded more (Figure 127Price increases over the past ten years have ben higher in countries where the central bank expanded the m
onetary base by m
ore), even though in more normal times, when inflation is low,  
inflation is little correlated with money supply growth.  Similarly, monetary policy is not to 
blame for the current elevated global inflation:  before last year’s jump in crude oil prices, 
global headline inflation had returned to its level in 2018-19. (Figure 128By early this year, headline CPI inflation had returned to the range it had ben in the decade prior to the pandem
ic. Then crude oil prices jum
ped)

12. Is low volatility a bug or a feature, and what
does that mean for markets?
Equities: Low correlations now a structural feature of the market, as long as 
Tech+/AI remains at the helm: The key driver of  lower equity volatility is how 
concentrated the market has become in Tech+/AI, both in terms of index weight and 
earnings contribution. On a cap-weighted basis, the S&P 500 has become much less 
sensitive to macro drivers, such as higher yields or oil price spikes (here). Index volatility 
depends on two aspects: how volatile the individual stocks are, and how closely they 
move together – the former has structurally moved higher in the post-Covid era, while 
the latter has moved structurally lower. Intra-index correlations sit near historical lows as 
dispersion remains elevated, with significant rotation being driven amongst AI sub-
themes and rate-sensitive beneficiaries vs. laggards, amongst other pockets. 
Record concentration is a double-edged sword as strong earnings growth in the most 
concentrated parts of the market continues to justify underlying Tech+ performance vs. 
rest of index  (here). That does not necessarily leave the market less exposed, however. 
Equities have become persistently more fragile, having become more prone to frequent 
(yet fleeting) bouts of volatility – they are just more difficult to trigger, barring an 
exogenous shock  (here, here).
Aspects exacerbating this dynamic include: 1) growth in leveraged ETFs and 
momentum-based strategies (e.g. tax-advantaged funds), 2) growth in systematic index 
gamma supply from short-dated option-writing strategies, 3) growth in systematic index 
vega supply from defined outcome ETFs, and 4) sporadic upside chasing from the retail 
cohort (here, here, here, here).  
There are two ways in which we foresee this dynamic breaking: 1) increased 
participation in other cyclical ex-Tech pockets of the market, which could see breadth 
improving, reflected in part by correlations rising from unsustainably suppressed levels as 
equities rise (a healthy, positive outcome; here), or 2) the AI trade faltering, driving a 
correlated selloff in the heaviest-weighted parts of the index (a negative outcome).
Rates/FX: The bug is already being fixed: Rates volatility has historically exceeded 
equity volatility during tightening cycles. Rate volatility has started to reprice higher, as 
we anticipated ahead of Fed Chair Warsh’s arrival this past summer (here, here). A Fed 
framework that provides less guidance to the market should naturally lead to higher 
volatility around US data releases and FOMC meetings, although it remains to be seen to 
what extent the Fed is prepared to ‘surprise’ markets. At the same time, growing fiscal 
concerns across major economies, most notably France, alongside several important 
elections next year, should help sustain the rise in bond volatility next year.
FX volatility, too, has started to rise moderately, although the rise is not uniform 
across currencies, and in totality continues to lag that of rates. While higher rates 
volatility in aggregate is helpful for FX volatility, the more important driver of FX volatility 
is  divergences within the rates complex. For much of 2026, we have seen a synchronized 
hiking cycle across  central banks as they responded in unison to the oil price shock, 
keeping yield differentials relatively stable. However, as policy  is becoming increasingly 
restrictive, expectations are starting to diverge over how well different economies can 
sustain higher rates, reflecting differences in fiscal risks and interest-rate sensitivities. 
Figure 134High FX volatility typicaly coincides with high equity and bond volatility  shows that pairwise correlations across G10 2y swaps have started to decline 
from extreme highs, which should support FX volatility.
That said, several factors are working in the opposite direction: First, CNY 
volatility remains very low with 12m realised USDCNH volatility at 2.4%, a low not seen 
since CNY’s devaluation in August 2015. Second, FX interventions in Japan caused initial 
spikes in volatility, but is ultimately resulting in a lower level of implied and realized 
volatility in USDJPY as it deters speculators and limits potential ranges to the upside. 
Third, without higher equity volatility,  there is a natural brake on how far FX volatility can 
rise. We have also observed greater nuance in the USD’s relationship with equities, as 
Tech-led equity declines have led to USD weakness, contrary to the typically negative 
USD-equity correlation. As a result, we favour a selective approach to FX volatility, 
focusing on markets where we see event risk or have a differentiated central bank view.

13. Does fixed income have a role in portfolios?
Over the last five years, the Bloomberg Global Agg bond index has returned -2.6% 
annualised, compared with 12% for MSCI ACWI in equities. According to AAII, 
investors’ equity allocations are north of 70%, while government bonds account for 
16%, leaving the widest gap in 25 years (Figure 135US Investor alocations acros Equity, Fixed Incom
e and Cash ). In addition, bond-equity 
correlation has remained positive through most of the past five years. Poor returns and 
diversification have pushed bond valuations relative to equities to nearly a 100-year low 
(Figure 136Relative valuation of Bonds to Equities in the US). Is there a case for fixed income in portfolios? We think there is.
We believe the market is pricing central banks’ terminal rates too high. Recognition of 
this should mean lower yields across the curve. Today’s narrative is dominated by a 
global capex boom and the associated belief in stronger productivity, a key reason 
markets think r* has risen and will stay high. UBS’ tech analysts are well ahead of 
consensus on hyperscaler capex estimates for 2027. Beyond 2027, however, hyperscaler 
growth is likely to slow. Meanwhile, outside sectors immediately adjacent to AI, such as 
industrials, utilities and materials, we see little evidence of a large rise in capex. 
Broadening of the capex boom remains limited. 
Nor do we detect a trend rise in productivity anywhere except Korea and Taiwan. In the 
US, lower labour-force growth may be reducing potential output by more than 
productivity is raising it. AI could change this, but it may not. The market makes 
insufficient allowance for that uncertainty – wrongly, in our view. Del Negro, Elbarmi and 
Pham show that neutral rates across economies have increasingly converged since the 
1980s (Figure 137M
edian r*estim
ates by country). Although the world is less globalised than immediately before the 
GFC, most protectionist measures have targeted goods rather than capital flows. Low FX 
volatility also suggests that rates across countries still move broadly together. With 
demographic pressures and trend growth weaker in China and Europe than in the US, 
the global saving-investment balance should continue to exert downward pressure on 
neutral rates, including in the US. The authors also conclude that the real neutral rate 
has risen since Covid, but remains well below early-2000s levels. 
While front-end and belly yields should fall, gains at the back end are likely to be more 
limited. We see a high likelihood of rising term premia across many markets, not least 
the US, the largest fixed market, which we  focus our comments on. Term premia rise will 
not be caused by competition for capital crowding out borrowers; we view that as 
cyclical rather than structural. Instead, it should be driven by a structural change in the 
supply-demand balance for US debt. Consider the mid-2000s ‘savings glut’. Alan 
Greenspan raised policy rates from 1% to 5% between 2004 and 2006, yet the 10-year 
yield remained around 4.5%, a conundrum for the Fed chair. Ben Bernanke argued that 
global investors had the willingness and the ability to channel their savings into US long-
end debt. This occurred  amid limited Treasury supply. The US fiscal deficit averaged just 
0.8% of GDP from 1996 to 2006, with public debt at only 35.5% by the end of the 
period. Twenty years later, the picture is almost reversed. With deficits around 6% of 
GDP, interest costs of 3.3% and public debt of 101%, long-term Treasury supply should 
rise materially from around mid-2027 ((Figure 138US fixed incom
e duration suply).  Meanwhile, foreign official holdings 
as a share of outstanding US debt have steadily declined.
But how quickly will term premia rise? Slowly, we think. As the world’s most liquid bond 
market, with extensive use as repo collateral and significant regulatory value, US 
Treasuries command a high convenience yield. Even as Treasuries have failed to diversify 
portfolios amid positive bond-equity correlation, they have not cheapened materially 
against swaps. Our analysis shows inflation is a key driver of bond-equity correlation. 
Below roughly 2.5% US core PCE inflation, that correlation has historically been 
negative (Figure 139Bond-Equity corelation in diferent states of US Inflation ).  We expect inflation to fall towards this range by H2 2027, limiting 
the rise in term premia despite higher net supply. At the back end, we expect the decline 
in short rates to more than offset a modest rise in term premia. We modelled equity and 
fixed-income allocations using our return forecasts, assuming modestly higher equity 
volatility, steady bond volatility and a return to modestly negative bond-equity 
correlation by H2 2027. An average of active risk-parity and maximum risk-adjusted-
return approaches recommends reducing equity exposure from just over 85% in 2026 
to 70% in 2027 and just under 50% in 2028, while increasing bond exposure (Figure 
140Portfolio alocation recom
ended and average of Active Risk Parity and M
ean Variance Optim
isation aproaches ). Fixed-income gains may be concentrated at the front end and belly, but we see a 
strong role for bonds in portfolios from here.

14. Will carry do well in 2027?
A carry strategy does well if nothing happens, and better if the underlying securities 
appreciate. With the exception of duration, 2026 extended the strong carry returns seen 
across asset classes since 2022. Under what conditions does carry thrive, and will these 
persist in 2027? 
We constructed an FX carry index, going long the three highest-yielding currencies and 
short the three lowest-yielding currencies in equally weighted baskets (Figure 141Historical perform
ance of a representative FX cary strategy). It 
delivered an annual return of 5% and a Sharpe ratio of 0.7, with drawdowns in only 6 of 
27 years. Performance was strongest in 2002–07 and 2022–26, when spot returns 
reinforced carry. We tested performance against US rates and inflation, global volatility 
and credit funding, and US equity performance both outright and relative to the rest of 
the world (Figure 142The strategy has historicaly done best when US rates are high, volatility is low and global equities outperform
 the US). 
 Carry has tended to perform well when US front-end rates are high. Both 2022 and 
2026 were strong years despite real rates rising 425bp and 200bp, respectively, 
reflecting more aggressive tightening elsewhere in 2022 and strong growth in 2026. 
Instead, carry is most negatively related to cross-asset volatility and credit-funding stress. 
It has also fared better in high-inflation environments. 
 Carry does best when equities are strong, particularly when global equities match or 
outperform the US. In 2002–08, this was associated with dollar weakness, boosting 
both sides of the FX carry trade but especially high-yielders. By contrast, modest US 
equity outperformance in 2022–26 left the dollar broadly flat. Carry instead benefited 
from depreciation in short-book currencies, particularly the JPY (Figure 143Roling 12m
 contribution of long and short legs to net cary returns ) (Figure 144Net 
cary returns: top contributing curencies by long and short leg ). 
 In ’27 we expect reasonable equity returns, with global markets keeping pace with the 
US. Growth in most major economies should be around trend, while inflation declines 
but remains above trend. This should favour carry. However, we see its appeal as 
diminished versus the past five years, both because risks are skewed towards a worse 
growth-inflation mix (Iran conflict) and because the two variables most closely related to 
carry, cross-asset volatility and easy credit funding, appear exhausted. 
 Our EM carry attractiveness framework – based on fundamentals, positioning and carry 
– also suggests caution is warranted from a bottom-up perspective. Conditions for
EM carry trades were fertile post Q1-22 – when EM central banks raised rates
aggressively to build high nominal and real carry buffers over DM, and foreign flows
capitulated – but have been below average over the last 12m, on aggregate (Figure 145M
edian 
Cary Score acros EM
 ). With limited signs of imminent improvement and the dollar likely flat to stronger, we
forecast no meaningful appreciation in high-yielding carry currencies ahead.
 Low-yielding currencies are also unlikely to repeat their contribution to carry in 2027. 
Continued BoJ normalisation should narrow US-Japan real-rate differentials and stabilise 
the JPY (Figure 146USDJPY and US-JP avg real interest rate diferential). Japanese inflation should also limit tolerance for further JPY 
weakness, while Swiss inflation gives the SNB less reason to resist CHF depreciation. We 
therefore see greater downside risk for the CHF, making it a better carry funder, though 
significant depreciation looks unlikely in either currency. 
 Without a major dollar trend, we would only own selective EM currencies with 
improving terms of trade and elevated carry, such as BRL and COP, funded in EUR. 
Overall, FX carry prospects look weak. Low equity correlation and volatility also make 
selling volatility unattractive, although semiconductor stocks and indices could be 
exceptions given high volatility and prospects for greater capital returns. Credit has 
begun to widen, and further widening could create better carry opportunities in 2027. 
Around and after the French elections, European bank and high-yield credit could 
become attractive. But we are not there yet.

15.
Gold and stablecoins: threats to dollar
dominance?
The de-dollarization narrative sparked by Liberation Day in April 2025 finally subsided in 
2026, with the Fed’s 25bp hike under Trump-appointed Chair Warsh on 16 September 
providing the final rebuttal to widespread Fed independence concerns. During this 
phase of the “USD debasement” narrative, gold’s rising share of global FX reserves and 
the growing adoption of an expanding stablecoin universe were frequently cited as 
tangible signs of the USD’s declining utility. The narrative’s demise offers an opportunity 
to review these developments in a new light: our takeaway is that the threat to the 
USD’s status from either gold or stablecoins is still minimal.
Starting with precious metals, gold is becoming a more active reserve asset among 
selected EM central banks. Turkey uses it within its liquidity-management framework 
(link, link, part 2.2). Ghana’s purchases of domestic production can inject liquidity while 
supporting local mining, turning reserve accumulation into a broader macroeconomic 
tool (link). And in China, gold trading against the renminbi is seen as supporting the 
currency’s gradual internationalisation; the Shanghai Gold Exchange’s International 
Board offers trading against CNH, while Hong Kong has announced plans for gold-
renminbi contracts (link). 
For developed-market central banks and EM central banks with passive gold holdings, 
the price rally has mechanically increased gold’s share of reserves. A recent paper by the 
IMF suggests considering gold under an investment framework (link). In practice, we 
think this could involve dividing holdings between a core “security” tranche, retained 
for resilience under stress, and a smaller actively managed portion. The latter could 
respond to elevated price volatility and potentially earn incremental yield. Broader 
institutional participation and new trading centres could deepen liquidity, making such 
engagement more practical. As gold becomes integrated into liquidity frameworks, the 
official sector and institutional investors could increasingly treat it as an alternative 
currency. Yet actionable plans to implement the above are still lacking. As such, 
prospects of gold substituting for the USD beyond specific and limited EM use cases 
remain embryonic, rather than an imminent concern. 
As for stablecoins, adoption has expanded rapidly but  payment use remains  limited. The 
IMF reports issuance doubled since 2024 to approximately $300bn in Sep 2025 (link, pp. 
14-15), while the BIS puts capitalization at $320bn by end-May 2026, still modest
against US bank deposits of ~$19tn (link). Crypto trading remains the dominant use
case. In the BIS’s 2025 Ethereum sample, ~60% of transfer events occurred within
complex transactions combining financial operations rather than standalone transfers
(link). Adoption beyond crypto is rising, with activity highest in Asia-Pacific in absolute
terms according to the IMF (link), but mass-market use remains very limited. The BIS
finds transfers below $250 represent only 0.4–0.9% of adjusted transaction value  (link).
Nor is the trend improving: UBS estimates economic payments at ~4% of raw stablecoin
transaction volume in 2026 YTD, flat vs 2025 and below ~5% in 2024 and ~10% in
2023 (link). Payment adoption by AI agents could potentially change this, and remains a
development worth monitoring.
Other factors underpin our scepticism about the appeal of gold or stablecoins as USD 
substitutes. 1) Gold diversification has not come uniquely at the dollar’s expense but 
reflects broader concerns about fiat currencies. IMF COFER data show JPY, CNY and GBP 
have also lost FX reserves share since 2022 alongside the USD. Alternatives face similar 
debt-sustainability issues (EUR, JPY, GBP), and/or capital controls (CNY). 2) Higher real 
interest rates increase the opportunity cost of holding  gold. 3) Asian surpluses continue 
flowing into dollar assets - increasingly equities. Focusing only on FX reserves misses 
growing holdings by state banks, pension funds and quasi-sovereign investors (link). 4) 
The 2026 Jackson Hole Symposium highlighted constraints on stablecoins replacing 
established monetary infrastructure. Schnabel emphasizes central-bank money’s 
settlement role; de Cos identifies stablecoins’ shortcomings relative to tokenized 
deposits; and Duffie questions their suitability for systemic wholesale settlement. Lastly, 
5) greater stablecoin payment use could even reinforce dollar dominance. Most are
dollar-pegged (99.5% according to UBS research), extending access to dollar claims. As
Eswar Prasad argues, lower cross-border frictions can interact with deeper dollar
markets to strengthen the USD’s advantages rather than displace them.

16. Do we need to worry about private credit and
private equity?
Higher Rates Meet Refinancing Risk: Private credit enters 2027 with potential stress 
deferred, not defused, in our view. We think US private credit default rates could rise to 
300bp to 7–8% by end-2027 as refinancing risk meets a higher-for-longer rate 
environment. We estimate that about 21% of US Private Credit debt matures over the 
next two years, concentrated in weaker 2020–21 vintages and software. The rate relief 
that improved interest coverage in 2024-25 is reversing as rates start moving higher, 
leaving weaker borrowers facing a combination of higher refinancing spreads, tighter 
terms and, increasingly, restructuring. But refinancing is only half the story: with exits still 
weak and net redemptions elevated, the second test will be whether existing liquidity 
can continue to convert illiquid assets into realized distributions. 
Software Disruption Is Slower Than Feared, Not Priced Out: Notwithstanding the 
immediate reaction post the US midterms, AI disruption is slower than feared, although 
credit risks remain. Enterprises are optimising model costs and matching models to 
specific tasks rather than replacing incumbent software. Only ~12% of BDC technology 
exposure appears at high risk, versus ~45% at medium risk, making gradual pressure on 
pricing, seat growth and retention more likely than widespread obsolescence. For 
leveraged software borrowers, even modest revenue pressure can produce 
disproportionate EBITDA declines through negative operating leverage. With 
technology representing roughly 25% of US and European private credit portfolios, the 
aggregate risk could be material: a sustained 10% annual EBITDA decline could push 
median technology interest coverage below 1.0x by late 2028, creating refinancing 
challenges as maturities approach. 
PE Exits Are Reopening Selectively, But Organic Liquidity Is Scarce: The reopening 
in private equity exits remains selective. 3Q exit value improved, led by strategic buyers, 
but deal counts barely rose and YTD activity remains below 2025. Large, high-quality 
assets are clearing, while the middle market, where private credit exposure is 
concentrated, remains constrained. This is expanding and ageing the backlog of PE-
backed companies, suppressing distributions and extending holding periods. 
Meanwhile, private-market inflows are weakening: the 5 largest non-traded BDCs in our 
sample recorded negative net flows in all three quarters of 2026 as elevated redemption 
requests coincided with inflows falling nearly 85% vs Q4 '25 levels. Weak exits and 
softer inflows are reinforcing liquidity pressures by limiting distributions and new capital. 
Broader retail and retirement access could help in 2027, but gradual adoption leaves 
near-term liquidity dependent on market performance and investor sentiment. 
Distributions Are Increasingly Financed, Not Realized: With realizations still 
constrained, private-market managers are making greater use of dividend 
recapitalizations, NAV facilities, continuation vehicles and other structured solutions to 
provide liquidity. These tools can bridge an extended exit cycle, but their economic 
effects differ: some facilitate asset sales, while others add leverage, subordination or 
duration. We estimate that financing layered across private-credit and private-capital 
structures totals roughly $1.5tn, within a range of approximately $1.25tn to $1.75tn. 
Undrawn revolvers and delayed-draw loans, equal to about 14% of BDC portfolio value 
and six times cash on hand, create an additional liquidity demand as borrowers 
approach maturity. The key question for 2027 is therefore not only whether distributions 
recover, but whether they reflect genuine realisations or potentially increase financed 
and repackaged risk.
Dispersion Rewards Selectivity, But Headline Risk May Return: We see 2027 
producing dispersion rather than broad systemic stress, with credit deterioration 
concentrated in older vintages, weaker capital structures and software. The key risk is 
transmission through private credit’s funding channels: banks provide roughly 45% of 
US private credit fund and BDC liabilities, while insurers increasingly supply capital 
through rated structures. Defaults, sustained redemptions or downgrades could 
potentially turn asset-level stress into broader balance-sheet pressure. Our base case 
remains dispersion, not contagion, but growing reliance on leverage and financial 
engineering has created more transmission channels. The central risk is that these 
mechanisms could feasibly create some stress in an inherently illiquid asset class when 
liquidity is needed most.

17. How strong is the case for Chinese equities?
What has been driving the market in 2026? 
Since the start of the year, investors have navigated a series of gradual policy-tightening 
developments. These included antitrust investigations into selected internet platforms in 
January (Trip.com), ETF selling by state-owned funds early in the year to temper a rapid 
equity rally, tighter oversight of cross-border securities activities beginning in May, 
stricter regulation on offshore trust taxation, as well as reductions in export VAT rebates 
across several industries such as autos and solar. At the same time, policymakers largely 
refrained from delivering broad-based stimulus despite weakness in consumption and a 
challenging macro backdrop. From a geopolitics front, Chinese exporters are also facing 
tariff concerns as they are gaining overseas market share from foreign players. On the 
positive side, AI emerged as the most important thematic driver of market performance. 
Chinese AI-related stocks significantly outperformed during 2Q26 as domestic investors 
participated in the global AI rally and priced in the potential benefits through domestic 
AI names. However, enthusiasm moderated in subsequent months due to concerns 
around US restrictions and domestic component shortages.
Where are we now post the recent sell-off? 
With the recent tech-led sell-off, MSCI China 12-month fwd PE valuation has reached 
9.5x, 1.2 std. dev. below the historical average and at the 8th percentile vs history. Major 
indices in China are now down 15-20% YTD and the tech-heavy indices such as the 
STAR50 are down YTD despite seeing more than 200% YoY earnings growth. Our 
tracking of fast money position data such as crowding scores and margin financing 
suggests net positions are back to beginning-of-the-year levels while HK equities' short 
interest is also the highest it has been. Meanwhile, earnings growth for AI tech hardware 
remains robust, with earnings upgrades of 12-15% in the last three months across most 
names. Meanwhile, valuations for the tech AI hardware names are now close to the 
historical trough and imply a major deceleration in EPS growth ahead. We believe the 
fundamental concerns for AI are overdone and historically when valuations reached 
such levels, these names were on average up >50% over the following 12 months. We 
are wary that investor sentiment on AI is fragile; however, we believe the risk/reward is 
looking more attractive again and interest may come back towards the end of the year 
as investors rebuild their portfolios for the new year.
What would turn investors more constructive on China?

More forceful policy support: Policy remains one of the most important drivers 
of Chinese equity performance. Measures to help with local government 
financing, boost household income and improve social welfare are likely to be 
viewed positively.

Further property market stabilization: We have seen signs of property price 
stabilization as the recent cancellation of the pre-sale model reduced property 
supply.

HBM breakthrough: One of the key bottlenecks in Chins’s AI supply chain 
remains in HBM; to the extent we see some meaningful breakthrough in the 
production of HBM, this could lift investor confidence in China’s domestic AI 
supply chain.

Further SOE funds’ purchase in the A share market: Purchases by state-
owned funds have historically acted as an important source of downside 
protection for Chinese equities. Beyond the direct flow impact, such purchases 
often serve as a strong policy signal. There seems to have been some purchases by 
SOE funds in recent months, and further purchases would boost sentiment and 
liquidity.

Earnings revision: We see some tailwinds for earnings for 2027, including a 
lessening impact from RMB appreciation and lower losses from food delivery 
competition. That said, component shortages and further tax rebate cuts could 
offset these benefits.

r to the 
narrative of this report for an explanation of this image/chart

the new year.

Global Economics & Markets Outlook 9 November 2026
ab 60
Global Economics & Markets Outlook
UBS Research
18. Will Europe’s resilience last?
Eurozone growth has proved surprisingly resilient this year in the face of the Middle East 
conflict and higher energy prices. We attribute this to several factors. First, higher EU 
defence spending and German infrastructure investment will have provided some 
stability. Second, Eurozone labour markets have remained resilient, with unemployment 
– at 6.5% – only modestly above its all-time low. This will have supported household
consumption, which accounts for 55% of Eurozone GDP. Third, partly as a result of the
2022/23 energy crisis, the Eurozone has continued to reduce its energy intensity by
improving energy efficiency and phasing out energy-intensive production; it has also
diversified its energy supply geographically. A unit of Eurozone GDP now requires only
half as much oil as in 1990. Fourth, AI-related investment and exports also seem to have
helped, although their impact is difficult to quantify. In addition, several transitory
factors appear to have provided support, including temporary tax cuts on fuel and
energy; front-loading of manufacturing production amid concerns about supply-chain
disruptions; and a shift in global demand for energy-intensive industrial production from
Asia to Europe, as reduced Gulf energy supplies affected Asian production more
severely.  Finally, equilibrium interest rates (r*) might have risen somewhat – potentially
reflecting fiscal stimulus and AI-related effects – implying that  the ECB's monetary
tightening may have restrained activity less than in previous cycles.
 So, will Europe’s resilience last? Our baseline says yes. We forecast Eurozone GDP 
growth to pick up a bit from 1.1% in 2026 to 1.2% in 2027 – around “trend”. The fiscal 
impulse should remain positive, adding 30bp to growth, as in 2026. Stable labour 
markets should support consumption, while capex should recover after slower 
investment growth in 2026. AI-related activity  should remain robust. Lower energy 
prices should also support confidence and activity. UBS oil and gas analysts expect Brent 
to average US$80/bbl in 2027 (down 12.6% from 2026) and TTF gas to average €45.3/
MWh (down 19.4%). An improving external environment should support net exports.
 However, these forecasts are not unconditional. Should geopolitical tensions intensify 
and energy prices rise again, we would see downside risks to our GDP forecasts. Growth 
might decelerate, although we see the hurdle to recession as high given fiscal support 
and resilient labour markets. Higher energy prices and global uncertainty would likely 
weigh on household consumption, capex and exports. They could also push inflation 
higher and force the ECB to raise rates by more than assumed in our baseline, creating 
further headwinds to growth.
Equity markets show the same resilience. Sector-weighted PMI new orders have moved 
firmly into expansion. This suggests that demand held back by the 2025 tariff 
uncertainty, and growing strongly from AI industrial demand, is now turning into new 
orders across industrials, technology and financials. Earnings estimates are following, 
with three-month EPS revisions led higher by the same sectors. Consensus now expects 
close to 20% Stoxx 600 EPS growth in 2026 and a further 10% next year. Two drivers 
stand out. First, German spending is clearly accelerating. Second, the AI cycle is moving 
on from supplying data centres with products to building the new factories that make 
more of those products. Semiconductor equipment capex, for example, is forecast to 
grow 36% in 2026 and 43% in 2027.
Europe's industrial capacity enablers make up a large share of the index. Financials, 
industrials and tech hardware/equipment account for around 51%. Adding energy and 
utilities, which gain from higher energy prices, takes the share with structural support, or 
that benefits outright, to more than 60%. If higher energy prices do weigh on growth or 
damage consumer demand too much, a scenario we model with our economists, the 
headwind could cancel out this momentum. So far, though, current energy prices have 
not stopped growth and profitability in the equity market from accelerating.

19. Has Japan beaten deflation, and what does it
mean for global assets?
We think Japan has beaten deflation. What began as an external inflation shock from 
higher energy prices, and a weaker JPY in 2022, has evolved into something more 
durable. Core-core CPI has averaged 2.9% in the past four years, average core wage 
growth has accelerated from 0.5%y/y in 2015-20 to 2.5%y/y in '25-26, while services 
inflation is becoming more persistent. Meanwhile, long-term inflation expectations, 
which had fallen close to zero during the deflationary era, have risen toward 2% across a 
broad range of economic agents, from households to businesses, and are becoming 
increasingly anchored around that level
BoJ increasingly sees its 2% inflation objective as achieved. Policy is moving from 
generating inflation toward normalization. With core-core CPI inflation (excluding the 
food tax cut) expected to average 2.9% in 2027 (UBSF), we expect the BoJ to raise the 
policy rate to 2% by 3Q27. Continued QT would reduce the BoJ’s balance sheet from 
91% of GDP currently to 78% by end-27 and 54% by '30 (UBSF). That would increase 
10-year equivalent duration supply to the private sector by 12%y/y in 2027 (CY); slower
than in '25 (21%y/y) and '26 (21%y/y), but faster than peers (e.g. 5%y/y in US). 10y JGB
yields rise to 3.2% in '27E (from 2.8% currently), albeit much more slowly than over the
past three years. We expect real yields to drive most of the move, with the curve
flattening as policy approaches neutral. More expansionary fiscal policy poses upside
risk.
Implications of BoJ’s 2% terminal rate for global assets look less ominous than feared. 
First, the pace of hikes matters more than the level. 2027 tightening accelerates only 
from 50bps/annum in ’25/’26 to 75bps in ’27E. Second, even after this move – which is 
well priced in forwards – the 2y JN-US yield spread of -2.7% is within bottom quantile of 
20y range (and 50bps worse than at the turn of ’26), while 10y JP real-yield spread is 
near 20y lows. Third, carry unwinds historically reflect VaR shocks, sharp risk-asset 
corrections or the pace of relative yield spreads rather than rate differentials. For 
instance, even in 3Q24 – the most recent episode of rapid yen appreciation – the 2y JP-
US spread was 175bps tighter than today, without triggering repatriation. Lastly, GPIF 
repatriation is a tail risk, but high governance hurdles keep this outside our base case. 
Nonetheless, it bears monitoring because even a 5 ppt of AUM shift into domestic bonds 
and equities would generate ~$90bn of flows. While manageable globally, follow-on 
life-insurer repatriation could amplify the risk of yen appreciation, carry unwinds.
Japan’s net portfolio flows improved in 2026, but only marginally; 12m rolling net 
portfolio flows of 2.8% of GDP are only marginally better than 2.3% in ’25. Even that’s 
driven mostly by stronger foreign purchases, rather than domestic selling of foreign 
assets. This improvement was also concentrated in equities, while debt inflows mostly 
reflected rotation away from bills into bonds, with momentum reversing in H2 2026. 
This loss of momentum in H2 26 likely reflects JP Equities' underperformance vs. US, and 
not necessarily BoJ hikes-driven pull in capital flows. FX Implications: Although there's a 
merit in EUR/JPY decline into 1H27, we're not anticipating a significant pullback in 
USDJPY.
Japan’s exit from deflation created an unusually supportive regime for equities: stronger 
pricing power, reforms and a weaker yen boosted margins, while negative real rates and 
a steep curve kept policy accommodative. Those tailwinds are now fading. The real yield 
has turned positive for the first time in ten years, and the yield curve is flattening, while 
the Tankan output-input price gap has narrowed sharply. Yet the TOPIX forward P/E 
remains near 10-year relative highs versus equal-weight S&P 500 and STOXX 600, 
leaving the risk-reward increasingly skewed toward relative underperformance.
Japan’s spillovers to global bonds have been negligible this year and should remain 
limited in 2027. UST yields exert far greater influence on DM yields than JGBs, with 
global yields  still primarily driven by policy expectations, inflation and growth. 
Expansionary Japanese fiscal policy could nevertheless push 10y JGB yields higher, 
modestly lifting DM yields and limiting our forecasted decline in USTs in '27. Moreover, 
Japanese overseas assets holdings, as a share of recipients' bonds/equity markets, 
average only ~5% across the top ten destinations.

20. Implications of the US and French elections?
Political uncertainty is impacting the policy outlook on both sides of the Atlantic: in the 
US, the midterm elections have ushered in increased Democratic control of Congress, 
and in France, the presidential elections are scheduled for April/May 2027.
In the US, increased Democratic control of Congress likely leaves shutdown risk and 
wider deficits as the near-term focus, but we expect potential spillovers into monetary 
policymaking. Investors will also be watching for increased oversight of AI and the data 
center buildout as this is a key driver of growth and equity market performance. 
Federal funding expires December 11, and the debt ceiling is expected to bind in Q1 
2027. A funding extension into 2027 likely comes during the lame duck. A debt ceiling 
increase during the lame duck is possible via reconciliation but we expect this too spills 
over into 2027. Bargaining given the likely lack of veto-proof majority for Democrats in 
the Senate, could widen deficits and raise shutdown risk. Democrats will likely seek 
higher non-defense discretionary spending, moderation of  incoming Medicaid savings, 
and in return Republicans will likely seek higher defense spending. 
Greater Democratic control could increase AI oversight, though bipartisan consensus 
remains limited. Requiring hyperscalers to cover power costs, grid upgrades and 
potential permitting reform offer more scope for agreement. State and local level action 
also warrants watching. There are currently 406 active moratoriums on data center 
construction at the local level and a quarter of State governors have issued executive 
orders limiting data center construction. Lifting deposit insurance limits and reforms to 
increase competition in the credit card market could have bipartisan support. 
The Senate result matters for the appointment process including Federal Reserve 
governors. With a Democratic majority in the Senate we expect as many as four 
Governors leave in 2027 or early 2028. This could impact the administration’s ability to 
install its nominees, providing the Fed with some additional institutional insulation, but 
at the same time potentially leaving vacancies  in the Board. 
France will hold presidential elections in April and May 2027 to choose a successor to 
Emmanuel Macron, who is constitutionally barred from seeking a third consecutive 
term. Current opinion polls place Marine Le Pen of the far-right Rassemblement National 
as the frontrunner. Her election would likely imply less support for European integration, 
a tougher stance on immigration, and a more uncertain fiscal outlook. In particular, she 
advocates lowering the retirement age, already low by international standards, financed 
in part through a reduced French contribution to the EU budget.
The election comes at a time when France's public finances are already under pressure. 
The budget deficit, at 5.4% of GDP in 2025, has consistently been among the highest in 
the Eurozone  over the past decade, with the expenditure and revenue ratio the second 
and third highest, respectively. A key driver is social spending, which exceeds the 
Eurozone  average by 3.7% of GDP, largely due to higher pension expenditure. Public 
debt was 115.7% of GDP in 2025 and remains on an upward path. Stabilising the debt 
ratio would require fiscal consolidation of at least 3½% of GDP. Yet fiscal adjustment has 
proven politically difficult, not least because of the fragmented parliament. 
 A key question in the coming months is whether a centrist, fiscally more conservative 
candidate can emerge as a credible challenger to Marine Le Pen. Investors will also 
closely monitor the legislative elections, likely to be held in June or July 2027, given their 
implications for fiscal policy. If parliamentary fragmentation persists, even under a new 
president, it would likely constrain both fiscal deterioration and fiscal consolidation. 
With political uncertainty set to persist and bond spreads rising, attention is also turning 
to the ECB's potential response, particularly the possible use of its Transmission 
Protection Instrument (TPI). Before intervening with the TPI, the ECB would likely assess 
financing conditions across the Eurozone  as a whole, including whether stress in 
government bond markets is spilling over to the broader economy. It may also consider 
other tools first, such as liquidity support.

Global Economics & Markets Outlook 9 November 2026

Global Economics & Markets Outlook
UBS Research
1. Long Nasdaq vs Russell 2000
We see the underperformance of Nasdaq 100 vs Russell 2000 since early October as an 
attractive entry point to a secular relative value trade that is on a multi-year uptrend. 
NDX 12m forward EPS has risen by ~2x more than RTY EPS over the past 12mo+, yet 
NDX has underperformed RTY as NDX P/E has fallen toward 2022 levels. 
The stronger fundamentals of the NDX in terms of  growth, profitability and 
exposure to the AI cycle  are not reflected in the valuation,in our view. NDX has a 
HOLT NTM cash flow ROI of ~30% vs RTY at ~10%. RTY is more vulnerable to tighter 
financial conditions: it has a larger share of unprofitable companies and higher median 
net debt/EBITDA, while NDX has median net cash. NDX also scores much higher on our 
REVS framework given better earnings momentum, growth and quality than RTY. 
Additionally, as the regime has shifted firmly out of recovery to an expansion/slowdown, 
it favors NDX over RTY.
AI demand is driving the cycle, and it is inflecting on the steep part of the 
adoption curve. Annualized q/q dollar growth in cloud revenue across hyperscalers and 
neoclouds nearly tripled to ~$86bn in Q3 from ~$31bn in Q1, on a ~$540bn run rate. 
Adoption curves rarely stall soon after they steepen, and chip-based forecasts point to 
~51GW of new data center capacity in 2027, nearly double this year's ~27GW. That 
spending lands mostly in NDX's semis, software and platforms. However, data center 
slowing and pauses after the US midterms are likely to be a headwind to 2028 capacity 
growth, and power is still the binding constraint - but that looks to be more in consensus 
estimates and more in the price after the recent Tech selloff.
Buybacks appear set to jump for Tech companies, with Tech LTM OCF up nearly 
40% y/y, and buybacks have tended to follow OCF with a 2-4 quarter lag. Nvidia and 
Micron alone could run at >25% of total LTM US buybacks, and both sit in NDX, while 
other Tech firms are likely to buy back shares after the outsized de-rating. On the other 
side, RTY companies are less profitable with lower margins and should not benefit as 
much from increased buyback flow. 
The principal risks to the trade are sharply lower rates for good reasons (lower 
inflation, etc) and a growth/AI disappointment. Evidence of broadening AI 
monetisation and accelerating cloud OCF from backlog conversion would validate AI 
capex sustainability and reverse the recent rise in risk premia across the whole AI supply 
chain. Also, RTY should  underperform as weaker borrowers face increasing funding 
pressure. Rising corporate issuance, led by AI-related financing, will compete with heavy 
government borrowing for capital.

Global Economics & Markets Outlook 9 November 2026
ab 68
Global Economics & Markets Outlook
UBS Research
2.
Long 
Global 
Industrials 
vs 
Consumer
Discretionary
Global industrials outperformed consumer discretionary early this year, but the pair has 
range-traded since  February. We think we will again see industrial outperformance as 
the data centre build now needs physical capacity, policy favours local production, and 
households are running out of spending power. We would go long MSCI World 
Industrials against MSCI World Consumer Discretionary.
Data centre suppliers are now building capacity of their own. The AI capex cycle 
needs large volumes of switchgear, transformers, cooling, cabling and memory. 
Suppliers already have multi-year backlogs, so they are now spending to expand their 
own production. That spreads the cycle from data centre operators to the capital goods 
companies that equip new factories. Some examples:

Power equipment: Transformers are the tightest bottleneck. Fortune Electric now 
runs three shifts and plans to raise capacity by 64% by 2027. Eaton's new three-
phase transformer plant costs $300m. That is more than seven times the average 
spend on each of the other 23 facilities in its expansion plan.

Cooling and modular units: Munters has expanded its Virginia data centre site by 
about 40%, and it can switch other factories to cooling if demand jumps. Comfort 
Systems has raised its target for factory-built modular capacity to 6m sq ft by end-
2027, and bought a 450,000 sq ft building to get there. Vertiv says its supply 
capacity for 2027–28 could roughly double, and much of it is already committed.
Reshoring and protectionism support US/EU production. Tariffs and supply-chain 
security favour new capacity in the US and Europe, and defence spending adds more. 
The US ISM manufacturing index rose to 55.5 recently, led by production/restocking.
Consumers are waiting for relief that hasn't come. Energy bills and interest costs 
now take a larger share of income, and real wages are barely keeping up. US holiday 
spending plans are down 1% year-on-year. In ASEAN, 72% of consumers are cutting 
discretionary purchases. China's auto sales fell 21.2% in September, and Australian 
retail sales have been flat since September. High-income US households are the 
exception but rely on a wealth effect that has been stagnant for six months.
Earnings revisions favour industrials. Positioning is crowded in consumer 
discretionary. Consumer discretionary revisions  have fallen below zero. Industrials 
revisions are still positive at about 1%. Consumer discretionary crowding is now quite 
elevated with a wide gap to industrials.  Stock-level REVS signals tell the same story: the 
industrials median is about 0.19 against about 0.07 for consumer discretionary, and the 
consumer sector has a much fatter negative tail.

Global Economics & Markets Outlook 9 November 2026
ab 69
Global Economics & Markets Outlook
UBS Research
3. Long MSCI Korea vs MSCI India
Korea remains our preferred AI exposure within EM: Its memory and semiconductor 
stocks (66% of index) sit at a key bottle-neck in the AI value chain. The result is a once-
in-a-generation earnings and free cashflow uplift. Importantly, the earnings upgrades 
are no longer restricted to the tech sector in Korea, but are broadening to other areas 
too. While there are concerns on the pace of datacentre rollouts in the US post the 
midterms, we note that 1) the AI story remains supply constrained rather than demand 
constrained, 2) the market valuation currently expects only 40% of UBS EBITDA 
forecasts for the next three years to be achieved 
On the other hand - India’s fundamentals remain on track, but its valuation premium is 
harder to justify while earnings revisions remain subdued. Its IT-services exposure also 
faces greater uncertainty from AI adoption  (and could even be seen as sitting on the 
opposite side of the AI adoption trade vs Korea). The divergent outcomes from AI also 
feed into a divergent external balance impact on economies: the Korean Won is better 
positioned while  India’s deficit leaves it more vulnerable. Meanwhile, EM active funds 
are overweight India and underweight Korea, suggesting some scope for positioning to 
shift if the earnings divergence persists. Our perception premium score shows that 
India's underperformance over the last 18 months is largely explained by weaker 
fundamentals, rather than a real de-rating.
