4. Long V2X vs VIX
Political uncertainty is rising on both sides of the Atlantic, but the risks differ: In 
the US, increased Democratic control of congress points to gridlock, shutdown risk, and 
wider deficits. Federal funding expires on 11 December and the debt ceiling is expected 
to bind in Q1 2027, with both likely spilling into next year. These risks matter, but they 
tend to play out through negotiations that are usually resolved, and any volatility that 
manifests around these events has historically been short-lived. Investors will also watch 
for tighter AI oversight, with 406 local moratoriums on data center construction already 
in place, and possible Fed Board vacancies if a Democratic Senate blocks nominees.  
France poses a different risk: President Emmanuel Macron cannot run again and far-
right leader Marine Le Pen leads the polls (Figure 194 Polym
arket ods for top five 2027 French presidential election candidates shows a com
anding early lead for Le Pen). Her platform includes less 
support for European integration and a lower retirement age. That would come on top 
of the Eurozone's most strained public finances: a deficit of 5.4% of GDP in 2025, debt 
of 115.7% of GDP that is still rising, and consolidation of at least 3.5% of GDP needed 
just to stabilize it. Legislative elections in June or July extend the uncertainty, and a 
fragmented parliament would limit any fiscal fix. 
The case for owning European equity volatility rests on precedent, timing, and the 
lack of a clear backstop. The volatility risk premium during the 2017 French election was 
the highest on record for a sovereign election (Figure 195 French elections in 2017 saw the highest volatility risk prem
ia in recorded history).  A decade later, Marine Le Pen 
starts in a stronger position in the polls, albeit with a more moderate policy agenda, but 
with French finances in a much weaker state. The 18 April and 2 May voting dates also 
give investors clear points to position around. The ECB could also likely try other tools 
before using its Transmission Protection Instrument, which potentially leaves room for 
spreads to widen and stress to spread into European equities. 
V2X  > VIX into French elections: We think investors should favor going long 
European volatility (V2X) over US volatility (VIX) heading into the 2027 French election 
cycle (spot reference: V2X at 21.3, VIX at 25.6). Investors may consider implementing 
this via V2X vs. VIX ATMF straddle swaps on an April expiry which would capture the 
second round of voting on 2 May and would be the live future expiry through the actual 
event. March expiry would technically not capture the first round of voting in its 30-day 
forward window, thus may not embed any election risk premia in the contract. Selling 
US equity volatility as a funding leg makes sense as record concentration driving 
elevated dispersion and record volatility supply continue to plague realized moves at the 
index level.

5. Long US Growth  & Quality vs US Value
Growth dispersion is elevated, as  fastest growers break out. We sort stocks into 
quintiles by fwd sales CAGR, our proprietary measure of consensus sales growth from 
next year out to five years. Stocks in the top quintile are forecast to grow sales 15.3% a 
year, the highest since 2003 and 2.2 stdev above the avg since 2004. The bottom 
quintile is forecast to grow just 1.8%. That 13.5pp spread ranks in the 83rd pctile since 
2004, and it widened this year as top quintile growth rose 2.6pp while the bottom 
slipped 0.2pp. 
Quality dispersion is even wider, with top quintile CFROI at a record 37%. We 
sort stocks into quintiles using HOLT cash flow return on investment (CFROI). The top 
quintile has added 8.4pp since Jan 2023 vs 1.0pp for the bottom, which sits at 4.6%. 
That leaves a 32.4pp spread, the 99th pctile since 2004 and well above its 22.2pp avg. 
Scaled by the median it is 2.3x vs a 1.9x avg, so quality dispersion is elevated on both 
measures. High returns also feed growth, since a company earning 37% on its capital 
gets more growth from each dollar it reinvests, as long as those returns hold.
Valuation dispersion has not followed, P/E spreads compressed this year.  The 
most expensive quintile has de-rated 4 turns YTD to 31.4x, while the cheapest slipped 
only 0.3 turns to 9.5x. That leaves a 21.9 turn spread, below its 24.6 avg since 2004 and 
in the 43rd pctile. Relative to the median the spread is 1.3x, only the 22nd pctile. This 
matters because fundamentals set the multiple. In our SPX valuation model, 100bp of 
fwd sales CAGR is worth 1.4 turns of P/E and 100bp of CFROI is worth 0.75 turns. Wider 
growth and quality spreads should have widened P/E spreads. They did not.
Growth and quality rank ahead of value in REVS. Within quality we prefer the high 
growth quadrant, as low growth quality behaves more like duration. Momentum ranks 
in between, since its composition has churned toward Biotech and Healthcare after the 
June derisking. Our REVS framework, which scores stocks on regime, earnings, 
valuation and sentiment, agrees. High quality scores well above low quality and high 
momentum well above low momentum, while both high and low value are negative. 
High value screens cheap, yet its earnings  scores is negative. Low value is the mirror 
image, expensive with strong earnings trends. So a value tilt today buys cheapness at the 
cost of earnings momentum, the trade-off the research basket pair avoids. 
Growth+CFROI at a Discount over Expensive Fundamentals is our favored trade, 
a ~22% P/E discount for 2.5x the sales growth. Our 56 stock Growth+CFROI at a 
Discount basket (UBEQHGCD) trades at 16x P/E vs 21x for Expensive Fundamentals 
(UBEQEXFN). Yet it has a 14% fwd sales CAGR vs 5%, a 33% CFROI vs 6% and a 6% 
FCF yield vs -2%. That leaves a PEG on sales CAGR of 1.1x vs 3.8x. 
Growth and CFROI rolling over is the main risk, but compressed P/E spreads 
already discount it. Upgrades to fwd sales CAGR are likely to slow after this summer's 
jump. Yet the market is not paying up for the growth and quality it has today.

6. Long Earnings momentum factor in Europe
Earnings momentum has been the best-performing factor in European equities since at 
least 2010. Our long-short series has roughly tripled in that time, ahead of price 
momentum at about 2.7x. Value, growth, size and quality all sit between about 0.8x and 
1.15x. We think the factor is still worth owning in Europe, even as momentum struggles 
elsewhere.
Quick-trading funds move prices on revisions - We see the source of the 
performance in earnings momentum coming from the relatively narrow investor base in 
European equities. Long-only institutions trade in size but infrequently and so it is hedge 
funds and quant funds, trading daily and reacting to revisions that set the price on most 
days and the trends over most weeks. Revisions therefore feed into prices quickly, but 
the trend lasts while slower investment processes catch up. Revisions also tend to beget 
more revisions as the underlying trends driving them tend to have persistence too.
Europe's upgrades come from domestic spending, not just AI - Globally, 
'momentum' has been  long the AI capex supply chain and short the companies AI may 
disrupt, and both legs have hurt it lately. Europe has much lower exposure to the AI 
theme compared to US/EM. The current top revisions are in sectors like shipping, energy, 
utilities/renewables, metals, capital goods and banks. ASML is the only obvious AI-
sensitive name but it supports capacity expansion that we see as the emergent 
momentum within that AI theme anyway. Upgrades in Europe are generally coming 
from fiscal policy/spending: German fiscal support for infrastructure and defence, wider 
EU defence budgets, banks lending into that capex, a rising ECB rate path that lifts net 
interest income, and electrification spending from the Rescue and Recovery Fund. 
Consumers and carmakers dominate the short side - Consumer companies in the 
auto and luxury sector continue to face demand and competitive pressures in China. 
Other consumer stocks like Lindt and ABF also have seen recent earnings downgrades. 
Domestically, high energy prices, higher yields and job and income uncertainty are 
squeezing Europe's consumer. In our view, these are persistent themes, which helps 
explain the high correlation between earnings momentum and our REVS framework 
allocations that favour consumer themes the least.
Revisions were more important than bond yields rising - Bond yields have 
unsettled markets in recent months, but as the US 10-year yield rose from 3.8% to 
5.3%, we showed that over the final three months, stocks with HOLT CFROI upgrades 
returned a median +10% more than companies without positive revisions. 15 of 17 
sectors showed a positive performance spread within the sector based on earnings 
revisions alone. Yields are important in setting valuations by sector according to bond 
betas, but revisions still decided which stocks won inside each sector and reinforce our 
view that the factor to own in Europe is earnings momentum.

7. US rates 1y fwd 5s30s steepener
As we approach the end of this mini hiking cycle, 1Y forward 5s/30s SOFR curve 
steepeners is our preferred trade in US rates, for a few reasons (Figure 200W
e expect the curve to stepen from
 stil relatively flat levels over 2027). First, the 
broad curve tends to trade counter-directionally with front-end policy 
expectations and is consistent with our medium-term bullish duration view. Even 
though the gap between market forwards and our forecast has narrowed over the past 
month, with the markets priced for roughly two hikes over the next year, we expect 
front-end policy expectations to remain relatively capped. Importantly, our economists 
expect that we’ll see more downward progress on inflation from the spring onwards, 
with core PCE projected to reach 2.5% by YE27 and headline PCE inflation at 2.1%. 
Moreover, with financing conditions exhibiting greater sensitivity to higher yields, and 
capex expectations moderating recently, particularly in the wake of the midterm election 
results, this should limit front-end tightening expectations. Looking further ahead, our 
economists continue to project two rate cuts in 1H2028. 
Second, cyclical patterns around the last hike in a cycle suggest long-end 
steepeners offer better risk-reward than front-end exposure. Figure 201Cyclical paterns sugest long-end stepeners ofer beter risk/reward ahead of the last hike  shows 
the average cumulative change in curve pairs from 3 months prior to the last hike in a 
cycle until 6 months after, based on the last four hiking cycles. Notably, the front end of 
the curve tends to flatten on average in the final months prior to the last hike, with the 
2s/5s curve flattening over that period in each of the last four cycles. Meanwhile, the 
5s/30s curve tends to trade more range-bound ahead of the last hike before leading the 
way steeper once the Fed goes on hold. With the last hike projected to be delivered in 
the first quarter, we think it's still too early to hold long duration or curve steepeners at 
the front end of the curve. This dynamic also favors holding steepeners in forward space.
Third, duration supply should continue to add steepening pressure to the curve 
into 2027. Over the past year, the acceleration in duration supply has been driven 
largely by IG corporate issuance, while Treasury duration supply has slowed modestly, 
net of buybacks. Next year, a further acceleration in IG issuance should be accompanied 
by a pickup in Treasury duration supply, as Treasury begins to increase coupon auction 
sizes around mid-year. We see fixed income duration supply (across IG corporates and 
Treasuries) rising from $3.84tn 10-year Treasury equivalents in 2025 to $3.99tn in 2026 
to $4.29tn in 2027. For now, we expect the rise in corporate issuance should continue to 
exert more steepening pressure on the swap curve relative to Treasuries, and we prefer 
holding steepeners on the SOFR curve (Figure 202Swap spreads have widened alongside higher long-end yields over the past year, likely partialy reflecting the presure from
 IG suply; also see here).

8. European rates 2s10s flattener
We like 2s10s ESTR flatteners, with the gap between 2y and 10y rates narrowing. The 
euro area faces a different inflation path from the US, with the peak still ahead just as 
German fiscal stimulus gathers momentum (Figure 203Germ
any's widening deficit into 2027 im
plies greater duration suply). We enter €STR 2s10s at 17bps 
targeting -20bps with stop at 35bps (Figure 204€STR OIS 2s10s flatener - Enter: 17bps; Target: -20bps; Stop: 35bps).
Markets may be too quick to dismiss the ECB’s response to supply-driven inflation. So 
far, tighter policy has not come at a material cost to growth, and UBS expects two more 
ECB hikes of 25bps in December and March.
Energy remains the key upside risk. Winter will put fuel costs back in focus for 
households, while second-round effects typically emerge only with a lag. Europe should 
have enough gas, but a cold winter or another supply shock could tighten the market 
quickly, revive inflation concerns (Figure 206Euro area 1y inflation expectation (EUSW
I1) trades at 3.32%
. Euro 1y forward in 1y inflation trades at 2.17%) and flatten the curve.
A more hawkish ECB should ultimately pin down the long-end of the yield curve. For 
now, wider French spreads have also not produced meaningful spillovers into equities 
and credit. We see upside risk to country spreads in H1 with France trading at 170bps or 
wider in case of deteriorating liquidity conditions  so 10y risk-free rates could also fall in a 
flight to quality.

9. Receive MXN 2y2y rates
Mexican rates have been one of our key trades in 2026 and, while excessive tightening 
expectations at the front end have come down, we think the move still has room to run 
into 2027. Rates are still priced for a sizeable and relatively front-loaded Banxico hiking 
cycle, with 120bp of hikes priced over the next two years, compared with just 50bp for 
the Fed over the same policy horizon, and roughly 200bp over the next five years. We 
think this is too aggressive given contained demand-side price pressures, improving core 
inflation dynamics and a Banxico Board that not only appears to have a high bar to 
resume hiking, but may also be inclined to fine-tune the policy rate lower based on the 
September policy minutes  (UBSe). 
While headline inflation may pick up in the near term as El Niño pushes agricultural 
prices higher, with oil adding further upside risk, we expect Banxico to look through 
largely supply-driven shocks and focus on more persistent price pressures. On this front, 
the picture remains benign: trimmed-mean core inflation is running close to 2% (3m 
SAAR), while Banxico’s supercore and fundamental measures remain within their 2-4% 
confidence band. Importantly, core services inflation, a key concern for Banxico, has 
started to improve after several months of stickiness, falling to 4.3% in September from 
4.6% in May, with monthly readings increasingly consistent with historical norms. 
Goods price pressures should also remain contained: processed-food inflation should 
ease given its lagged relationship with international FAO food prices, while the growing 
presence of hard discounters and elevated inventories should help keep other 
merchandise inflation low. Against this backdrop, we expect headline/core inflation to 
fall to 3.4%/3.7% by end-27, both below consensus and within Banxico’s tolerance 
band.
Growth momentum has picked up after an extended period of weak activity, supported 
by recovering investment and a booming tech sector. Tech exports are up 150% y/y and 
now exceed auto exports at USD200bn (9.5% of GDP). Despite relatively low domestic 
value added, the sector has contributed around 0.6pp to growth over the past year 
(UBSe), roughly half of the expansion, but with more limited spillovers to formal 
employment (1.5% y/y). USMCA uncertainty should continue to restrain private 
investment at the margin, even as we remain constructive on an eventual agreement, 
while the 2027 budget points to a modestly smaller fiscal impulse, with the public-sector 
borrowing requirement down to 3.9% of GDP from 4.1% in 2026. Against this 
backdrop, we expect growth to accelerate only modestly to 2.2% in 2027 from 1.7% in 
2026, not enough, in our view, to drive sustained demand-side price pressures.
In this context, we recommend 2y2y receivers, targeting a 75-100bp decline from 8.4% 
currently as rate expectations normalize. The sector offers around 4bp of monthly roll, 
comparable to the 2y point but with considerably less sensitivity to near-term oil risks. A 
key risk to this trade is the MXN and the potential for a disorderly depreciation. While a 
more dovish policy stance could ultimately lead to a weaker peso, strong BoP 
fundamentals should help contain excessive FX volatility, and we think the Board is 
unlikely to deliver an aggressive dovish pivot that could compromise the peso's stability.

10. Receive 3y2y AUD vs paying 3y2y NZD rates
Receive AUD 3y2y (6mth BBSW) vs paying NZD 3y2y. Entry: +67bps or 1.9 standard 
deviations above the long-run average; Target: +30bps; Stop: +75bps.
In the current global environment, rates volatility means holding an outright duration 
position while obeying stop losses has been very challenging for investors. Hence, we 
recommend going long AUD 3y2y rates vs short NZD 3y2y rates. The reasons we like this 
trade include: 
1. This cross-market spread is trading wide of our fair value estimate of +36bps.
Based on central bank projections and market pricing, our fair value estimate
should tighten towards +7bps by end-27. The model variables include Trans-
Tasman deviations in unemployment, inflation and the 3-month rates.
2. This cross-market expression circumvents most of the global noise. The 12-month
rolling correlation with US 10-year Treasuries is currently nearly 0. While there has
been a relatively  wide range over time, this is statistically independent of the level
of and changes in US 10-year rates.
3. We like the asymmetric risk setup heading into the election. The trade would
perform if the RBNZ turned hawkish and hiked aggressively following an
expansionary shift in fiscal policy. Alternatively, it may not underperform much if
the RBNZ delivers further surprises on the dovish side, given the market is likely to
think the RBNZ are falling further behind the curve, which would see 2s5s steepen.
4. The AUD leg is likely to work best if the RBA keeps policy restrictive, without hiking
too much  more, in line with UBS's terminal forecast of 4.85%. The split 6-3 vote to
hike in November suggests this scenario looks likely.
5. Carry is broadly neutral. This is relatively attractive, in our view, given how difficult
it has been to run positive carry, short rate positions in the NZD market.
6. AUD Kangaroo issuance has boomed $95bn in 2026 YTD, up 14% y/y. This should
lead to net receiving in the 3-8 sector across swap and cross currency markets. So
we also don't mind receiving 3y2y AUDUSD XCCY with this tailwind behind the
curve segment.
Key risks: 

AUD fiscal policy turns even more expansionary, potentially well ahead of the 2028 
federal election in response to a sharp rise in populism. This could push the AUD-
NZD rates complex wider.

NZ immaculate disinflation could allow the RBNZ to remain dovish and the NZ 
currency to continue depreciating. However, this looks unlikely with labour 
productivity currently weak and select domestic inflation categories sticky.

11. Long EU IG vs US IG
We initiate a Long (1.8 x 1) EU IG vs. US IG, duration and FX-hedged, implemented 
through index-tracking credit vehicles, underpinned by the following key convictions.
First, we see the recent selloff as an attractive entry point into EU IG: spreads reached 
~110bp at the wides and, at current levels, offer compelling asymmetry into 2027 
against our 95bp Q2’27 target. While roughly 20% of the widening reflects France, the 
bulk has been driven by the higher-for-longer rates narrative and the greater rates 
sensitivity of EU credit relative to the US. France remains a key risk to our view, but the 
repricing has been sharp and we believe spreads are approaching a more balanced 
clearing level for French political risk. We prefer targeted convex trades for further 
France downside over regional credit RV. High-quality EU credit now looks materially 
cheaper post-repricing, especially on a volatility-adjusted basis.
The second leg of the thesis is that Europe has priced substantially more of the rates 
shock than the US, leaving a cleaner catalyst for relative outperformance as inflation 
turns. We expect EZ inflation to pass its inflection point and the ECB reaction function to 
turn incrementally more dovish into Q2, shifting the debate from further tightening to 
pricing out hikes and ultimately pricing in cuts. A positive risk would be a sharp fall in oil 
prices as tensions ease in the Middle East. Crucially, even if higher-for-longer pressures 
partially unwind in the US, we do not think this necessarily translates into US IG 
outperformance: the rates premium is embedded in EU spreads to a much greater 
extent.
Third, AI risk sentiment has amplified the selloff, but we see limited evidence of broader 
impairment in EU IG beyond idiosyncratic and sector-specific exposures. Our 2027 AI 
concern remains primarily a supply/technical story. Even if European issuance reaches 
the same share of the IG index as US issuance in 2026, with a comparable Tech-sector 
spread impact, we estimate the first-order effect at less than 2bp of index widening.
We think the trade should also perform well across a range of alternative scenarios by 
Q2'27:
Scenario #1 – Oil at $150/bbl: the inflation shock keeps both central banks restrictive, 
with the Fed at 4.75% and the ECB at 3.5% by Q2’27. However, with considerable 
negativity already priced into EU spreads and the lack of a negative growth shock in 
Europe in this scenario, we expect EU IG widening to be contained. Positive carry should 
act as a tailwind. We estimate the trade delivers ~0.2%.
Scenario #2 – Oil at $60/bbl: lower energy prices provide a larger disinflationary boost to 
Europe, allowing the ECB to keep rates on hold in Q1’27, despite a hawkish Fed. EU IG 
spreads subsequently outperform into Q2’27, reversing the relative spread widening vs. 
US IG since August this year. We estimate the trade delivers ~1.0%.
Scenario #3 – Bond yields at 6.5%: while this is our most bearish full-year scenario for 
European credit, the rates component should drive European outperformance on a 
total-return basis through 1H’27, reflecting the lower sensitivity of European rates to the 
growth and fiscal narrative. We estimate the trade delivers ~0.1%.
Scenario #4 – AI disappoints: a sharp US slowdown triggers aggressive Fed easing to 
2.5%, by Q2’27, while the ECB responds more moderately. US IG underperforms 
materially. We estimate the trade delivers ~0.9%.

12. Long SOAF vs Brazil credit
SOAF and BRAZIL long-end currently trade around similar spreads levels, which in our 
view underprices diverging fiscal/rating outlooks as well as supply dynamics. We 
recommend going long SOAF 49s vs BRAZIL 47s targeting a z-spread gap of -45bps (vs 
-4bps currently). Both bonds have roughly the same cash price.
In South Africa, primary surpluses (UBSf 1.9%/GDP in 2027) have become a policy 
anchor to deal with an unfavourable nominal GDP/interest rate mix, in a context of a 
fiscally conservative GNU setup. Brazil's growth/interest rate backdrop is  challenging 
too. But in  Brazil the bar for reaching primary surpluses in coming years is high. In fact, 
UBS econ team expects  headline deficit next year to be > 200bps above consensus.
Consider this, South Africa and Brazil's debt/GDP ratios rose more or less in tandem 
between 2022 and 2025 (by 7.3/7ppt, respectively). But 2026 was a turning point and 
UBS now expects a clear decoupling. For Brazil we project the debt/GDP ratio to rise 
10.8ppt  between 2026 and 2028, while in South Africa we expect a decline (of 1ppt). 
Accordingly, rating momentum in South Africa should stay  positive in the next 6-12m 
with outright rating upgrades to BB+ on the cards.
We find this RV attractive also because  supply of BRAZIL bonds could become 
headwinds for spreads. Brazil credit spreads have been consistently  tight in a BB context 
despite having interest expenses (7.5%/GDP) that exceed even  the average single-B 
bucket (6.1%/GDP). We  attribute this to BRAZIL scarcity value as the stock of bonds is 
low - equal to ~  2%/GDP (vs 6%/6.4% of GDP in Mexico/Colombia, for example). But 
this could change, we think. The gap between Brazil LC and HC yields is > 900bps or 
~2STD above its 10y avg. This is likely to increase the Treasury's appetite for issuing 
eurobonds. We see upside risk to our gross supply projection of $9.5bn.
What risks to monitor? The ANC party in South Africa is due to hold its elective 
conference in Dec 2027 where a successor to President Ramaphosa will be voted and 
announced - to become the party's nominee in the next general elections (due in May 
2029).  We expect markets' attention to shift to this event not before the middle of 2027 
or once there is clarity on possible nominees. However, we acknowledge that 
introducing uncertainties to South Africa's fiscal and reform agenda  earlier could 
weaken the case for the long SOAF leg of the trade. Otherwise, we note that Brazil is a 
net oil exporter and South Africa is a net oil importer. But we do not expect oil prices to 
be a big driver here given that in both cases  fiscal revenues are not heavily affected by 
the price of oil.

13. Long Gold vs EUR
We recommend buying a 30 June 2027 expiry XAUEUR 4055 digital call for 
19.9% of payout (spot reference 3585.95), with maximum losses limited to the option 
premium. This expression combines our constructive gold view—as per our end-27 
XAUUSD target of $5,000/oz—with our bearish EURUSD bias, as defined by our end-
2027 1.10 target. As such, we see XAUEUR upside extending to 4545 by end-2027.
The October break in gold’s traditional correlation appears set to reverse. So far 
in Q4, gold’s long-standing negative correlations with US real yields and Fed hiking 
expectations have come under pressure, with gold failing to rally amid lower US yields. 
Instead, gold took its lead from de-grossing in US equities ahead of US midterm 
elections and following mixed US tech earnings, trading more like a risk asset than a risk 
hedge. We view this as a temporary positioning effect, rather than a lasting regime 
change. With XAU risk reversal skews now bid for puts over calls (Figure 215 The risk-reward to gold longs is m
ore apealing now that puts are again trading at a prem
ium
 to cals), the risk-
reward of positioning for a return to traditional gold trading dynamics looks attractive, in 
our view.
French political uncertainty could weigh on EUR and reassert gold’s risk-hedge 
credentials. The prospect of an extended period of political uncertainty ahead of the 
French elections in April/May leaves us looking for EUR to trade as low as 1.07 by end-
Q2. We see risks that  this uncertainty will weigh on business confidence, growth 
expectations and on priced-in ECB rate hikes, causing rate differentials to move against 
the EUR. ECB action or policy innovations to contain French spreads during periods of 
high stress could also be XAU-positive as such developments are likely to be viewed as a 
further step towards fiscal dominance, especially if they expand the ECB balance sheet, 
ultimately reinforcing gold’s hard money appeal. 
Official-sector buying and seasonal demand should also support gold. Official-
sector gold purchases accelerated over the last two quarters, lifting YTD volumes by 
around 6% y/y. Seasonal Chinese demand ahead of Lunar New Year in early February 
should support gold, with investment remaining the dominant driver (Figure 216Investm

Investment demand from China is ongoing and should cushion gold pullbacks without a 
strong jewellery-demand recovery. Fresh Q1 investor allocations are also likely after this 
year’s substantial positioning reduction. Sentiment remains fundamentally bullish, 
though Q4 event risks and expected Fed hikes have kept investors sidelined. With US 
mid-term elections out of the way, a price recovery could revive interest, with fear of 
missing out reinforcing upward momentum.

14. Long JPY vs CHF
We recommend going long a 30 June 2027 expiry CHFJPY 187.55 digital put, for 
19.40% of payout (spot ref 193.89), with maximum losses limited to the upfront 
premium. The central idea to this expression is that prospects of FX intervention by  the 
BoJ / MoF and the SNB will curtail upside potential in the pair and allow it to move lower 
in a euro area stress scenario. The position benefits from positive carry and the strike  is 
set immediately below  the 12m low in the pair at 187.61, established on 30 Sep 2026. 
BoJ / MoF FX intervention is likely to limit JPY weakness. On the JPY front, we 
believe BoJ / MoF FX intervention fears limit potential for the yen to weaken beyond our 
2027 USDJPY 160 target. The trade idea incorporates this view, while also providing 
exposure to the possibility that Japanese investors might repatriate assets or at least 
reduce overseas portfolio holdings. 
CHF strength is also likely to be capped by SNB FX intervention. On the CHF side, 
prospects  of extended European political uncertainty leave us constructive on CHF vs 
EUR in H1, but we also believe that the SNB will counter excessive CHF appreciation via 
FX intervention, as we estimate it did most recently in larger sizes in April 2025 and May 
2026. We as such believe that EURCHF dips below 0.90 will likely find support as 
markets anticipate SNB intervention, and view CHF rallies vs higher-yielding currencies 
as opportunities to go short the franc to collect carry, with our constructive USDCHF 
0.84 target a direct expression of this view.

15. Long GBP vs SEK
We recommend going long a 30 Jun expiry GBPSEK 13.40 call with a European 
knockout barrier at 14.10, offered at 0.35% of notional, a significant discount vs the 
equivalent vanilla call offered at 0.90% (spot reference 13.1193). The EKO barrier at 
14.10 assumes the range high of the last 20 years holds. We see scope for GBPSEK 
upside for the following key reasons:
1. GBP should outperform SEK in a high global rates environment. GBP is
supported by a relatively high policy rate and a domestic economy that is proving
resilient to higher rates. In contrast, Sweden has the lowest policy rate within G10
outside the traditional funders of CHF and JPY, which leaves SEK struggling in pro-
carry markets. We expect the high global rates environment to persist, prolonging
the carry dynamic favouring GBP over SEK.
2. Relative capital flows support GBP over SEK. In the latest BoP data, the UK’s
broad basic balance (sum of current account, net FDI inflows and net portfolio
inflows) was -1.5% of GDP versus Sweden’s -3.8%. We think UK flows can
improve following a strong year for inbound M&A announcements, creating a
pipeline of future FDI flows. Sweden, meanwhile, has suffered considerable equity
portfolio outflows, dragging on SEK. US tech weakness more recently may have
generated some SEK-supportive repatriation flows, though we note that previous
such episodes proved short-lived and UBS equity strategists remain bullish on US
equities and AI.

Alternative

Global Economics & Markets Outlook 9 November 2026
ab 83
Introduction
If the last two years have shown anything, it is that modal forecasts – the single most 
likely outcome – are of limited use when the distribution of outcomes is fat-tailed or 
even bimodal. Energy prices could be very high or much lower depending on whether 
the Middle East conflict is resolved, and the futures curve has been a poor guide to 
actual prices. The AI capex cycle has likewise swung between exuberance and bear-
market gloom, given the unprecedented scale of investment, the rapid shift in market 
share from closed to open-source models, uncertainty over monetization, and funding, 
energy and political constraints. That volatility matters because the sector is driving the 
bulk of US growth, over half of global trade growth and most of the rise in equity 
valuations. 
As in every Global Markets Outlook, we therefore consider a number of alternative 
scenarios to help map the range of potential outcomes – an exercise that is especially 
useful this year. The aim is to think through the mechanics of how each could unfold and 
to size the market impact, and the modelling involves the entire Economics & Strategy 
group. We deliberately choose scenarios disruptive enough to move the global needle. 
Each is a low-probability event, though, given how fat the tails have become, less 
improbable than such scenarios would normally be. 
Description Scenarios
We have modelled four scenarios:
First, oil (Brent) at $150/bbl, on an assumption that shipping traffic through the Strait 
of Hormuz remains disrupted, attacks on ships, and potentially other infrastructure in 
the region, continue, and that inventories continue to draw, tightening markets further. 
We assume that prices rise quickly to $150/bbl in the first half of 2027, and that by Q1-
2028 a deal of some sort has been reached. Coupled with demand destruction, that 
pushes prices back below $100 by end-2028. Gas prices are assumed to largely track oil. 
We assume a normal winter where storage levels in Europe run down to 25%, and 
where the ban on long-term LNG contracts from Russia is potentially delayed, if needed, 
to keep prices in check. 
Second, oil (Brent) at $60/bbl. Dark transits are assumed to continue, with most of 
the oil getting out of the Strait of Hormuz, no further attacks on infrastructure post US 
midterms, and US/Iran agree to a deal to regulate traffic in the Strait. Markets would 
remain tight as inventories need to be rebuilt, but under this scenario there is sufficient 
détente between Iran and the US to allow markets to price out political risk premium 
and for shipping costs to come down. 
Third, 10-year US Treasury yields at 6½%. This is not an 'overheating' scenario, 
which we have modelled in the past, but rather an exogenous bond shock scenario 
where yields rise on some combination of fiscal concerns, high issuance from 
hyperscalers, growth optimism and a perception that r* is rising. We assume that global 
bond yields follow a similar trajectory as the US, with yields grinding higher and reaching 
their 6½% plateau by H2-27, before things start to break. As we discuss below, we 
ended up having to assume some inflation stickiness in this scenario, so that the central 
banks don't respond immediately when higher yields start to disrupt the economy (in 
some of our permutations the  bond yield shock self-corrected  too quickly in recessionary 
scenarios, which basically meant you could not see the bond shock clearly in the charts). 
Fourth, an AI 'disappointment' scenario. There are many ways in which such a 
scenario could play out and at some level it does not matter in that the core modelling 
objective was to see what would happen if the S&P had a 25%  correction. One plausible 
route is intensifying competition from Chinese open-source models, with lower prices 
and growing market share, which raises monetization fears for AI labs and, to a lesser 
extent, the hyperscalers. Slowing growth in monetization triggers a reassessment of AI 
optimism and an equity market correction. Weaker consumption then compounds it by 
squeezing the advertising and retail cashflow of Amazon, Alphabet and Meta, the same 
companies funding much of the AI capex. A 25% correction in US equity markets would 
destroy equity wealth equivalent to roughly 60% of GDP, on par with the GFC and the 
dot-com bust, as the equity market has more than doubled in size since then.  
The outlook for energy markets and 
the global AI trade feels more binary 
than 'modal'.
We consider four alternative 
scenarios to our baseline.
Brent oil at $150/bbl through end '27.
Brent oil at $60/bbl.
US Treasury yields at 6.5% with other 
markets following.
AI disappointment leading to a 25% 
equity market correction.

Global Economics & Markets Outlook 9 November 2026
ab 84
Impact on Global Economy
Scenarios 1 & 2: Oil at $150/bbl versus $60/bbl
The growth effects of the two oil scenarios are asymmetric (Figure 221Global GDP - deviation from
 baseline). Both are 
roughly equal-sized moves from the ~$100/bbl spot price when we ran the scenarios. At 
$150/bbl, global growth would be 40bp lower in 2027 and 20bp lower in 2028. At $60/
bbl, it would be only about 10bp higher in each year. 
Two factors drive this asymmetry. First, the policy response: we assume central banks 
react faster to rising inflation than to falling inflation. They hike by an additional 25-
50bp when oil is high and leave rates largely unchanged from baseline when it falls. 
Second, prices are stickier on the way down, especially core inflation. At $60/bbl, 
headline inflation falls by about 40bp in 2027 and core by only 10bp. At $150/bbl, 
headline rises by 140bp in 2027 and core by 40bp, with a further 20bp in 2028. Across 
regions, the high-oil scenario drags more on growth in emerging markets (EM) than in 
developed markets (DM), especially among oil importers.
Why the hit is smaller than in March. The $150/bbl growth hit is about half what we 
estimated in March. Growth responds to changes in oil prices, not their level. With spot 
already at $100/bbl, getting to $150 is a smaller and slower move than the one-year rise 
from $60 we modelled in March. That means less inflation within the year and so less 
erosion of real consumption. It is not obvious that moving from current oil price levels 
would necessarily cause a recession, though that partly depends on how markets 
respond (we have assumed that the S&P and Eurostoxx are roughly 15% below our 
baseline) and higher oil prices would still be a significant drag on growth.
Supply shocks with some spillover. At heart, both scenarios are supply-side inflation 
shocks (Figure 198). The spillover to core is meaningful: a 40bp rise in global core in 
2027 pushes inflation past levels where workers are likely to push for wage 
compensation. But the inflation impulse also fades fairly quickly. Once the base effects 
drop out a year after the shock, the rate of change falls to zero or turns negative. The 
growth effects start to fade by Q4 2027, a quarter before energy prices start to slow.





more, but higher-for-longer inflation delays its cuts, which widens the gap (see the 
panel chart at the end of this section).

halves if you spread it over two 
years.

Global Economics & Markets Outlook 9 November 2026
ab 85

NBP (Poland): 125bp above baseline, partly because its very accommodative 
starting point prompts a stronger reaction.

BCB (Brazil): 200bp above baseline. In practice it cuts only to 12% rather than the 
10% in our baseline, so real rates stay restrictive. 
In almost all countries, we assume some fiscal support (fuel subsidies, tax cuts) to soften 
the impact on consumers.
Policy rates at $60/bbl. Policy rates change little from our baseline. Core inflation is 
only 5–15bp lower depending on the region. The headline relief also starts from inflation 
that is generally above target, so it isn't enough to bring forward planned cuts by much. 
The exceptions are the RBA, RBNZ and ECB,  and several EM central banks (India, the 
Philippines, Poland, South Africa and Indonesia), which end 25bp below our baseline 
(only RBI is 50bp below). If energy prices ease early enough, the ECB skips the Q1 hike 
we currently expect (it hikes only once more in this scenario). The RBA, RBNZ and most 
EM central banks instead bring cuts forward somewhat.
Bottom line. The average policy rate changes  by year-end are of similar size in the two 
scenarios, though in opposite directions (Figure 228Global policy rates - deviation from
 baseline). But there is meaningfully more 
central bank activity  in response to higher oil prices than to lower ones. Intra-year (2027) 
policy rates globally increase 29bp (GDP-weighted) in Q3/Q4 2027 vs -11bp with lower 
oil.
Scenario 3: Bond yields at 6½%
The growth hit in this scenario is much larger than in the oil shocks (Figure 222Global growth QoQ, sar). Because 
the yield shock builds gradually, most of the damage lands in 2028: global growth is 
120bp below our baseline that year, against 20bp in 2027. The US, Canada, Japan, the 
Eurozone and South Africa all record consecutive quarterly contractions in 2028. 
The yield path. We assume 10y US Treasury yields reach 5.5% in Q1-27, 6% in Q2-27 
and peak at 6.5% in Q3-27. Some of the rise reflects strong fundamentals, but we 
assume a large part is exogenous, and that part does the most damage. Feedback loops 
then pull yields back down as growth weakens and central banks respond: to 6% in Q4-
27, 3.75% by mid-2028 and 3.1% by end-2028. Growth falls below the baseline from 
H1-27 as financial conditions start to tighten. Quarterly annualized global growth is 
43bp below the baseline by mid-2027 and 140bp below by mid-2028.
A large tightening in financial conditions. Most of the economic damage comes 
through the assumed market reaction to higher yields. Causality can run either way: 
from rates to the economy to equities, or from rates to equities to the economy. Given 
how much US consumption now relies on equity wealth effects, we think the second 
chain dominates. By end-2027 the S&P 500 is 16% below our baseline, or about 6% 
down on the year. That means household equity wealth is roughly 35% of GDP lower 
than in the baseline. How much this hits spending depends on how long the correction 
lasts. By end-2028 the S&P is still about 9% below the baseline. The Eurostoxx is 23% 
below the baseline by end-2027 (10% down on the year). IG and HY spreads are 115bp 
and 375bp wider, respectively, by mid-2028.
US modelling. We used the full set of FRB/US financial-conditions channels for equities 
and credit. We usually trim FRB/US coefficients because the economy is less sensitive to 
interest rates today, but we did not fully trim the equity channel. Equities now make up 
almost 40% of household net worth, roughly double the 20% the model assumes. We 
did not, however, push consumption all the way down to the level the model's wealth-
to-income relationship implies.The result is a mild US recession. Consumer spending 
slows and investment weakens, most notably in residential and non-residential 
structures. We have not included the further downside from a likely fall in national 
house prices, which are already declining in real terms.
US Policy response. In 2027 policy rates run about 1pp above the baseline, as resilient 
growth pushes unemployment lower and inflation higher. As the economy turns, we 
expect the FOMC to cut sharply, by more than a standard policy rule would imply, to 
offset the drag from a higher term premium further out the curve. That is consistent with 
recent FOMC comments. Fed funds bottom at 1%. Even with unemployment climbing 
There is meaningfully more central 
bank activity if oil prices go higher 
than if they go lower.

to 5.6% by end-2028, we think a Warsh-led Fed would be reluctant to go all the way to 
the zero lower bound. Core inflation also limits how far it can go: it falls from above 3% 
to 2.3% by end-2028, but that is still above target. Fiscal policy offers little help either. 
Our modelling suggests 6.5% yields would add about 0.5pp to the federal deficit, 
taking it to around 7% of GDP.

Rest of the world. The pattern is similar elsewhere, with the biggest hit in 2028. After 
the US, the most affected economies are Canada, Japan, Turkey, South Africa and 
Mexico. China, Malaysia and Thailand are among the least affected. Europe sits in 
between, with growth 100bp below the baseline in 2028, for two reasons. First, its 
financing is more bank-based, which partly insulates it from bond yields. Second, equity 
wealth effects on consumption are much weaker there. 
Inflation. Inflation runs flat to slightly above the baseline. The 2027 resilience that 
drives yields higher slows disinflation from this year's inflation shock, with marginal 
excess demand for energy and chip shortages from the strong AI capex cycle. The 2028 
downturn comes too late to reverse that within our horizon. This is mainly a developed-
market story, but it also applies to Brazil and Mexico, where currency weakness triggered 
by the market correction adds to inflation.
Policy response elsewhere. Central banks start from a weak position: they are still 
dealing with an inflation shock, which limits how quickly they can respond once high 
bond yields start to do damage. The Bank of Canada faces a growth and unemployment 
mix as bad as the US and follows the Fed in cutting aggressively. Its policy rate ends 
325bp below our baseline by end-2028, at 0.25%, effectively the zero lower bound. 
The BoJ reverses most of its hikes and moves back to 0.25%, but its cumulative easing is 
smaller because it starts from a lower rate. We think the ECB and BoE both cut to slightly 
below neutral, at 1.5% and 2.8%, respectively. Only two central banks end above the 
baseline by end-2028. First, the RBA, which initially hikes above our baseline in 2027 as 
fundamentals appear to improve but then reverses that to land a touch above current 
levels when Australia meets the global headwinds in 2028. Second, the Banco Central 
do Brasil, because financial-conditions shocks tend to bring a weaker currency and a 
more cautious central bank.
Scenario 4: AI disappointment
Global growth depends to an unusual extent on the US capex cycle to build out AI 
infrastructure. At over 3% of US GDP this year and over 5% next year, AI investment is 
driving most of the growth in global trade, most of US growth (and all of its investment 
growth), and a good part of the rise in global equity valuations. That dependence on a 
single engine is also the global economy's greatest vulnerability. The gap between AI-
related revenue and the investment being made is so large that it leaves little room for 
disappointment. Revenue would not need to fall to shake confidence that firms can 
monetize their investment. A slowdown in its pace of growth, the second derivative, 
could be enough. 
 With unemployment climbing to 
5.6% we would see the FF rate at 
1%.
In this scenario, most easing comes 
from the US, Canada and the BoJ, 
with the ECB, BoE lagging. RBA and 
BCB end up above our baseline.

Global Economics & Markets Outlook 9 November 2026
ab 87
Because the technology is moving so fast, there are many routes to disappointment:

Cheaper models. Cheaper, less energy-intensive models are catching up with the 
most expensive frontier ones and taking market share from closed-source models. 
Given how leveraged the AI ecosystem is to the frontier labs, that is a risk.

On-device compute. A large share of future compute could run on local devices 
rather than in the huge data centers now being built.

Local data centers. Data centers could become increasingly local, because 
inference needs to sit geographically close to users. It is then unclear how much of 
that workload, particularly for open-source models, would run on hyperscaler 
platforms.
Things could also go right. Adoption is still very low, and we are getting ever more 
compute out of each chip, so future capex could fall sharply relative to revenue. For this 
scenario, however, we assume these risks materialize and trigger a large equity 
correction.
US impact. Equipment investment contracts and falling equity valuations deliver a 
material negative wealth shock. With household equity wealth around 230% of GDP, a 
25% correction [vs baseline] cuts that wealth by about 57% of GDP. Even with a very 
low propensity to consume out of wealth, say 2%, that alone takes over 1pp of GDP off 
consumption. Consumption realigns more closely with income, and the saving rate rises. 
We also remove the positive productivity shock in our baseline, which fails to materialize 
as investment is scaled back.
Losing that productivity boost removes a disinflationary force, because higher 
productivity growth keeps unit labor costs down. Even so, unemployment rises rapidly 
and the economy disinflates. The FOMC therefore cuts quickly to the zero lower bound. 
That is further than in our bond-yield scenario, because here inflation falls faster and the 
labor market weakens more sharply. Fiscal space remains limited beyond the automatic 
stabilizers. We would expect tax rebates and extended unemployment benefits similar to 
past recessions, but well short of Covid-era relief. We do not expect the FOMC to restart 
QE within the forecast horizon, and we assume no MBS purchases.
Global growth impact. This is our most damaging  scenario (Figure 221Global GDP - deviation from
 baseline). The peak 
growth drag is similar to the bond-yield scenario, but the shock is front-loaded, so the 
cumulative loss is larger: global growth over 2027–28 is about 2.1pp lower, against 
1.4pp in the bond-yield scenario. The US loses its two main sources of growth, AI capex 
and equity wealth. US growth runs about 2pp a year below the baseline, close to zero in 
both 2027 and 2028. Most of the decline happens in 2027. The 2028 annual figure is 
held down largely by statistical carry-over. 
Twelve economies record two or more consecutive quarters of contraction: the US, 
Canada, Japan, Switzerland, the Eurozone, the UK, Korea, Taiwan, Singapore, Hong 
Kong, Brazil and Mexico. Taiwan is hit hardest. Its growth falls 9pp below our baseline in 
2027 and nearly 8pp below in 2028, to -0.7% and -1.9% y/y. Other parts of the AI 
supply chain also see large declines. Korea's growth is 3.4pp below the baseline in 2027 
and 3.1pp below in 2028. Malaysia  and Mexico are also hit hard, Mexico largely as a 
transshipment hub, though with some value-added impact. Europe and EM economies 
outside the AI supply chain see smaller declines. 
Labor market and inflation. Global unemployment is 0.6pp above the baseline in 
2027 and 0.7pp above in 2028, reaching 5.6% and 5.8%. The rise largely tracks the 
growth hit, but it is particularly steep in the US and in Canada, which is exposed partly 
through the US. Headline and core inflation are both about 20bp below the baseline on 
average over 2027-28. That is a modest decline for a recession, for two reasons. Inflation 
responds to labor-market slack with a lag. And losing the productivity boost raises unit 
labor costs, which partly offsets the disinflation from a wider output gap. 
Policy impact. Policy rates respond more to the large growth declines than the more 
modest inflation declines. This is a recession scenario and in the US, Canada, Japan, 
Switzerland, and HK policy rates move to the zero lower bound. Compared to the 2027 
baseline, the largest cuts are in the US (425bp), Canada (325bp), Japan (200bp), Korea 
(175bp), HK (275bp) and Mexico (150bp). Only Brazil's rates rise in response to the 
weakness in financial markets. F

Global Economics & Markets Outlook
UBS Research
Scenarios for markets
Oil at $150/bbl
This scenario has the potential to create both a margin shock and an evaluation shock in 
the markets. A traditional playbook to an oil supply shock is one of dollar strength, 
flatter curves, wider spreads and significant underperformance of banks and consumer 
sectors amidst falling equities. This should largely play out, as should underperformance 
by Europe and EM risk assets. However, with equity valuations still well in excess of long-
term averages and allocations to equities close to 25-year highs, the downside in stocks 
could be much higher than traditional models would suggest. With central banks keen 
to prevent second-round effects of higher energy prices, rates could rise sharply, if 
temporarily. The Momentum factor could be a major casualty in addition to the 
traditional rates-sensitive sectors. As the semiconductors trade has become the 
dominant industry picked by Momentum, this liquidity shock is the channel through 
which higher energy costs are transmitted to the AI trade.
Oil at $60/bbl
Our base case is Brent averaging $80/bbl in 2027 and TTF gas €45/MWh. A sharp decline 
could revive consumption and broaden investment beyond Tech, benefiting Small Caps 
and global equities relative to US Large Caps and Tech. This is a larger risk to our 
recommendations than higher energy prices. We do not presently believe in a major 
rotation away from AI, but in this scenario, US-centric large caps should underperform 
small caps and global stocks, even if they do well in absolute terms. Consumer and 
Financials would benefit most from a reversal in oil. Lower allocations to US equities in 
favour of Europe and emerging market equities could lead to a notably weaker dollar. 
Central banks would be able to bring rates significantly lower, leading to strong 
performance of the belly and front end. Curves should steepen but the long run should 
perform well in absolute terms.
Bond yields at 6.5%
We see this scenario as potentially creating a more sustained hit to equity markets 
through a US recession. Interest rate-sensitive sectors are already suffering in the 
economy and the markets, but a liquidity shock of this magnitude would hurt the 
momentum and AI trades as well, leading to a big drawdown in equities. The recession 
should have implications globally that might last longer but be less acute than the 
scenario in which oil prices temporarily rise to $150 per barrel. While it would be fiscal 
fears that contribute to bond yields staying at such high levels, and these fears would 
undermine the dollar, a US recession has typically created global risk aversion and hurt 
global currencies more. We expect a very sharp underperformance of EM currencies 
relative to higher-rated developed market currencies and gold in this scenario.
AI disappointment
Given how imbalanced the US economy and US stock market are, disappointment in AI 
is by far the worst-case scenario for assets. This would lead to a sooner and deeper 
recession in the US and likely take global equities into a bear market. Europe would likely 
outperform the US and emerging markets, but would not be able to avoid a bear market 
itself. Disappointment in AI should create serious strains in the credit market as well, 
with high yield spreads widening close to 700 basis points. Although volatility would rise 
significantly, the single biggest flow keeping the dollar strong, the flow into US equities 
from abroad, would likely reverse. This would mean that this scenario is the most bearish 
for the dollar, with euro dollar likely to rise towards 120 and gold rise towards $7,000. 
Gold would also be helped by aggressive rate cuts from the Fed, which should be 
enough to take 10-year bond yields towards 2.5%, a rise in term premium 
notwithstanding.

USA

AI capex momentum continues to underpin the expansion in 2027.

Inflation expected to fall, but not in the near-term keeping rate hikes in play.

We expect a 0.25 pp rate hike in December and another in January (or Q1).
Resilient...underpinned by AI
Resilient. There is  no better word for the US expansion. Not too hot and not too cold. 
Real GDP growth was 2.0% in 2025, 2.1% over the four quarters ending in Q3, and we 
estimate on track for 2.5% in 2026.  Roughly 2¼% real GDP growth over the past two 
years is not super strong,  close to our projections of  an accelerating trend in structural 
growth as AI productivity gains take hold. The unemployment rate has oscillated 
between  4.0% to 4.5% the last two years, enough to slow nominal wage inflation.
We expect another strong year of tech capex next year.  That has powerful spillovers too, 
including the related wealth and the demand impulse supporting other investment. But 
there are offsets. We import much of that capex, so net exports should remain a 
significant  drag on growth. Plus, the federal government reopening added 0.2 pp to 
2026 Q4/Q4 real GDP growth. That won't be repeated. The recent rise in interest rates is 
already weighing on  housing indicators. Importantly, fiscal policy flips to a drag in 2027 
with no new One Big Beautiful Bill Act. Tax refunds  for households should be lower in 
2027 than 2026, and federal cuts should weigh on the fiscal impulse next year. We 
expect that more than offsets falling gas prices.

Consumer powering forward on equity market wealth
 Real personal consumption expenditures grew 2.5% over  the year ending in September, 
while real wage growth was negative and real disposable income grew less than 1.0%. 
Why has consumption so outpaced income? Rising equity market wealth.  Essentially all 
the post-pandemic gains in real wealth have been due to rising equity markets alongside 
AI-related  optimism. Equity market wealth is now almost 40% of household net worth. 
Our equity market analysts expect further gains ahead. We also expect headline inflation 
to fall, a tailwind for real income. But, rates are higher and one big impulse flips.  
The US economy continues to be 
resilient.
A number of factors suggest 
somewhat slower US growth in 2027.
Upside risks? If the AI momentum 
becomes a rising tide lifting all boats, 
including broadening real income 
gains for households. 
Downside risks? If the AI buildout 
stumbles and that spills over into 
lower equity market wealth.
We expect two more 0.25 pp rate 
hikes, then a pause. If inflation fails 
to improve during the pause, one risk 
is the FOMC resumes raising rates 
later in the year. 
We use a growth accounting 
framework to try to understand and 
estimate the US economic potential. 
We expect an AI boost to come and 
less drag from population aging.
Equity market wealth has risen to 
almost 40% of household net worth 
and is expected to rise further in 
2027, that wealth maintaining 
resilient consumer spending growth.

In 2026, OBBBA cut taxes, including on tips, overtime and for seniors, plus it literally sent 
checks to households. A ~$60 billion  boost to  tax refunds was sent to households, 
rebating 2025 taxes paid in 2026. That income falls in 2027.  Higher interest rates mean 
higher rates on credit cards, car loans and home equity loans, for example. After 
projecting 2.6% growth in real personal consumption expenditures in 2026, we expect 
some slowing to 2.2% in 2027, before reaccelerating to 2.4% growth in 2028,  roughly 
following the contour of the fiscal impulse and interest rates.
Housing activity likely to remain under pressure
Residential investment has declined in five of the last six quarters because of higher 
financing costs that just went higher. Builders hold elevated inventories: there are 
approximately 1.4 new homes for sale for every 1,000 people in the US, a level unseen 
since 2008 and the mid- to late-1980s before that. Inventories of existing homes have 
normalized from post-pandemic lows. The forward looking indicators like single-family 
permits, mortgage applications for a home purchase, and pending home sales all point 
to weakening ahead. The recent backup in mortgage rates suggests declines in 
residential investment for the next few quarters, likely also weighing on remodeling, and 
related sales and brokers' commissions.
AI-related capex  to power growth
Business fixed investment contributed 1.5 pp on average to  real GDP growth (saar) thus 
far in 2026, powered by the AI build out.  Hyperscalers appear set to spend ~$1.8 trillion 
in 2027, up from ~$1 trillion in 2026. Surprisingly, in growth rates, that  implies  slower 
nominal growth in 2027 than 2026, but we assume  less slowing in real terms in 2027 
Housing activity remains under  
pressure from mortgage rates, which 
should weigh on 2027 growth.

Global Economics & Markets Outlook 9 November 2026
ab 104
and  the slack gets picked up elsewhere. We estimate a multiplier of 1.5 on that AI-
investment, creating demand for wiring, generators, and air conditioners, for example, 
supporting broader investment. We expect equipment investment to increase strongly 
again, rising 15.3%  in 2027. We also expect investment in intellectual property products 
to increase 5.6%, supported by  software investment. Structures investment has 
resumed expanding (thanks partly to data centers), and research and development has 
been helped by the OBBBA business tax incentives (that help likely wanes from here). 
We expect  the 2027 growth  of BFI to remain unambiguously strong,  up 9.1% in 2027 
after rising 10.0% in 2026, but investment is a growth rate and the base is rising rapidly. 
Consequently, we see capital investment's contribution to growth remaining elevated in 
2027, but not driving acceleration. Even though the impulse is mostly AI, we expect the 
capex wave continues in 2027 before slowing more noticeably in 2028. In 2029, we 
expect that the  high level of real investment may be hard to sustain.

Government to flip from a support, to a headwind
Real government spending has risen  3.3% annualized so far in 2026, boosted by 
reopening after the 2025 government shutdown. In 2027,  fiscal support flips  to a 
headwind. We estimate the fiscal contribution to growth swings from ~+0.5 pp in 2026 
to  ~-0.2 pp in 2027 on a Q4/Q4 basis. Plus, state and local budgets have been under 
pressure, partly payback from pandemic expansion and  federal cuts. Medicaid work 
requirements  take effect on January 1, creating an estimated  ~$11 billion headwind. 
Other cuts and state cost sharing are coming too.  The help from  the tax cuts fades and 
OBBBA's  ~$60 billion boost to 2026 tax refunds gives way to lower refunds  in 2027.

Net exports: Imports to weigh on growth, trade policy in play
A significant share of the AI build out is  imported. Just as we expect tech investment 
growth  to continue in 2027, so too should the drag from net exports.  The demand for AI 
is creating production and income in Asia, the US exporting that domestic demand.  We 
are currently tracking a -1.2 pp contribution to real GDP from net exports in 2026, and 
project a -0.8 pp contribution to Q4/Q4 growth in 2027.
Trade  policy remains in play next year, even with divided government.  The USMCA 
remains in force, as before, but discussions continue with the shift to annual reviews. 
The bilateral trade truce with China is currently set to expire on January 10, 2027 
(otherwise facing a quadrupling of the tariff rate imposed on Chinese goods). 
Alternatively, we could see tariff relief. On October 23, 2026, the Administration 
announced modifications to Section 338 tariffs on Canadian goods, altering the 
distribution of tariffs, though the weighted average tariff rate remained at 11.7%.

ab 106
Inflation: relief coming, but not until the spring 
Amid shocks from tariffs, a war, and strong demand for semiconductors, inflation has 
been on the rise. Headline CPI inflation  jumped from 2.3% in April 2025 and 2.4% in 
February 2026 to a peak of 4.25% in May 2026 driven by soaring gasoline prices before 
declining to 3.74% currently. Price increases for items other than gasoline have risen 
too, but overall  core inflation is  mixed. Core CPI inflation is down 0.4 pp since 
September of last year to 2.6% in its most recent reading. In contrast, core PCE price 
inflation has risen 0.3 pp since September last year to its current level of 3.1%.

Source: Bloomberg, UBS
Other measures of underlying inflation trends tend to show inflation that has generally 
continued to decline over the past year but remains above the FOMC’s 2.0% PCE price 
inflation target. The persistence of  above-target inflation depends heavily on the 
progress of the shocks hitting the economy. Tariff levels remain elevated, and their 
impact on inflation should gradually wane over the next two years unless tariffs increase 
again. The impact of crude oil prices should also wane with headline inflation slowing 
sharply in the March, April, and May data next year as last spring's  large  gasoline price 
increases are lapped. That said, a renewed rise in crude oil prices could put that easing in 
jeopardy. The inflationary push from AI adoption  and rising semiconductor prices is also 
likely to wane over the next couple of years as those investments  boost productivity, 
pushing  down inflation. In the near-term, however, that risk tilts to the upside as  rising 
semiconductor prices could radiate out from computers and mobile phones to 
televisions, appliances, and vehicles.
Overall, until the spring of 2027 we expect little slowing in inflation, and first some 
uptick. The downward bias to last year’s October and November inflation data, a result 
of the government shutdown, will dissipate, leading to an upward bias, pushing up 12-
month changes for housing rents that will persist until next April’s data is released. That 
said, the underlying trend for housing rents remains downward for close to another year 
as new lease rent increases remain 1½ pp or more below their pre-pandemic pace. Next 
year will likely be the  sixth year in a row of above target inflation, but downward 
progress should be made starting in the spring. Core PCE price inflation is projected to 
decline to 2.5% by the end of next year with headline PCE price inflation at 2.1% (both 
post-pandemic lows). Absent more shocks the downtrend should continue with 
headline PCE price inflation hitting 2.0% in early 2028 and core reaching target in early 
2029. 
Labor market: productivity disappointment to turn upward?
In our first projection of the year, on January 3, "We expect a bumpy 2026" we 
projected nonfarm payroll employment would average 68K jobs per month in 2026, the 
labor force participation rate would end the year at 62.1%, and productivity growth 
would be 2.0%. As of this writing, NFP has averaged 86K jobs per month this year, as H1 
productivity growth disappointed, and the labor force participation rate sits at 61.9%, 
weighing on the unemployment rate more than expected. Productivity growth has run 
at 2.1% over the last four quarters. This is still low versus a typical over 2-1/2% pace in a 
high productivity regime.

Global Economics & Markets Outlook 9 November 2026
ab 107
In 2027, we expect slower job gains on the back of slower real GDP growth. Amidst 
ongoing slow labor supply growth, that should put a little upward pressure on the 
unemployment rate. We also expect productivity growth to accelerate structurally in the 
coming years, which  should temper the need for more jobs  overall. In theory the regime 
shift higher in structural productivity  we expect should imply higher growth and real 
wages, but the timing  may be difficult for workers. What does that mean? Rising 
productivity,  low unit labor cost growth, and low bargaining power for workers  should 
prevent the labor market from being a source of inflationary pressure.
A new FOMC regime under Chairman Warsh
Chairman Warsh now holds the reins.  The message since Jackson Hole has been 
inflation is not moving with sufficient speed (or at all) toward the Committee's 2.0% 
objective. At the September FOMC meeting, 18 of the 19 FOMC participants thought at 
most  another  two rate hikes would be needed. We expect two more rate hikes, which 
seems consistent with recent communications. Including the September rate hike,  that 
three rate hike recalibration would put the target range for the federal funds rate at 
4.25% to 4.50%. After that, we expect the FOMC to then pause and evaluate.
We expect that pause to extend into 2028 but a few things need to happen. Inflation 
needs to improve before the FOMC's patience wears thin. Inflation from domestic 
pressures, like the labor market, needs to remain quiescent. A new negative supply 
shock also needs to be avoided, like supply-chain disruptions, or the intensification of 
chip prices or the oil shock. However, if inflation remains stuck at 3.0% after six months 
of waiting, unless downside risks  emerge, the FOMC may initiate another sequence of 
rate hikes. Also, if inflation expectations rise sufficiently or domestic pressures  like 
nominal wage growth rise to a worrisome degree, the FOMC might hike then too.
We assume over the course of 2027, the median FOMC assessment of the longer run 
rate rises to between 3.25% and 3.50%. We expect recommendations out of the task 
forces lead to little fundamental change. Some long run alterations to payment systems 
or other changes could reduce demand for reserves, but we expect the "ample reserves" 
regime to remain. If there is a change to the SEP, we expect the first thing to go would be 
the submissions for the longer-run dot. Of the meeting calendar changes, we expect the 
October 2027 meeting is set aside for discussion rather than potential action.
We expect Chairman Warsh to stick to his 'no forward guidance', but other board 
members could provide useful messages at times, such as Vice Chair Philip Jefferson. His 
term as Vice Chair expires in September 2027, though he could remain as a Governor. 
We expect Governor Powell to step down in 2027 too. More changeover could come as 
we move through 2028, including the retirement of FRB of New York President Williams. 
Typically new faces rotate through the participants and we expect that  in 2027. We still 
await the naming of the next FRB of Atlanta President, for example.

Canada

We expect the Canadian economy continues to improve with a broadening in 
the drivers of growth as fiscal policy supports investment with confidence 
improving for consumers and businesses. 

Headline inflation should moderate in 2027 but if the economy improves we 
think slack could be eaten up through 2027 leading to stronger core inflation. 

We think the Bank of Canada will deliver a series of three hikes to leave the 
policy rate at 3.0% by Q1 2027 and then wait and see. Into 2028, they could 
further adjust policy as slack is eaten up. 
A sustainable recovery?
Canada’s recovery should broaden in 2027–28, but improving demand will meet a 
slower-growing supply side. We forecast real GDP growth of 1.9% in 2027 and 2.2% in 
2028, following 1.0% in 2026. Resilient consumption, a gradual investment recovery 
and expanding exports should support activity, even as fiscal support fades and trade 
uncertainty persists. Headline inflation should ease to an annual average of 2.1% in 
both years, from 2.8% in 2026. Nevertheless, elevated inflation expectations and 
declining spare capacity should keep policy focused on inflation risks. We forecast the 
overnight rate  at 3.0% at end-2027 and 3.50% at end-2028.
Growth: a broader, measured recovery
The improvement in consumption, investment and exports through mid-2026 offers a 
firmer starting point, although we do not extrapolate these gains. Private consumption 
grows 1.9% in 2027 and 2.1% in 2028 in our forecast. Lower headline inflation and 
gradual labor-market improvement should ease pressure on real incomes and support 
confidence. However, the one-time household credit top-up seen this summer is not a 
lasting growth driver. Mortgage resets, further policy tightening and slower population 
growth will constrain aggregate spending. 
Investment should recover gradually from a weak base, with gross fixed capital 
formation expanding 0.9% in 2027 and 1.4% in 2028. Technology spending, supply-
chain adaptation and investment tax incentives should support capital expenditure, 
while higher energy prices provide some support for energy-sector investment. Housing 
remains uneven, with slower immigration and higher financing costs limiting the 
recovery. Fiscal initiatives support activity but their incremental impulse should fade: 
government consumption growth slows to 1.1% in 2027 and 1.0% in 2028. Tariff-
response assistance can cushion disruption, but fiscal support in the form of loans and 
multi-year public-private projects do not translate into an immediate spending boost.

Labor market: demand improves, supply slows
September employment fell 68K, leaving the four-month average change at -4K, while 
unemployment rose to 6.5%. Wage growth of 2.3% year-over-year leaves a lower risk 
of labor market driven inflation pressures. We expect broader growth to improve labor 
demand gradually, with unemployment averaging 6.2% in 2027 and 5.9% in 2028, 
down from 6.5% in 2026. But slower immigration and population aging are integral to 
this decline. A lower unemployment rate will not necessarily signal a strong hiring cycle 
when labor-force growth is also slowing.

the horizon than the headline pace alone suggests. Renewed employment weakness

Inflation: energy relief, persistence risks
Our inflation outlook is more benign than the BoC’s, particularly for 2027. We forecast 
headline inflation averaging 2.1% in both 2027 and 2028, with year-end rates of 2.1% 
and 2.2%, respectively. The BoC’s latest projection instead has a 3.1% annual-average 
rate in 2027. We expect the energy-driven headline overshoot to fade with smaller 
spillovers into underlying prices. September’s divergence supports that view: headline 
inflation was 3.1%, but CPI excluding food and energy was 2.1%, while the weighted-
median and trimmed-mean measures were 1.9% and 1.8%. The current pressure 
appears concentrated rather than generalized.
The principal risk is that prolonged energy-cost pressure passes more broadly into prices 
and expectations. Consumer five-year inflation expectations reached 3.9% in Q3, and 
firms reported greater uncertainty about a return to target inflation. Businesses can 
absorb input costs temporarily, but sustained pressure increases the likelihood of pass-
through. Retaliatory tariffs or currency depreciation would add to inflation risks, while 
declining spare capacity could support underlying pressure later. Conversely, weak wage 
growth, weak employment and limited inflation breadth would reinforce our 
expectation of a smaller spillover.

Policy: further tightening, conditional on persistence
The October 25 bp hike to 2.5% marked a shift toward managing inflation-persistence 
risks. Monetary policy cannot reverse an energy supply shock, but the BoC aims to limit 
its spillover into other prices and expectations. We forecast another 25 bp increase to 
2.75% at the December meeting, followed by 25 bp to 3.0% at the January meeting 
before hold through the remainder of 2027 to reassess after the recalibration. As 
demand strengthens into 2028, we would expect further tightening from the BoC with 
slack being eaten up.
This path remains highly conditional. If the BoC’s more persistent inflation outlook 
materializes, a faster or longer tightening cycle would be plausible. If core pressure stays 
contained and employment weakens further, there would be greater room to pause. 
Trade disruption could complicate that choice by simultaneously weakening demand 
and raising costs. Overall, our baseline is for broader growth, lower headline inflation 
and gradual labor-market rebalancing. The central policy challenge will shift from 
containing energy spillovers in 2027 toward assessing whether the economy’s slower-
growing supply side can accommodate improving demand in 2028. 
Inflation’s composition, not just the

Global Economics & Markets Outlook 9 November 2026

Global Economics & Markets Outlook
UBS Research
Latin America

We expect the regional economy to lose momentum in 2027 on account of 
Brazil, but other countries should witness economic rebounds.

Fiscal adjustment cannot be postponed in Brazil and Colombia.
Continued economic softness despite some important tailwinds
Despite having to deal with some external shocks (higher oil prices and disruption to 
global supply chains on account of the Middle East conflict, US tariffs), the region has 
enjoyed some notable tailwinds this year, including more favourable terms of trade, 
strong demand for key commodities and minerals, a dynamic US economy lifted by AI-
investment, and geopolitical diversification away from global hotspots. Nevertheless, at 
an estimated 1.6%, regional growth ex-Venezuela has disappointed relative to our 
original forecast (2.0%). Moreover, it has continued to underperform global growth 
despite the surge in commodity prices, a condition that historically was associated with 
outperformance. Interestingly, the undershooting of our regional growth forecast was 
not driven by the two largest economies – Mexico is poised to perform a little better than 
we had expected this time last year, Brazil a little worse – but rather by Argentina and 
Chile, two countries that despite the implementation of (or the promise to implement) 
more orthodox economic policies, didn't deliver a broad-based, investment-led recovery.
Figure 278: Latin America Regional GDP Growth vs world 
differential (%)
Latin America Regional GDP Growth vs world differential (%)
Source: Haver, IMF, UBS estimates
Figure 279: Latin America Growth (%)
Latin America Growth (%)
Source: Haver, UBS
Brazil should drag regional growth lower in 2027, but others to rebound
For 2027, we are expecting the region to reduce its growth to 1.4%. This is largely on 
account of the fact that we expect Brazil to slow sharply, delivering flat growth in 2027: 
the economy was already in a mid-cycle slowdown, and that could now accelerate and 
leave it teetering on the edge of recession largely on account of a sizable retrenchment 
in investment – reflecting fiscal uncertainty – and a slowdown in government 
consumption. To a similar extent, high real interest rates and fiscal consolidation should 
take a toll on domestic demand in Colombia, with a rebound in growth only likely in 
2028 if the government’s reform agenda takes flight. 
Elsewhere in the region, we are more upbeat on economic growth. We think Mexico is 
likely to maintain its recent momentum, driven by the strength of electronic exports – 
tied to the rollout of data centers in the US – by a budding recovery in investment, with 
greater cooperation between the private and public sectors, and by clearer rules of 
engagement with the US on trade. In Chile, the economy appears poised for a rebound, 
coming off this year’s expected contraction and with upside potential amid a large 
investment pipeline that has broadened well beyond copper. We expect Peru to remain 
the best performing economy in the region next year, supported by strong mining and 
non-mining investment, potential post-El Niño reconstruction, and rising real wages. 
Argentina, meanwhile, may post a small acceleration in growth next year as some of its 
micro-reforms start bearing fruit and credit recovers, but it will likely remain a two-speed 
economy, with booming primary sectors and more sluggish labour-intensive sectors in 
services and manufacturing.
