Global Economics & Markets Outlook 9 November 2026
ab 115
Fiscal adjustment cannot be postponed in some countries
A number of countries may face a fiscal reckoning in 2027. Colombia and Brazil fall into 
that category, with their nominal deficits approaching double digits as a percentage of 
GDP at present. Under new President de la Espriella, Colombia has approved a 2027 
budget that lays bare the challenges ahead (a primary deficit without adjustment of 
4.5% of GDP, a nominal deficit of 9.4%) and the government has now published a 
Rescue Bill that lays out the main drivers of the proposed adjustment, targeting 2.4% of 
GDP in consolidation for next year. Congressional negotiations are likely to dilute the 
package, but the government retains some room to contain budget execution by 
freezing or postponing expenditures. On balance, we see an adjustment of around 1% 
of GDP as feasible in 2027, below the government’s proposed consolidation but 
sufficient to mark the start of a gradual fiscal correction. In Brazil, meanwhile, we think 
President Lula will have no option but to deliver a fiscal adjustment in his fourth term in 
office. However, our base scenario is for a modest adjustment that takes the primary 
deficit from 0.6% of GDP at present to balance later in his new term, with no 
meaningful improvement next year (the government has already committed to new 
expenditure and the minimum wage adjustment is already set). Notably, this fiscal 
adjustment would not stabilize the debt. Absent much greater pressure  on BRL, we do 
not currently sense strong conviction on the part of the authorities that a stronger 
adjustment is  necessary.
Figure 280: Primary Balance (4q sum, % of GDP)
Primary Balance (4q sum, % of GDP)
Source: Haver, UBS
Figure 281: Public Debt (% of GDP)
Public Debt (% of GDP)
Source: Haver, UBS
Outside of these two countries, the market has started to take notice of the gradual 
deterioration in Mexico's debt-to-GDP metrics and is questioning whether its investment 
grade could be at risk. We think this risk is not imminent, but Mexico may have to 
accelerate its fiscal consolidation after the midterm elections next year to avoid it. In 
Argentina, the government remains steadfast in its commitment to a zero fiscal balance, 
but this could prove more challenging next year with budgeted inflation closer to 
consensus, reducing the effectiveness of the inflation tax and likely putting more 
pressure on further discretionary spending cuts to hit the government’s fiscal targets.
El Niño, oil prices, and sticky services all a challenge to inflation in 2027
On the inflation front, countries in the region should continue to be buffeted by external 
shocks, including the risk of higher oil prices for longer and the effects from El Niño. On 
the former, countries’ ability to cushion the hit through fuel subsidies depends on their 
fiscal room to do so, ranging from Mexico (which can absorb the cost of stabilizing 
prices at the pump) to Chile (which transfers the costs to consumers, albeit slowly). On El 
Niño, which is expected to be “strong” to “very strong” in Q1’27, according to NOAA, 
the inflation impact will come mainly through perishable foods, with Colombia and Peru 
particularly vulnerable, albeit for different reasons (Colombia through droughts, Peru 
though excess rainfall). Under a strong El Niño, inflation could end up some 160 bps/130 
bps higher than in our base case in Colombia and Peru, respectively, with peak impact by 
late 1Q'27. The impact on the rest of the region is likely to be more muted.
Colombia and Brazil could face a 
fiscal reckoning next year.  The 
Colombian authorities appear more 
alert to the challenges. 
 A number of external shocks pose 
upside risks to inflation next year...

Figure 282: Headline 12m inflation
Headline 12m inflation
Source: Haver, UBS
Figure 283: Headline CPI ex food and energy (12m)
Headline CPI ex food and energy (12m)
Source: Haver, UBS
Beyond these external shocks, core inflation in the region remains elevated. Core goods 
inflation has improved in recent months on the back of post-Liberation day tariff 
normalization and cheaper Chinese imports in key sectors like autos. But services 
inflation remains well above target in all countries in the region, reflecting wage 
pressures, higher fuel prices and ongoing relative price adjustments, but also general 
downward stickiness in this component of CPI.
Monetary outlook dependent on external conditions, growth, and fiscal 
consolidation 
A complex outlook for inflation and expected Fed tightening should lead countries with 
strong growth, or witnessing a rebound, such as Chile and Peru, to increase interest 
rates next year. A dovish Central Bank in Mexico is likely to divorce itself from the Fed, 
keeping interest rates on hold through our forecast period and even delivering some 
limited cuts if the peso does not depreciate excessively. For Colombia and Brazil, the two 
countries with the highest benchmark interest rates at present (12.25% and 13.75%), 
the ability to reduce rates will depend not only on the behaviour of inflation, but also on 
the credibility of the fiscal plans they present, and on their ability to implement them. 
Our base scenario assumes that Colombia will be able to reduce nominal interest rates 
by 100bps. In the case of Brazil, a moderate fiscal effort would allow some room to cut 
interest rates by some 250bps next year provided the Real remains in the 5.50 region 
against USD, a plausible scenario in light of positive terms of trade and Brazil's high 
carry. In the event of no fiscal effort, unlikely in our view, we think Brazil high debt-to-
GDP level (84% at present) would leave it on the verge of fiscal dominance, a situation 
whereby the BCB’s inflation fighting mandate would be subservient to the Treasury’s 
need for a lower interest burden. Put differently, higher inflation would be required to 
stabilize the debt. 
External accounts remain supportive
Despite the fiscal and monetary challenges facing the region, the one positive light in the 
region is that the external accounts look healthy throughout our forecast period. The 
region appears well-placed to satisfy the increased investment in AI, either through the 
supply of critical minerals such as copper, or by inserting itself in global supply chains, as 
we are seeing in Mexico. Terms of trade are likely to remain positive for the region given 
the positive outlook for commodity prices. And a number of countries should see an 
improvement in FDI flows given the opening of their economies. Healthy external 
accounts should continue to provide an important buffer for regional currencies, 
although we expect FX performance to remain differentiated. High carry and supportive 
external fundamentals should provide some cushion to BRL and COP, while MXN’s 
recent repricing has improved its valuation support. By contrast, weaker domestic 
fundamentals and limited carry could leave CLP more vulnerable, particularly if elevated 
oil prices persist and growth fails to recover.

A lighter political calendar
2027-28 will likely prove less momentous from a political perspective than 2026, with 
only Argentina scheduled to hold presidential elections. Given sub-40% approval 
ratings for President Milei at present, the outcome of next year’s election is not a 
foregone conclusion, increasing the likelihood of a surge in household demand for 
dollars that could put upward pressure on interest rates and on the peso, and might 
complicate meeting the government’s large external obligations coming due next year. 
Apart from having accumulated reserves this year, the authorities will also have access to 
the Chinese and US Treasury swaps if need be. Mexico will hold mid-term elections in 
June 2027 to appoint a new Congress and several governorships, testing President 
Sheinbaum’s and Morena’s ability to continue mustering the qualified congressional 
majority and large regional footprint they currently enjoy. Elsewhere, Venezuela’s path 
and schedule towards democratic elections remains uncertain. More generally, Lula’s 
reelection in Brazil and Morena’s current dominance in Mexico should lay to rest the 
perception of an inexorable right-wing swing in the region.

Global Economics & Markets Outlook 9 November 2026
ab 118
Argentina

Labour-intensive sectors are a drag on the economy, failing to attract 
investment.

An uncertain electoral outlook could complicate the government’s financing 
next year, despite growing reserves and external swaps.
A two-speed economy showcases the challenges of stabilization 
We were expecting the Argentine economy to expand by 5.0% this year, but growth 
now looks set to end 2026 at 1.4%. This result – GDP has expanded by only 4% since 
President Milei took office three years ago – hides a two-speed economy. On the one 
hand, the export-oriented primary sectors are flying: in the first eight months of the year, 
mining (including oil and gas) expanded by 13.2% y-o-y while agriculture grew by 9.2% 
y-o-y. On the other hand, more labour-intensive sectors, such as commerce, public
administration and defence, and manufacturing, were still contracting y-o-y.
This divergence illustrates both the achievements and challenges of Milei’s 
macroeconomic stabilization. The primary export sectors, backed by Vaca Muerta’s oil 
and gas projects, the RIGI long-term investment regime (USD 76bn approved so far, a 
further 133bn under evaluation), high commodity prices, and an excellent harvest, are 
benefitting from the opening of the economy. They generate hard currency and have 
contributed to the exchange rate appreciating in real terms this year (ARS has 
depreciated by 6% so far this year, while inflation has clocked in at 24% in the same 
period). By contrast, labour-intensive sectors are suffering from increased competition, 
lower government spending and subsidies, weak domestic demand, and arguably, an 
overvalued exchange rate. At an aggregate level, the economy has lost formal sector 
jobs, while informality and unemployment have risen.
Can the strength of the primary export sectors ultimately lift the rest of the economy? 
Given that they account for less than 1% of workers, this is unlikely to happen through 
employment, even if related manufacturing and services develop. Instead, their steady 
flow of dollars may generate macroeconomic stability, supporting lower domestic 
interest rates and a recovery in credit. Meanwhile, the government continues to push 
deregulation to allow faster adjustment in labour-intensive sectors. Still, these efforts are 
not generating a surge in non-primary investment, and the government has resorted to 
non-budgetary stimulus measures – such as the indirect use of FGS assets to fund 
mortgages – to rekindle activity. With uncertainty surrounding next year’s presidential 
elections, a surge in investment is unlikely, and we expect consumption to remain soft, 
leaving exports to continue doing the heavy lifting on growth. We expect the economy 
to expand by 2.6% next year.
Milei’s fiscal anchor under strain by weak economic activity
Low economic growth poses a fiscal challenge for a government that remains 
committed to delivering primary surpluses of 1.4% of GDP in 2026 and 1.5% of GDP in 
2027, and nominal balances also in the black. However, through the first nine months of 
2026, the primary surplus contracted by 12.1% in real terms, as a 3.5% real decline in 
total revenue was not offset by a 2.3% real decline in primary expenditure. The 
weakness is concentrated in tax revenue, which fell by 4.3% in real terms, while non-tax 
revenue rose by 7.4%, driven by proceeds from privatizations (+1345.5% in real terms). 
Excluding this one-off component, the decline in the primary surplus would widen to 
19.4% in real terms, while the decline in the overall fiscal surplus would increase from 
31.2% to 54.8%, suggesting that a substantial share of the cumulative result relies on a 
non-recurring source.
In 2025-26, the government partially relied on the inflation tax to deliver a reduction in 
real expenditure. In 2027, this will be more challenging as budgeted inflation (18%) is 
more in line with consensus. Meeting the government’s fiscal targets embedded in the 
2027 budget depends on the government’s optimistic growth target (3%) being 
realized. Otherwise, the authorities will have to rely more heavily on expenditure 
restraint, given the lack of political and fiscal room to restore revenue through tax 
increases or further asset sales. The challenge is that room for discretionary spending 
cuts is increasingly limited, particularly in education, healthcare, and infrastructure. 
The external accounts are taking off some of the pressure at present
A two-speed economy that is 
currently failing to lift all boats
Highly dynamic primary sectors 
attracting investment and generating 
hard currency, but labour-intensive 
sectors going through adjustment 
and losing jobs
One-off privatization receipts mask a 
weaker underlying fiscal position. If 
growth falls short of the 2027 
budget’s 3% assumption, meeting 
primary surplus targets will require 
further spending restraint despite 
increasingly limited room for cuts.

Global Economics & Markets Outlook 9 November 2026
ab 119
While weak activity may be putting the fiscal targets at risk, it is helping the external 
accounts and the recovery of reserves. Export receipts look set to top USD100bn for the 
first time this year, some 15.5% higher than in 2025, while imports look at best flat, 
reflecting the weakness in demand. Our expected trade surplus for the year – USD 30bn 
– is a record high, and ensures a current account surplus of USD1.2bn. On top of next
external debt issuance, it has also allowed the Central Bank to increase its purchase of
reserves to the tune of USD16bn so far this year. Gross reserves have increased by
USD9.4bn to USD50.5bn, while net reserves (as measured by the IMF) have increased to
-USD5bn – still in negative territory.
Meeting the government’s financing needs in an election year a key challenge 
for 2027
Maintaining a large trade surplus in 2027 may prove critical to meeting the 
government’s financing requirements, estimated at some USD25bn. An uncertain 
electoral outlook, Argentines' revealed preference for increased dollar accumulation 
ahead of elections, Fed tightening, the authorities’ unwillingness to validate rates on 
domestic dollar debt maturing post-election, and international debt markets effectively 
closed to Argentina (with spreads versus Treasuries at over 750bps, versus a low closer to 
400bps earlier this year), will focus attention on the government’s ability to tap local 
peso debt markets and purchase dollars from the Central Bank for foreign payments. 
The Central Bank must continue accumulating reserves while satisfying this additional 
demand – potentially over USD15bn by our estimates – without putting undue pressure 
on the exchange rate and inflation. The authorities have hinted at other dollar funding 
sources – such as the US Treasury and Chinese swap lines – to prevent a rerun of the 
financial weakness before the 2025 midterm elections, but it is unclear to what extent 
those lines could be used. Still, the reserve accumulation goal set by the IMF for mid-
2027 (consistent with some USD 4bn in net purchases)  looks difficult to achieve.
Inflation gains from here likely to be gradual
Monthly inflation has failed to fall to below 1% this year as President Milei had targeted, 
but it does appear to have stabilized below 2% in recent months. Inflation is currently 
running at 33.4% on a 12m basis and 23.9% through September. Services inflation 
continues to outpace that of goods on a 12m basis, although the gap has narrowed 
recently because goods inflation has accelerated. Pressure on services continues to 
reflect the ongoing adjustment in regulated prices, with housing, utilities and fuels 
recording the strongest yearly and monthly increases. We expect inflation to continue to 
improve in our forecast period, but the decline to single digit annual inflation is likely to 
prove slow, challenged by inflation inertia. The government’s commitment to a zero 
deficit has eliminated the largest source of inflation in Argentina by preventing the 
monetization of the fiscal deficit. But further improvement in inflation may require that 
demand for real money balances in the economy improves, something that has yet to 
materialize. There is also the risk that electoral uncertainty could put renewed pressure 
on the currency next year, but the authorities have shown an inclination for letting 
interest rates rise to prevent a large depreciation and higher inflation, even if this 
translates into weaker credit and activity growth. We expect inflation to end 2027 at 
21%.
An uncertain electoral outlook
President Milei’s approval ratings have softened to below 40% amid a weaker economy, 
pressure on household incomes and recent political controversies. With the October 
2027 election still some distance away, the electoral landscape remains open. Province 
of Buenos Aires governor Axel Kicillof is emerging as a potential challenger, although we 
note that the field within the Peronist opposition remains uncertain. See here for further 
context. While a more competitive election could periodically raise market concerns over 
policy continuity, there are reasons to expect greater institutional durability than in 
previous Argentine political cycles. La Libertad Avanza is likely to strengthen its position 
in Congress and the Senate, and commitment to fiscal discipline appears to be 
broadening beyond the government itself. Even in a more competitive political 
environment, this could reduce the risk of a wholesale reversal of the current economic 
framework.

ab 122
Brazil

Brazil's fiscal challenge remains unchanged: stabilizing debt-to-GDP will 
require sizeable and persistent primary surpluses.

Fiscal policy should remain the main driver of the macro outlook, with 
implications for the currency, inflation and the Selic rate.
Surprising election result, same fiscal challenges
President Lula staged the first runoff turnaround in Brazil's history, defying political 
experts, polls and our own aggregator, which had Senator Flávio Bolsanaro at 51% and 
Lula at 49%. After finishing second in the first round, with 45.2% of valid votes versus 
Bolsanaro's  47%, the incumbent added 10.4mn votes and won the runoff with 51.8% 
of valid votes. The Senator's vote total increased by  3.7mn, from 56.1mn to 59.8mn, 
and reached 48.2%. 
Lula therefore captured 74% of the 14.1mn additional votes available to either 
candidate in the runoff. These comprised 9.3mn votes cast for other candidates in the 
first round, 1.8mn from the decline in blank and null votes, and 3mn from higher 
turnout. The increase in turnout between rounds was particularly unusual. Overall, the 
results point to significant mobilization in favor of Lula between the first and second 
rounds. We see increased turnout and vote migration as the explanation for the result.
Despite the surprise at the polls, the main economic challenge has not changed. Fiscal 
policy remains at the center of Brazil's macro outlook. In 2023, the government 
introduced a new fiscal framework targeting a balanced primary result in 2024, 
followed by surpluses of 0.5% of GDP in 2025 and 1% in 2026. At the time, consensus 
expected deficits of 0.8%, 0.5% and 0.3%, respectively. Actual deficits eventually came 
in at 0.4%, 0.4% and 0.5%, not far from those initial market expectations.
Figure 293: Turnout change from the first round to the 
runoff (% of eligible voters)
Turnout change from the first round to the runoff (% of eligible voters)
Source: TSE, UBS
Figure 294: Primary balance targets, expectations and final 
results (% of GDP) *
Primary balance targets, expectations and final results (% of GDP) *
Source: BCB, Planning Ministry, UBS (*) 2026 Actual is UBSe
We draw two conclusions from this experience. First, the underlying primary result 
matters more for debt dynamics than the formal target. In recent years, the Ministry of 
Finance excluded some expenditure items, including precatórios (court-ordered debt), 
from the fiscal target. This allowed the government to comply with the formal 
framework despite running deficits. For markets and debt dynamics, we think the 
unadjusted primary balance is  the more relevant measure. 
Second, market expectations matter more than the targets themselves. The Focus 
Survey already suggested in 2023 that investors did not fully expect the announced fiscal 
path to materialize. The subsequent undershoot was therefore largely anticipated, and is 
fair to say that the current government fiscal performance was very much in line with 
market expectations. 
President Lula captured 74% of the 
additional available votes in the 
runoff, resulting in the first 
turnaround in Brazil's history.

Global Economics & Markets Outlook 9 November 2026
ab 123
Fiscal performance also benefited from considerably stronger revenues than initially 
expected. Part of the increase reflected one-off revenues, which by definition should not 
recur, while part came from stronger economic activity. The latter was supported by 
credit and fiscal impulses that are no longer present. Given the high cyclicality of tax 
collection, the slowdown in GDP that we expect should also weigh on revenues, likely 
increasing next year's deficit.
Why is a fiscal adjustment paramount?
The core issue is debt-to-GDP. Brazil already has one of the highest public debt ratios 
among emerging markets, and the increase in recent years has also been considerable. 
Stabilizing the ratio requires persistent primary surpluses, particularly while the cost of 
government debt remains above nominal GDP growth. 
Figure 295: Gross debt (% of GDP)
Gross debt (% of GDP)
Source: BCB, UBS
Figure 296: Primary balance (% of GDP)
Primary balance (% of GDP)
Source: BCB, UBS
There are two ways to improve the primary balance: raise taxes or reduce expenditure. 
Brazil's tax burden is already around 35% of GDP, comparable with that of many 
European economies. We therefore do not think the required adjustment can come 
from revenues alone. Moreover, with the newly elected Congress leaning more toward 
the center-right, we think the Executive would face significant constraints in securing 
support for adjustments only on the revenue side. 
Figure 297: Net revenues breakdown (% of GDP)
Net revenues breakdown (% of GDP)

Spending breakdown (% of GDP) *

However, reducing selected tax exemptions or incentives would be less difficult than 
increasing taxes. We believe half the fiscal effort can be done by reducing those. A first 
investigation reveals that total exemptions represent around 4.5% of GDP. We think the 
pool that is possible to be cut sums up to 1.3% of GDP.  Thus, with today's starting point 
Why adjust? Stabilizing debt will 
require persistent primary surpluses 
and spending restraint.

Global Economics & Markets Outlook 9 November 2026
ab 124
(~0.6% deficit), a  0.7% surplus could be reached by cutting half of those exemptions 
(0.65%) and reducing an additional 0.65% of GDP in expenditures. We recall that the 
cuts would have to be negotiated in Congress.
On the expenditure side, one of the most consequential potential changes would be to 
the minimum wage rule. The minimum wage currently grows according to nominal GDP 
and indexes around 60% of federal expenditure, particularly pensions and social 
benefits. We estimate that limiting its adjustments to inflation, thereby eliminating real 
minimum wage growth, could generate savings of as much as 0.7ppt of GDP 
cumulatively over 2027-30. Changes to constitutional floors for health and education 
could provide up to another 1ppt of GDP during the presidential term, in the most 
optimistic scenario.
Even if the administration chooses lighter measures, the degree of budget rigidity means 
that a sizeable expenditure adjustment would likely require constitutional changes. With 
around 95% of the budget already mandatory, we think there is limited room for a 
material consolidation based only on discretionary spending such as congressional 
appropriations.
What could the fiscal adjustment look like?
We see four possible fiscal paths from 2028 onward. We expect limited room for change 
in 2027 because the government has already committed to new expenditure and the 
minimum wage adjustment is already set. 
The first scenario assumes persistent primary deficits of 0.75% of GDP, broadly 
consistent with recent prints and with no changes to the current fiscal framework. The 
second assumes a credible long-term balanced primary result. The third assumes primary 
surpluses of 0.75% of GDP, while the fourth assumes surpluses of 1.5% and 
privatizations. These scenarios reflect what we think markets could ultimately view as 
achievable outcomes, rather than the targets an incoming administration might initially 
announce.
1. Persistent 0.75% primary deficit: tail risk
We assign a very low probability to this scenario, which would imply a particularly 
concerning debt trajectory. If markets conclude that persistent deficits are the 
administration's intended path, we would expect a sharp currency reaction, eventually 
forcing a change in policy. We do not think any government would deliberately choose a 
path toward fiscal dominance. The resulting macro environment could begin to 
resemble 2015-16, with tighter financial conditions amplifying the economic slowdown 
and increasing recession risks.
The longer-term equilibrium under this scenario would imply potential GDP growth of 
around 1%, a 7% real equilibrium rate and inflation of around 6%, while USD-BRL 
would weaken beyond 6.50 next year. We do not expect this scenario to fully 
materialize, as market pressure would likely force a fiscal adjustment. If markets 
perceived this as the chosen path, we would expect GDP to contract by around 1% next 
year.
2. Balanced primary result: central case scenario
This is our base case and, importantly, would still not stabilize debt-to-GDP. However, we 
think markets would remain willing to finance the country under this scenario. A 
favorable global backdrop, including a relatively contained dollar and supportive terms 
of trade, combined with Brazil's carry, could keep the BRL relatively strong versus peers. 
Paradoxically, benign financial conditions would reduce the immediate incentive for a 
more ambitious fiscal adjustment, even though further consolidation would ultimately 
be needed.
The resulting macro equilibrium would remain relatively weak: we estimate potential 
GDP growth at around 1.7%, slightly below our current 2% estimate, with long-term 
real rates around 6% and inflation at 4.5%. We would expect GDP growth to be around 
zero in the first year and USD-BRL around 5.50 in today's prices.
Even a meaningful fiscal adjustment 
would not stabilize debt-to-GDP.

Global Economics & Markets Outlook 9 November 2026
ab 125
3. Primary surpluses of 0.75%: alternative scenario
We assign a lower probability to a more meaningful adjustment because government 
statements have so far provided limited indication that an adjustment of this magnitude 
is viewed as necessary. Paradoxically, a weaker external environment, a stronger USD 
and sharper BRL depreciation could increase the probability of this scenario by forcing a 
stronger domestic policy response. The appointment of a finance minister with a 
stronger preference for fiscal consolidation could lead to a similar outcome.
This would represent a more favorable macro equilibrium, with potential GDP growth 
around 2%, long-term real rates at 5.5% and inflation at 4%. In today's environment, 
we estimate USD-BRL would strengthen toward 5.10 in real terms.
4. Primary surpluses of 1.5% and privatizations: tail risk
We see a negligible probability of an adjustment of this magnitude. Achieving it would 
likely require changes to the minimum wage rule and constitutional floors for health and 
education, measures the current government has previously rejected. We also assume 
government asset sales equivalent to 2% of GDP.
This would represent the most favorable macro equilibrium, with potential GDP growth 
around 2.5%, long-term real rates at 4.5% and inflation at 3.5%. In today's prices, we 
estimate USD-BRL could strengthen toward 4.50.
Figure 299: Debt/GDP alternative scenarios (% of GDP)
Debt/GDP alternative scenarios (% of GDP)
Source: BCB, UBS
Our base case is a balanced primary result, which would slow, but not reverse, the 
increase in debt-to-GDP. By the end of the next four years, we estimate debt would 
reach 102% of GDP under this scenario, compared with 97% under a 0.75% primary 
surplus. A 5ppt difference in debt-to-GDP is already significant and would widen further 
over time. Our base case assumes that a more ambitious fiscal adjustment, towards a 
primary surplus of 0.75% or more, would only take place after 2030.
The debt arithmetic illustrates the broader challenge. With a persistent 0.75% primary 
deficit, we estimate gross debt would reach 111% of GDP by 2030, a 27ppt increase 
from current levels, twice the increase seen in the past four years. Even with a 0.75% 
primary surplus, we think debt would still rise to 97% of GDP, around 13ppt above 
current levels.
A 1.5% primary surplus would produce a considerably better outcome, although debt 
would still end 2027 around 5ppt above current levels. Combined with government 
asset sales, however, this scenario could bring debt-to-GDP closer to today's level in 
2030. Even relatively ambitious primary surpluses would not immediately stabilize debt, 
but the differences across fiscal paths become increasingly significant over time.

The next 14 months through December 2027
We expect the BCB to keep the Selic unchanged at least in the December 2026 and 
January 2027 meetings. By then, we should have greater clarity on the next finance 
minister, the fiscal framework and the leadership of both houses of Congress, all of 
which will be important for the debt trajectory.
We expect economic activity to remain weak over the next few quarters, with GDP 
growth of -0.5% QoQ in 3Q26, 0.0% in 4Q26, followed by -0.2%, 0.1%, 0.4% and 
0.5% through 2027. Growth should then gradually converge toward our new potential 
growth estimate of 1.7%, slightly below our previous 2.0% estimate.
Conclusion
Brazil needs a credible fiscal adjustment capable of easing financial conditions and 
reversing part of the recent deterioration in confidence. The fiscal path should, in turn, 
shape the outlook for the currency, inflation and monetary policy. 
The economy is already going through a mid-cycle slowdown. A deterioration in fiscal 
credibility could amplify that slowdown by raising risk premia and tightening financial 
conditions further.
The currency does not fully reflect these fiscal risks at present, partly due to high carry 
and supportive terms of trade, in our view. A deterioration in the external environment, 
for example through additional Fed tightening, could weaken these buffers and lead 
USD-BRL to depreciate relatively quickly.
Inflation and monetary policy should remain closely linked to FX and its effect on 
inflation expectations. We forecast IPCA inflation at 5% in 2027, with USD-BRL at 5.50 
at year-end. For now, Copom is reassessing the outlook, and upcoming decisions should 
depend importantly on the evolution of inflation expectations in the Focus Survey.
The next domestic signposts are the appointment of the finance minister, potentially in 
early December, and whether the incoming administration presents a credible fiscal plan 
for its term.
Fiscal policy remains the key macro variable. A credible adjustment could ease financial 
conditions across the economy; without one, higher debt and risk premia would 
constrain the outlook for BRL, inflation, rates and growth.
Fiscal policy will remain the key 
driver of BRL, inflation and rates.

Global Economics & Markets Outlook 9 November 2026
ab 128
Chile

After a mild recession this year, we forecast a rebound in 2027 but mostly on 
base effects.

On reforms and investment reactivation, signs are encouraging but growth 
payoff remains to be seen.
Growth to rebound in 2027  mostly on base effects
This year, the Chilean economy will likely post a 0.2% contraction, largely below 
consensus and our own expectation at the start of the year (~2.5%). Specific factors had 
a dent on growth such as a high base of comparison for agricultural output, 
compounded by lower output in key crops earlier this year, and record rainfall disrupting 
productive activities in July/October. However, and beyond those, what stands out is the 
underlying weakness of the domestic economy, with a consumer hit by the surge in fuel 
prices in Q1 and a weak labour market, and modest investment growth that fails to turn 
into a broad-based recovery even with high copper prices. With many of these 
headwinds dissipating, we expect GDP growth to rebound to 3.0% in 2027. Yet roughly 
two-thirds of that growth reflects favourable base effects, leaving only a relatively 
modest recovery in domestic demand. Lower oil prices should provide some relief to 
households, while the gradual execution of a pipeline of mining and other investment 
projects should support activity, particularly in the second half of the year.
Reform agenda is encouraging, but the growth payoff remains to be seen
The government recently secured approval of the Reconstruction Bill, aimed at boosting 
investment through lower corporate taxes, tax invariability for qualifying investments, 
streamlined environmental and regulatory approvals, and targeted housing and 
employment measures. Reducing permitting bottlenecks arguably offers the clearest 
near-term growth upside. Indeed, even before the Bill’s approval, efforts to accelerate 
environmental permitting coincided with a sharp rise in project approvals, with the SEIA 
clearing USD 40bn in investment projects in January-August, more than in the same 
period of any year over the past two decades. While the ultimate growth payoff will 
depend on implementation and on how quickly approved projects move into 
construction, these early results provide some reason for cautious optimism that the 
reform agenda could help unlock investment and strengthen growth over time. This 
could prove particularly relevant for mining, where declining ore grades are reducing the 
growth dividend from high copper prices, and for construction, where regulatory 
bottlenecks have weighed on activity.
CB: acting amid rising inflation risks, but not the start of a full-blown hike cycle 
The CB faces an increasingly uncomfortable trade-off between weak activity and rising 
inflation risks. Recent growth weakness is partly driven by temporary supply factors, 
while high copper prices and a growing investment pipeline suggest a better demand 
outlook ahead. Meanwhile, higher oil and food prices should eventually prove 
temporary, but their persistence, alongside renewed peso weakness and a more 
challenging global monetary backdrop, raises the risk of broader price pressures and a 
deterioration in inflation expectations. Our forecast incorporates 12m inflation ending 
the year slightly below 5%, and staying around those levels in Q1'27, with upside risks 
should oil prices continue rising or El Niño intensifies. We believe these concerns will 
ultimately outweigh the weakness in near-term activity, prompting the CB to tighten 
policy. However, we expect a limited and preventive adjustment rather than the start of a 
full-blown hiking cycle, more akin to the modest tightening of late 2015. Our baseline is 
for 50bp of hikes in Q1, with the risk of an earlier start if inflation surprises materially to 
the upside before year-end.
Fiscal and external balances stable and supported by copper
High copper prices should continue to provide an important buffer for Chile’s external 
and fiscal accounts in 2027. Our house copper price view is supportive of a marginal 
widening of the current account deficit as domestic demand recovers. On the fiscal side, 
stronger mining revenues, complemented by the cyclical recovery in tax receipts, should 
more than compensate for the revenue impact of scheduled corporate tax cuts for next 
year. Overall, healthy copper revenues should keep fiscal and external imbalances 
contained as growth recovers.

year but mostly on account of base 
effects. Agenda for reducing red tape 
represents the clearest near-term 
growth upside.

Colombia

Fiscal adjustment, macro rebalancing and El Niño to shape  outlook.

Banrep to keep restrictive stance through 1H'27 once Niño, fiscal risks recede.
Gradual fiscal adjustment to start in 2027, extending beyond spending cuts
With elections behind us, prospects for a policy shift to address fiscal imbalances have 
become more tangible. The latest fiscal update revealed a worse-than-anticipated deficit 
trajectory, but also provided a more realistic picture of the cost of inaction. Against this 
backdrop, the government’s Rescue Bill targets a 2.4% of GDP adjustment in 2027, 
rising to around 4% over the medium term. Note only ~0.6% of GDP in the adjustment 
target for 2027 reflects expenditure cuts, with the remainder coming from shifting 
social-security financing toward employers and contributors, and higher taxes (VAT and 
PIT). Congressional negotiations are likely to dilute the package, given limited political 
incentives to cut spending, raise taxes or increase social security costs to employers in a 
regional election year. Delayed approval would also reduce the fiscal impact in 2027, 
although the government retains room to contain budget execution by freezing or 
postponing expenditures. All told, we pencil in a 1% of GDP adjustment for next year, 
short of the government’s target but sufficient to mark the start of a gradual fiscal 
correction. Even partial approval of the Rescue Bill, alongside proactive debt 
management, could help reduce Colombia’s sizeable financing needs in 2027. 
Successful negotiations with the IMF over potential financial assistance could further 
strengthen market confidence in the durability of the adjustment and the government’s 
commitment to fiscal sustainability.
Growth to moderate in 2027 as rebalancing starts
Growth remained resilient through much of 2026, but momentum has recently started 
to moderate. Beyond the disruptions to productive activity from the August 10 
earthquake, signs of cooling across tertiary sectors and the labour market are becoming 
more visible. We expect this trend to continue, with growth slowing from 2.3% in 2026 
to 1.8% in 2027. The external backdrop should remain broadly supportive, even as oil 
prices normalize later in the year (recall that workers' remittances are now comparable in 
size to oil exports). Domestic factors point in the opposite direction: the cumulative 
impact of monetary tightening (+300bp since Dec-25) and the expected slowdown in 
public spending amid fiscal tightening should weigh on domestic demand, driving a 
gradual rebalancing of growth. As for the investment outlook, progress on the 
government's reform agenda, including fiscal consolidation, promotion of the oil sector 
and reforms to reduce red tape, offers scope for a recovery, although greater clarity is 
likely only by 2028, when we expect this demand component to lead the broader GDP 
recovery (2.6%). In that regard, successful implementation of government measures to 
boost investment in the hydrocarbon sector, critical to reversing the downward trend in 
sector output, provides upside potential to our forecast.
El Niño, oil and fiscal milestones to keep BanRep vigilant through 1H27
On the price front, the composition of inflationary pressures is likely to change over the 
coming months rather than fully dissipate. While inflation dynamics in 2026 were largely 
driven by the record 23% minimum wage hike and its impact on core inflation, the 
arrival of El Niño conditions is likely to bring renewed pressure through more volatile 
components, particularly fresh food and energy, from late 2026 and through much of 
1H27. We expect inflation to end 2026 at 6.9%, with upside risks of up to 7.5% should 
El Niño prove more intense. For 2027, assuming a 7-9% minimum wage hike and El 
Niño conditions persisting through 1H27, we see room for a resumption of disinflation 
in the second half of the year, with inflation declining to 5.0% by year-end, still well 
above Banrep’s 3% ±1pp target range. As such, we expect Banrep to maintain a 
cautious stance amid inflation risks, with an additional and final 25bp rate hike in Q4 
that would leave the policy rate at 12.50%. In 2027, inflation risks and the unfolding of 
fiscal milestones would keep the monetary authority on the sidelines until late 2Q, when 
we expect a shift in the policy stance towards rate cuts (UBSe: 11.50% YE27). 
Finally, a word on COP. After a rally against the dollar in 2026 (up 15% YTD), our 
baseline incorporates relative peso resilience at least through the first part of the 2027, 
as Banrep’s cautious stance continues and the fiscal adjustment plan takes shape. In the 
second part of the year, the start of gradual easing and a relatively less supportive 
external backdrop, including lower oil prices, should drive the peso to 3250/USD by YE.

Global Economics & Markets Outlook 9 November 2026
ab 132
Mexico

The economy is starting to post a broad-based recovery and should average 
growth of roughly 2% in our forecast period.

Banxico’s ability to divorce itself from the Fed and potentially deliver further 
easing depends largely on MXN.
Mexico is consolidating an increasingly broad-based economic recovery
Mexico’s economy looks poised to end 2026 with growth closer to 2% than to the 1.3% 
we had originally estimated. For 2027, we now see growth at 2.2%, but risks could be 
to the upside. Growth is consolidating, recession risks have receded, and recent 
evidence suggests that the recovery is becoming somewhat broader.
Tech export boom  has legs. Tech exports are rising by more than 150% y-o-y and now 
exceed auto exports in nominal terms. Admittedly, the sector imports close to 95% of its 
inputs and operates largely as a re-assembler, adding relatively little value per unit 
exported. But with 12-month accumulated tech exports now exceeding USD160bn, 
even a small amount of domestic value added adds up quickly. By our estimates, tech 
contributed 0.6 pps to growth in the last year, explaining roughly half of the expansion 
during that period, and this contribution could be accelerating. Demand for Mexican-
manufactured servers, computer boards and chips should remain strong through our 
forecast period on the back of large and ongoing AI-related investment by US 
hyperscalers as they roll out their US datacenter expansion. 
The consumer should remain resilient. Private consumption growth, while 
admittedly not strong, has averaged 2.4% over the past year and remains supportive of 
economic activity. Real wages continue to rise, partly supported by minimum-wage 
increases, while employment growth has gained momentum relative to last year. 
Government social programs and pension outlays also continue to expand. Remittances 
are still making a negative contribution to household income growth, but this could 
change in light of recent peso weakness.
 More importantly, investment is starting to recover. The public sector is leading 
through a rebound in infrastructure spending. Private investment is also reviving in 
residential and non-residential construction and, more recently, in machinery and 
equipment, with capital imports accelerating. In the absence of greater clarity on 
USMCA, sustaining this rebound may hinge on the success of Plan Mexico, which calls 
for closer cooperation between the state and the private sector through mixed 
contracts. Progress has been slow, but interest in joint public-private projects in 
electricity and roads is increasing, even as interest in oil and gas remains subdued. 
Inflation and monetary policy outlook
Headline inflation is rising, reflecting a rebound in fresh agricultural prices that is likely to 
intensify as the effects of El Niño become more visible. However, fresh agricultural 
inflation tends to mean-revert, and we expect Banxico to look through this as a 
transitory shock. The authorities are more likely to focus on core inflation, where there is 
greater room to be constructive. Our year-end core inflation forecast stands at 3.7%, 
below consensus and closer to Banxico’s own forecast. Moreover, alternative gauges 
mostly point to more subdued inflation ahead. Diffusion indices are improving; 
trimmed-mean core inflation is running closer to 2% on a three-month seasonally 
adjusted annualised basis; and Banxico’s supercore and fundamental inflation measures 
remain contained within their 2%-4% confidence band.
Processed-food inflation should ease  in coming months, judging by international FAO 
food prices expressed in pesos, which domestic prices tend to follow with a 9-12m lag. 
Inflation in other merchandise goods remains low, supported by the widening presence 
of hard discounters and the liquidation of inventories accumulated ahead of tariffs 
introduced earlier this year. Monthly services inflation is also becoming better behaved 
and more consistent with its historical average. Some categories that had previously 
exerted pressure, including restaurants, are now registering smaller price increases.
Risks nevertheless remain. MXN has weakened with the rise in US interest rates, and 
further depreciation could  put upward pressure on merchandise prices. Continued 
minimum-wage increases should pressure  labour-intensive sectors, while the gradual

ab 133
shortening of the working week between 2027 and 2030 will raise labour costs. High 
international oil prices will also weigh on imported prices and items such as air fares, 
although the authorities retain the fiscal capacity to keep domestic pump prices stable.
Banxico left its benchmark rate unchanged at 6.50% at its September meeting. We 
think the majority of the five-member board retains a dovish disposition. Core inflation is 
increasingly well behaved; some services pressures may reflect supply constraints rather 
than excess demand; previous monetary restrictiveness may still be operating through 
the economy; higher domestic savings may have lowered the neutral rate; and the 
output gap remains large. Whether Banxico can divorce itself from the Fed will 
ultimately depend on the peso and the inflationary pass-through generated by any 
depreciation. The authorities may care less about the level of the currency than about 
whether trading remains orderly. Disorderly, one-sided trading and gapping prices could 
lead Banxico to consider intervention through volatility auctions before raising rates. The 
threshold for monetary tightening therefore remains high. In fact, and in line with the 
dovish tone of the majority in the September minutes, we see scope for some limited 
fine-tuning in the benchmark rate to the downside in our forecast period.
Fiscal outlook
The proposed 2027 budget keeps  the deficit on a modest  downward trajectory. The 
public-sector borrowing requirement is budgeted at 3.9% of GDP, against an estimated 
4.1% in 2026, while the primary surplus rises to 0.5% of GDP from 0.1%. Public debt is 
nevertheless forecast to continue climbing before stabilising at 56.5% of GDP by 2031. 
The budget’s assumptions are more conservative than in recent years and closer to 
market consensus. Revenue is expected to rise from 23.0% of GDP in 2026 to 23.2% in 
2027. Tax revenue increases by 0.5% of GDP, largely because of a proposed corporate 
tax reform that caps deductions, restricts the use of previous losses and reduces the net-
interest deduction against EBITDA from 30% to 20%. The government estimates that 
the reform could generate MXN140bn annually by, in effect, introducing a minimum 
corporate tax of 1% of revenue, which could be challenging for low-margin sectors such 
as retail.  Expenditure, meanwhile,  remains at 26.7% of GDP. Lower amortisation needs 
reduce assistance to Pemex, creating room for higher welfare, education and healthcare 
disbursements. Infrastructure investment is also scheduled to rise by 3.1% in real terms. 
Even if the 2027 targets are met, the planned consolidation appears modest for a 
sovereign whose credit ratings sit close to the lower boundary of investment grade  and 
longer-term official forecasts do not yet contain a clearly articulated adjustment. Debt 
stabilisation relies heavily on physical investment falling to 1.8% of GDP from 2028, 
compared with 2.6% budgeted for 2027. This would conflict with Plan Mexico’s 
objective of raising investment and looks more like a residual needed to stabilise debt 
than a developed fiscal strategy. Mexico probably requires a fiscal effort of around 
1.0%-1.5% of GDP to stabilise its debt and to prevent a loss of its investment grade 
rating. The options are limited: expenditure cuts, making Pemex operationally profitable, 
or higher taxation. With non-oil tax revenue at only 15.4% of GDP, higher taxes could  be 
the path of least resistance. We think the Sheinbaum administration will have to turn its 
attention to fiscal more readily after the mid-term elections in June 2027. 
USMCA outlook
A bilateral tariff agreement with the US could be achievable in the short term. Such an 
agreement could lower the Section 232 tariff on finished autos from 25%, after 
deducting US content, towards 15%; clarify the treatment of auto parts; consolidate 
recent arrangements on steel and aluminium; and confirm that USMCA-compliant 
goods remain exempt from Section 301 tariffs. In exchange, Mexico would probably 
need to provide stronger guarantees against transhipments from third countries, 
particularly China. Higher regional-content requirements, closer alignment with US 
controls on sensitive goods and tighter screening of inward investment are likely to form 
part of the negotiations. It remains unclear whether Mexico would accept alignment on 
external tariffs that could place its free-trade agreements with third countries at risk.
An agreement along these lines would reassure investors, but it should not be confused 
with a comprehensive USMCA settlement. The wider framework is likely to remain 
subject to annual reviews, with intellectual-property rights, labour rules, agriculture, 
energy and rare-earth investment, and environmental issues still to be resolved. We 
nevertheless remain constructive on the outlook for the  bilateral trade relationship: the 
US remains at the negotiating table; Mexico’s conciliatory approach has produced less
